# Round 2 ideas: "same capability, much cheaper"

Date: 2026-10-04. 3 generators × 5 ideas = 15, one per strategy: R = cheaper copy of a published result, S = software replaces hardware, P = cheap open version of a commercial feature. Each idea had to name the expensive reference, one non-obvious insight, and a powered experiment.
Review: 3 adversarial reviewers per idea, same rubric as `rounds.md` (8.5 = survive, 7.5 = near-miss). Departure from the original rule (stated up front): the **top 5 after round 1 were revised even though they were below 7.5**, then re-scored from scratch by 3 fresh reviewers.
Outcome: completed 2 rounds. **No idea reached 7.5.** Best median overall: 5.67.

## Ideas, reviews and revisions

### P2.v1 (round 1, median 5.67): Four posts and a $96 lidar: retroreflector positioning vs. RTK for wire-free yard and driveway robots

- **Question:** Can a ~$96 LD19 dToF lidar plus 3-4 retroreflective posts reach the ≤3 cm horizontal accuracy that RTK mower systems advertise (Husqvarna EPOS: 2-3 cm), across a 15 x 15 m yard and driveway in full sun? How does its error compare with a ZED-F9P RTK receiver on the same surveyed points?
- **Hypothesis:** With intensity gating and known-radius cylinder fitting, the reflector system's median horizontal error will be ≤3 cm out to 8 m with ≥3 posts visible in full sun (≤2 cm at night). That is within 1.5x of RTK-fixed error in open sky and better than RTK at the garage mouth and under eaves. The hypothesis is falsified if sun-induced false returns, intensity clipping or edge mixed-pixel bias push the 8 m error above 5 cm.
- **Measurements:** 40 ground-truth points surveyed by steel-tape trilateration from two baseline nails (about +/-5-10 mm), covering open driveway, eaves, garage mouth and lawn. At each point, 60 s of static logging from both systems in 3 sessions (noon sun with posts back-lit or front-lit, overcast, night), giving 120 paired point-sessions per system. Metrics: horizontal error (cm) CDF and 95th percentile; error vs. range and post geometry (GDOP); RTK fix/float fraction and false-fix rate (fix reported but >10 cm off). Dynamic: 20 closed-loop runs of a 20 m route per system, scoring endpoint error against a chalk mark. Analysis: paired differences at identical points (Wilcoxon signed-rank, bootstrap CI on the median difference) and a TOST on continuous paired error with a pre-registered ±1 cm margin. With 120 pairs and SD(diff) of about 2 cm, the 90% CI half-width is about 0.3 cm, so equivalence is decidable, unlike the earlier n=25 success-rate designs.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Expensive reference: RTK positioning as sold in wire-free mowers. Husqvarna EPOS claims 2-3 cm (press release in prior_art.md); the Navimow i105N is $799-1,000. The team measures a SparkFun ZED-F9P RTK2 rover ($259.95, verified) with free public NTRIP corrections, or a second F9P as base (+$259.95), on the same points. Conceptual industrial reference: reflector-based laser navigation scanners for AGVs (for example the SICK NAV350 class; price and specs not verified this session).
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: a retroreflector returns far more light than any natural surface. A consumer dToF lidar rated only +/-45 mm per point can therefore (a) find posts by intensity alone in 30 klux sun, with trivial data association, and (b) recover each post center by fitting the 10-30 hits on a cylinder of known radius. Range noise then averages down by about √N and bearing is centroided below the beam spacing. Why it is not foregone: the LD19's intensity channel may clip near posts, edge hits produce mixed-pixel range bias, sun raises the noise floor, and beyond about 8 m only a few hits land per post, so RTK in open sky may clearly win. Reflector localization itself is old (AGV papers below use industrial scanners indoors). What is new is whether a $96 hobby lidar meets the 2-3 cm consumer-RTK spec outdoors, measured head-to-head on a continuous metric. Searches: Crossref found indoor AGV reflector work only; Google Scholar and Patents could not be searched this session, so the team must re-check for LD06/LD19 reflector-localization papers and GitHub projects.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Research on AGV Global Positioning Algorithm Based on Reflector and Lidar (IJCSIT, 2024)](https://doi.org/10.62051/ijcsit.v2n3.10); [AGV global localization using indistinguishable artificial landmarks (ICRA 2011)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2011.5979759); [Husqvarna EPOS virtual-boundary press release (2-3 cm claim)](https://www.husqvarnagroup.com/en/press/husqvarna-expands-innovation-leadership-virtual-boundary-technology-professional-robotic); [Waveshare LD19 dToF lidar (+/-45 mm, 30 klux rating)](https://www.waveshare.com/product/dtof-lidar-ld19.htm)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: 4 posts (about 1 m of 2-inch PVC with a band of high-grade retroreflective sheeting) on weighted quick-set bases; a lidar/antenna mast on the existing hoverboard rover so lidar and antenna share one vertical axis; a survey kit (nails, steel tapes) and a point-marking jig. Software student: LD19 driver (ROS 2 or Python); intensity gate, clustering and least-squares circle fit; trilateration plus EKF with wheel odometry; RTK logging with fix-state flags; closed-loop route follower usable with either source; paired-error analysis.
- **Cost:** $860: LD19 lidar $95.99-99 (verified; Waveshare lists it as discontinued, so buy an LD06/STL-19P-class equivalent early, price UNVERIFIED). Posts and reflective sheeting ~$40 (estimate). Rover base ~$260 (hoverboard $139 verified + ESP32 $19.95 verified + frame ~$100 estimate). Raspberry Pi 5 4GB ~$110 (verified in A2). RTK baseline: SparkFun ZED-F9P $259.95 (verified) + multiband antenna ~$60 (estimate) using free NTRIP; add +$320 for an own base if no CORS station is near. Steel tapes and marking ~$35 (estimate). Total about $860, or about $1,180 with an own RTK base (FLAG). Cost ratio for the positioning capability: about $140 (lidar + posts) vs. about $580-$640 for a hobby RTK pair (4-5x) and vs. a $799-1,000 RTK mower (6-7x). Industrial reflector scanners would be far higher (UNVERIFIED).
- **Venue:** IEEE CASE 2027 (Mar 1). A cost-effectiveness study of automation infrastructure fits CASE better than a new-method IROS paper. Backup: IROS 2027.
- **Main risk:** The LD19's intensity channel may clip or be too coarse to separate posts from sunlit glass or chrome (cars, windows), creating false posts. Fallback: use two-band posts (retro band plus black band) for a geometric signature and report the false-detection rate as a finding. If accuracy misses 3 cm, the paper reports the measured error-vs-range curve, which still answers the cost question.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 6 / I 5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* Prior work already covers retroreflective cylinders plus lidar evaluated against DGPS on a bounded outdoor area (Lutwitzi et al., IV 2022, doi:10.1109/iv51971.2022.9827085). Reflector GDOP analysis exists (IEEE Sensors J 2024), and open-source reflector localization packages exist (RLML). What remains is mainly a cheaper sensor and a sunlight characterization. The 'RTK degrades under eaves and at the garage mouth' arm is the same kind of result the earlier 25-reviewer round already called foreseeable. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and the software exists. However, the core '10-30 hits per post' premise is wrong for the LD19 as specified (verified on the Waveshare page: 4500 Hz sampling at a typical 10 Hz scan gives about 450 points per revolution, about 0.8 deg). A 6 cm post subtends about 0.43 deg at 8 m and about 1.7 deg at 2 m, so it gets roughly 0-2 hits per scan. Static 60 s averaging can rescue the static test, but dynamic accuracy cannot be rescued this way without much wider posts. Two further problems: full sun (roughly 100 klux, general knowledge, not verified this session) is about 3x the 30 klux rating, and the RTK frame and steel-tape frame have to be aligned. *Impact:* This is a single-site sensor characterization. A CASE/IROS reviewer would expect static RTK to win in open sky, as CACS 2023 already found, and would expect reflectors to win where GNSS is shaded. The likely outcome is a SoutheastCon, ARSO or workshop paper rather than CASE main track. As a college-application story it is respectable but not distinctive. *Main criticism:* Reflector-plus-lidar localization with retroreflective cylinders, checked against GNSS outdoors, already exists (IV 2022). The research question therefore reduces to whether one particular $96 sensor is good enough, and its answer is largely predictable: RTK wins in open sky, reflectors win under eaves. The cost advantage is also overstated (about 2.3x vs. a single NTRIP rover, not 4-7x). And the key enabling assumption (many hits per post) is false at the LD19's angular resolution for 6 cm posts, so the dynamic results will likely be weak. *Closest prior work:* [Lidar and Landmark based Localization System for a Wheeled Mobile Driving Simulator (Lutwitzi, Betschinske, Albrecht, Winner; IEEE IV 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iv51971.2022.9827085); [Reflective Markers Assisted Indoor LiDAR Self-Positioning Algorithm Based on Joint Optimization With FIM and GDOP (IEEE Sensors Journal, 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/JSEN.2023.3294934); [AGV global localization using indistinguishable artificial landmarks (ICRA 2011)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2011.5979759); [Research on AGV Global Positioning Algorithm Based on Reflector and Lidar (IJCSIT 2024)](https://doi.org/10.62051/ijcsit.v2n3.10); [RLML: Reflector-based LiDAR Mapping and Localization (open-source, GitHub)](https://github.com/monkeydchopper/RLML); [Outdoor Positioning Based on ROS LiDAR Navigation Compared with RTK GPS Accuracy (CACS 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/cacs60074.2023.10325865); [Reflective Fiducials for Localization With 3D LiDAR Scanners (IEEE Access 2019)](https://doi.org/10.1109/ACCESS.2019.2909467); [A vehicle self-localization method utilizing reflector code recognition using LiDAR on vehicle (ITNAC 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/itnac66378.2025.11302743); [Sudriabotik Eurobot code (Raspberry Pi + LD06 lidar)](https://github.com/jameseloire/Sudriabotik)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 7.5 / I 5.5 = 6.00. *Novelty:* Reflector triangulation with lidar is a mature AGV technique: the IJCSIT 2024 paper and ICRA 2011 do exactly this, and arXiv 2107.10597 already benchmarks a reflector-LiDAR system against UWB. Lidar-only, RTK-free wire-free mowers also already ship (AM Robots Storm, 2024). What remains new is a characterization of one $96 dToF sensor in sun, compared with RTK. That is a known technique in a new setting with modest new measurements, and the answer (cm-level within ~5-8 m) is fairly predictable. I could not search patents or Eurobot/hobbyist practice (search budget exhausted). Cheap lidar plus retroreflective beacon posts is, unverified, widespread in Eurobot, and old beacon-mower patents likely exist, so the true score may be lower. *Feasibility:* The parts are off the shelf, the software (intensity gate, circle fit, trilateration) is simple, and most data is static logging that can be done over a few weekends at home. The main risks: the LD19 is discontinued on Waveshare (verified in prior_art.md), so a clone's intensity channel must be bench-tested early. Retroreflector saturation and range-walk bias may need calibration. Aligning the tape-survey frame with the RTK ENU frame is a subtle step that can bias the comparison. The 20 closed-loop runs per system add rover-integration risk late in the timeline. *Impact:* A clean paired-error dataset of a hobby lidar against a ZED-F9P is a respectable SoutheastCon, ARSO or ICRA-workshop paper. A CASE or IROS main track is unlikely: the comparison is not like-for-like (local surveyed infrastructure with roughly 8 m coverage vs. global, infrastructure-free positioning), and the method contribution is thin. The college-application story ('$96 lidar matches RTK') is decent. *Main criticism:* This is a sensor characterization of a decades-old technique (lidar plus retroreflector trilateration, standard in AGVs and plausibly in student competitions), presented as 'same capability, cheaper than RTK'. The two systems do not deliver the same capability. Reflector posts need surveyed infrastructure and cover only a few meters to about 8 m per post set. RTK is global and needs no setup. Matching 2-3 cm within 8 m of surveyed posts is a predictable result that does not support the cheaper-equivalent claim, and the cost ratio is overstated by counting an RTK pair when free NTRIP needs only one receiver. *Closest prior work:* [Research on AGV Global Positioning Algorithm Based on Reflector and Lidar (Zhao, Jin, Huang, IJCSIT 2024)](https://ijocsit.org/articles/2-3-research-on-agv-global-positioning-algorithm-based-on-reflec/); [Application-driven Test and Evaluation Framework for Indoor Localization Systems in Warehouses (arXiv 2107.10597)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2107.10597); [AGV global localization using indistinguishable artificial landmarks (ICRA 2011)](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/5979759/); [Robotic lawn mower (Wikipedia): AM Robots Storm, a lidar mower with no external positioning (2024)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robotic_lawn_mower); [Husqvarna EPOS press release (2-3 cm RTK claim)](https://www.husqvarnagroup.com/en/press/husqvarna-expands-innovation-leadership-virtual-boundary-technology-professional-robotic); [Sun et al., trolley-collection localization: RFID vs keypoints vs UWB vs reflectors (Robotica 2025, arXiv 2303.06551)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5 / F 6.5 / I 5.5 = 5.67. *Novelty:* Reflector trilateration with a 2D lidar is decades old. It has indoor papers (ICRA 2011, IJCSIT 2024, both DOIs verified) and open-source hobby stacks (RLML, 2D_reflector_localization_Algorithm on GitHub), and a lidar mower without RTK has been sold since 2024 (AM Robots Storm, per Wikipedia). What is left is a cheaper re-implementation: the first published outdoor, in-sun characterization of an LD19-class lidar on retroreflectors, paired against a ZED-F9P. That gap is real but thin, and the RTK-under-eaves result is the same kind of foreseeable finding the earlier review rejected. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and off the shelf, and the static matrix (40 points x 3 sessions x 60 s) is doable on a few weekends. But the central sensing assumption fails on the spec sheet. Waveshare lists 4500 Hz at about 10 Hz, which is 0.8 degrees per point. A 2-inch (60 mm) post then gets about 1.1 hits per scan at 4 m and about 0.5 at 8 m (my arithmetic), not the 10-30 hits per post the proposal claims. Cylinder fitting is therefore impossible at the ranges that matter. In addition, the LD19 is DISCONTINUED (verified; Waveshare points to the D500 kit), its range is 12 m against a 15 x 15 m area, and the outdoor sessions fall in Dec-Feb. *Impact:* A careful paired TOST between a $96 lidar and RTK, with error-vs-range and sun/night curves, is a clean quantitative sensor-characterization paper. It fits ARSO, SoutheastCon or a CASE workshop or WIP track (CASE WIP closes Apr 1, 2027 per venues.md). A CASE or IROS main-track reviewer would likely see it as a known technique benchmarked on a hobby sensor, with no new algorithm and no task-level result. It is a respectable story for college applications, but not a standout. *Main criticism:* The idea's own physics undercuts its accuracy claim. At the LD19's 0.8-degree point spacing, a 60 mm post beyond about 3 m is hit by about one beam per scan, so there is no cylinder to fit. The post center then comes from a single, often edge-clipped, mixed-pixel return with +/-45 mm range noise. Saturation and range-walk bias on retroreflectors adds to this (a known ToF effect; not verified for the LD19). What survives is a re-implementation of a decades-old, open-sourced technique on a cheaper sensor, compared against an RTK receiver whose garage and eaves failures the earlier review already called foreseeable. *Closest prior work:* [Research on AGV Global Positioning Algorithm Based on Reflector and Lidar (IJCSIT 2024)](https://doi.org/10.62051/ijcsit.v2n3.10); [AGV global localization using indistinguishable artificial landmarks (ICRA 2011)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2011.5979759); [RLML: Reflector-based LiDAR Mapping and Localization (open-source, GitHub)](https://github.com/monkeydchopper/RLML); [2D_reflector_localization_Algorithm (GitHub)](https://github.com/unlogical0327/2D_reflector_localization_Algorithm); [Sun et al., indoor localization methods for trolley collection (Robotica 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551); [Wikipedia: Robotic lawn mower (AM Robots Storm, 2024, first lidar mower; Husqvarna EPOS 2-3 cm RTK)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robotic_lawn_mower); [Integration of Laser Scanner and Odometry for Autonomous Robotics Lawn-mower (ASABE 2015)](https://doi.org/10.13031/aim.20152190609)

### R2.v1 (round 1, median 5.50): NoMaD navigation policy on a $350 Raspberry Pi robot: distance-indexed context to survive low frame rates

- **Question:** When the NoMaD visual navigation diffusion policy (published on a LoCoBot with Jetson-class compute) runs on a Raspberry Pi 5 at a fraction of its intended inference rate, does indexing the context frames by distance traveled (from wheel odometry) instead of by time recover the paper's exploration success and collision numbers?
- **Hypothesis:** Running NoMaD naively on a Pi 5 CPU (estimated well under 2 Hz) gives a past-frame context spacing that no longer matches the training data. Collisions will rise to at least 1.0 per run (paper: 0.2) and exploration success will fall below 80% (paper: 98%). Picking context frames at the distance spacing the training data implies, and executing the predicted waypoint chunk with odometry while the next inference runs, cuts collisions per 10 m by at least 50% versus naive Pi and gets within 25% of the same model run on an off-board laptop GPU. Falsified if distance indexing gives no significant improvement, which would mean the policy is rate-robust and compute is not the bottleneck.
- **Measurements:** Three conditions on the same robot: (A) Pi 5 naive time-based context, (B) Pi 5 distance-indexed context with chunk execution, (C) the same checkpoint on a team laptop GPU streamed over Wi-Fi (the 'expensive compute' reference). 3 fixed courses (indoor home, garage, patio/driveway), 10 runs per course per condition = 90 runs. Conditions are interleaved and randomized within each session, with start poses marked on the floor. Metrics: collisions per 10 m (continuous, primary), exploration success (reach the course end within 3 min), goal-conditioned success, and progress fraction. Analysis: Poisson/negative-binomial regression of collisions with course as a fixed effect, and Wilson CIs on success compared with the paper's 98% / 0.2 collisions / 90% goal-conditioned. 30 runs per condition makes rate ratios of about 2x detectable (estimate). Binary success is reported but not used for the headline.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Sridhar, Shah, Glossop, Levine, 'NoMaD: Goal Masked Diffusion Policies for Navigation and Exploration' (arXiv 2310.07896, https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07896, code at general-navigation-models.github.io). Real-world tests on a LoCoBot in 6 environments: exploration 98% success with 0.2 collisions (vs 77%/1.7 for subgoal diffusion), and 90% goal-conditioned success. The paper notes it runs on 'less powerful onboard computers (e.g., NVIDIA Jetson Orin)'. Reference cost: LoCoBot plus Jetson Orin, prices UNVERIFIED (team's estimate is several thousand USD). Within the study, condition C (laptop GPU) is the expensive-compute baseline on identical hardware.
- **Novelty claim:** The one insight: the low-cost failure is a train/test mismatch in context spacing caused by low compute rate, not model capacity. Re-indexing context by odometry distance makes the policy rate-invariant at no hardware cost. The outcome is open: the transformer may be robust to spacing, in which case B equals A, or the Pi may simply be too slow to react, in which case B and A both fail far below C. Either way a reviewer learns where the compute floor of navigation foundation models lies, which deployment papers state as a claim but do not measure. arXiv abstract searches ('NoMaD navigation low-cost', 'navigation foundation model Raspberry Pi') returned nothing. Scholar/GitHub were NOT searched (budget exhausted). Low-cost rovers that run such models via remote compute (e.g., FrodoBots) should be checked and cited by the team.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [NoMaD: Goal Masked Diffusion Policies for Navigation and Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07896); [LeKiwi low-cost mobile base (SIGRobotics UIUC), Raspberry Pi 5, BOM $184-$499](https://github.com/SIGRobotics-UIUC/LeKiwi); [OpenBot: Turning Smartphones into Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2008.10631); [FrodoBots EarthRover low-cost teleoperated rovers ($149-$399)](https://newatlas.com/robotics/earthrover-mini-4g-rover/)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: assemble the LeKiwi base (3D-printed, omni wheels, servos) with a wide-angle camera mount and bumper switches to count collisions objectively, and set up the three marked courses. Software student: port the released NoMaD checkpoint to ONNX on the Pi 5, add the odometry-based keyframe buffer and chunked waypoint executor, set up the laptop-GPU streaming condition, and write a logging/analysis pipeline.
- **Cost:** $360: LeKiwi base-only 12V BOM $251.50 (verified on its BOM page; that page also lists Pi 5 4GB at $60 and 2 USB cameras at $25.96, but whether the base-only total includes the Pi is not confirmed, so add $60 to be safe). Wide-angle USB camera upgrade ~$30 (estimate). Bumper microswitches and wiring ~$15 (estimate). Filament ~$20 (estimate). Laptop with NVIDIA GPU owned ($0). If no GPU laptop is available, a cloud GPU for condition C costs ~$30 (estimate). Reference LoCoBot+Orin not bought. The cost ratio is UNVERIFIED but likely >10x.
- **Venue:** IEEE/RSJ IROS 2027: learning-based navigation and deployment is core IROS scope. A Mar 1 paper can also be reused for an ICRA 2027 workshop on foundation models or low-cost robotics.
- **Main risk:** Pi 5 CPU latency for the diffusion head may exceed about 1.5 s, making every Pi condition fail regardless of context handling. Fallback: cut denoising steps, use the ViNT head or add a Hailo NPU HAT (~$70, estimate). The rate gap then becomes the measured variable (a success-vs-inference-rate curve), which still answers the question.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 6 / I 5.5 = 5.50. *Novelty:* I found no paper that measures the compute or rate floor of NoMaD/ViNT on a Pi-class CPU. An arXiv abstract query for 'visual navigation' AND 'Raspberry Pi' returned 0 hits, and Guerrier et al. 2603.25937 used AGX Orin without studying rate. The two levers are not new, though. Async chunk execution is Real-Time Chunking (2506.07339). The official explore.py already decouples context capture from inference, so the 'distance-indexed context' fix addresses a mismatch the reference code largely does not have. *Feasibility:* The hardware is cheap and verified: the LeKiwi base-only 12V BOM is $251.50 and already includes the Pi 5 and two USB cameras. NoMaD is small (19M parameters, K=10 denoising steps), so an ONNX port on a Pi 5 is plausible, probably at several Hz (my estimate, unmeasured). The risks stack up. The Pi may be fast enough that A equals C (a null result). LeKiwi's narrow generic cameras and holonomic servo base are a distribution shift that may dominate everything. A home or garage gives few novel exploration environments. 90 interleaved runs plus a topological-graph goal mode is heavy for part-time weekends. *Impact:* 'Navigation foundation models on a $60 computer' is a timely, fundable story and a good fit for an ICRA 2027 workshop or ARSO, and it reads well for college applications. Main-track IROS is unlikely. With 3 home courses, one checkpoint, and an offset mechanism that a reviewer can show the reference code already handles, the work looks like a deployment report. Any Pi-vs-paper gap will be confounded by camera/FOV and embodiment shift, which Guerrier et al. already document. *Main criticism:* The central mechanism is likely a strawman. In the released NoMaD deployment code (explore.py), the context queue is filled by the camera callback at the configured 4 Hz, independently of how fast inference runs. A slow Pi therefore does not distort context spacing unless the team deliberately couples capture to inference. What actually degrades at low rate is action staleness/latency, which Real-Time Chunking already addresses. The 'distance spacing the training data implies' also does not exist as a single value: training metric_waypoint_spacing spans 0.06 to 1.59 m. On top of that, any Pi-vs-paper gap is confounded by camera FOV and embodiment shift (Guerrier et al. 2026 already report frequent NoMaD collisions on new platforms). The experiment may therefore measure neither compute nor context spacing cleanly. *Closest prior work:* [Sridhar, Shah, Glossop, Levine - NoMaD: Goal Masked Diffusion Policies for Navigation and Exploration (arXiv 2310.07896)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07896); [Guerrier, Soma, Pavlasek, Beltrame - Can Vision Foundation Models Navigate? Zero-Shot Real-World Evaluation and Lessons Learned (arXiv 2603.25937)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25937); [Black et al. - Real-Time Execution of Action Chunking Flow Policies (Real-Time Chunking, arXiv 2506.07339)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07339); [visualnav-transformer official deployment code (explore.py)](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer/main/deployment/src/explore.py); [visualnav-transformer robot.yaml (deployment config)](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer/main/deployment/config/robot.yaml); [visualnav-transformer data_config.yaml (training datasets)](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer/main/train/vint_train/data/data_config.yaml); [SAFER-Nav: Enhancing Safety for Visual Robot Navigation via Segmentation-Aware Fine-Tuning (arXiv 2606.11636; found via arXiv API listing, abstract not opened)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11636)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 4.5 / F 6.5 / I 5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* I found no paper or repo that measures NoMaD on a Raspberry Pi. My arXiv API listing for NoMaD returned nothing on edge or Pi deployment, and a GitHub repo search returned 0 results. So the question of where the compute floor lies is technically open. But the claimed insight is weak. In the official explore.py, context frames are buffered at the camera rate, independent of inference. The training data already mixes 0.06-0.72 m frame spacings with actions normalized by spacing (GNM design). Picking keyframes by distance traveled is a standard trick. What remains is mostly a cheaper re-deployment with a latency sweep. *Feasibility:* The software exists: a released checkpoint, a small 96x96 model with 10 diffusion steps, and the LeKiwi BOM. Bumper-counted collisions and 90 runs over about 5 weekends are realistic. The risks: (1) the official stack uses ROS, so the team must port it to ONNX on the Pi themselves. (2) NoMaD may perform poorly on LeKiwi in all three conditions, because the low camera, different field of view, slipping omni wheels and noisy servo odometry are all unlike the training robots. A floor effect would then hide any A/B/C difference. (3) Since the official code already decouples the context buffer, the 'naive' condition A may need to be built deliberately wrong. *Impact:* An honest curve of success versus inference rate for a navigation foundation model on a $60 computer is a reasonable result for an ICRA workshop, ARSO or SoutheastCon, and a good story for college applications. It is unlikely to be accepted at the IROS main track. The likely results are a null (the policy is rate-robust, which the mixed-spacing training data predicts) or 'the Pi is too slow to react', and either one reads as a deployment note rather than a new method. 2026 work already evaluates NoMaD zero-shot in the real world (arXiv 2603.25937) and pushes faster models (DevGRU, arXiv 2608.18470). *Main criticism:* The proposed failure mechanism does not match how NoMaD is actually deployed. In the released explore.py, the camera callback fills the context queue at the camera's frame rate, separately from the inference loop. So a slow Pi does not change context spacing. It adds action latency and stale waypoints. On top of that, the model was trained on datasets with frame spacing from 0.06 to 0.72 m and actions normalized by that spacing, so it is likely already robust to spacing. Condition A therefore risks being a strawman, and the headline insight is probably a null result. The true variable is control latency and replanning rate, which a sweep alone can measure. *Closest prior work:* [visualnav-transformer (official GNM/ViNT/NoMaD code), deployment README and explore.py](https://github.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer); [visualnav-transformer deployment/config/robot.yaml](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer/main/deployment/config/robot.yaml); [visualnav-transformer train data_config.yaml (metric_waypoint_spacing per dataset)](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer/main/train/vint_train/data/data_config.yaml); [NoMaD: Goal Masked Diffusion Policies for Navigation and Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07896); [Can Vision Foundation Models Navigate? Zero-Shot Real-World Evaluation and Lessons Learned (Guerrier, Soma, Pavlasek, Beltrame)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25937); [DevGRU: Depth-guided Visual Navigation using a Collision-aware Recurrent Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18470); [LeKiwi (SIGRobotics-UIUC)](https://github.com/SIGRobotics-UIUC/LeKiwi)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5.5 / F 6.5 / I 5.5 = 5.83. *Novelty:* I found no paper or project that runs NoMaD on a Raspberry Pi or measures the effect of context spacing against inference rate. That gap is real. But Guerrier et al. 2026 (arXiv 2603.25937) already re-evaluates NoMaD's real-world collisions independently, and DevGRU (2608.18470) already benchmarks its inference time. The proposed fix is close to what the released code already assumes: actions are normalized by metric waypoint spacing, and the deployment code ties waypoint scaling to frame rate. That makes the contribution a deployment-engineering tweak plus a measurement, not a new mechanism. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and off the shelf (LeKiwi base-only $251.50 plus a $60 Pi 5, both verified), the model is small (19M parameters, 96x96 inputs, 10 denoising steps), and 90 runs over about 5 weekends is doable. Three risks remain. First, porting the ROS1 deployment stack to a Pi without ROS. Second, a low camera height and a different field of view on LeKiwi, compared with LoCoBot, could make all three conditions fail and leave nothing to compare. Third, the core premise may not hold: the Pi may already run near the 4 Hz the authors deployed at (my estimate, unverified), which would erase the gap the study depends on. *Impact:* This is plausible for an ICRA/IROS workshop or ARSO as a 'navigation foundation models on a $60 computer' measurement study, and it makes a good college-application story. But it tests one model on one robot, three home courses and one trick. Without a clear positive effect, it is unlikely to clear IROS main-track review in a year when many NoMaD follow-ups already appear on arXiv (VANDERER, SAFER-Nav, SaferPath, DevGRU, ODG-NoMaD, all 2026). *Main criticism:* The study depends on a large compute-rate gap and a spacing mismatch that probably do not exist. The released model is light (96x96, 19M parameters) and was deployed at only 4 Hz and 0.2 m/s. Its training data already spans roughly a 10x range of metric frame spacing. So conditions A and B will likely perform about the same. Meanwhile, the camera-height and field-of-view shift from LoCoBot to LeKiwi, plus NoMaD's already-documented collision problems (Guerrier et al. 2026), may dominate all three conditions. The result would then measure embodiment shift, not compute. *Closest prior work:* [NoMaD: Goal Masked Diffusion Policies for Navigation and Exploration (Sridhar, Shah, Glossop, Levine)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07896); [Can Vision Foundation Models Navigate? Zero-Shot Real-World Evaluation and Lessons Learned (Guerrier, Soma, Pavlasek, Beltrame, arXiv Mar 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25937); [visualnav-transformer official deployment code and configs (robot.yaml, data_config.yaml, nomad.yaml)](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer/main/deployment/config/robot.yaml); [DevGRU: Depth-guided Visual Navigation using a Collision-aware Recurrent Model (Han, Kim, Kim, arXiv Aug 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18470); [SAFER-Nav: segmentation-aware fine-tuning for visual navigation (arXiv Jun 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11636); [LeKiwi low-cost mobile base BOM (SIGRobotics-UIUC)](https://github.com/SIGRobotics-UIUC/LeKiwi/blob/main/BOM.md)

### S2.v1 (round 1, median 5.50): Learning to see the 1-3 cm apron lip with a $25 camera, labelled by the robot's own bumps (vs. an active-stereo depth camera)

- **Question:** Can a $25 monocular camera, trained only on labels that the robot's IMU and wheel current generate automatically each time it physically crosses a lip, detect 1-3 cm driveway apron, gutter and threshold lips ahead of the robot as reliably as a $429 active-IR stereo depth camera (OAK-D Pro) on the same robot?
- **Hypothesis:** For lips at or under 3 cm, the proprioceptively supervised camera reaches detection AUC at least as high as the OAK-D Pro's geometric step detector at 0.5-1.5 m range, because the lip height is at or below the stereo depth noise floor at that range while the appearance cues (seam line, shadow, material change) are strong. For lips over 5 cm the depth camera wins. Falsified if the camera's AUC is more than 0.05 below the depth camera's for small lips under leave-one-site-out testing.
- **Measurements:** Both sensors log every approach simultaneously (paired). Sites: 10 lips across about 4 properties and school (aprons, gutter seams, garage thresholds, lifted slabs), heights measured with calipers (mm). Conditions: day vs dusk (2) x 8 approaches per site = 160 approaches, with each approach ending in a traversal that auto-labels it. Metrics: detection AUC, detection range at 95% precision (m), and lip-height estimation error (mm, continuous). Analysis: leave-one-site-out cross-validation (so learning cannot memorize sites), paired bootstrap CIs on AUC difference stratified by lip-height bin (<=3 cm, >3 cm). Power: about 80 small-lip approaches give a bootstrap AUC CI of roughly +/-0.04 (estimate).
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Luxonis OAK-D Pro, an active stereo depth camera with IR dot projector, US $429 (https://shop.luxonis.com/products/oak-d-pro, verified 2026-10-03). It answers the earlier reviewer objection that the OAK-D Lite (passive) was a weak reference. The team runs a standard ground-plane-fit + step detector on its depth stream. The substitute is a $25 Raspberry Pi Camera Module 3 (https://www.raspberrypi.com/products/camera-module-3/, 'from $25', verified). That is about a 17x cost reduction.
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: a lip the robot can feel is also a lip it can label. The traversal impulse from the IMU and current, back-projected through wheel odometry to the image frames taken 0.5-2 m earlier, yields free, exact-location labels. A tiny lip is geometrically below the depth noise of a $429 stereo camera at 1-2 m but visually salient, so the cheap camera may actually win on the hard case that reviewers said cheap sensors miss. Self-supervised traversability from proprioception exists for off-road terrain cost (How Does It Feel?, ICRA 2023; V-STRONG, ICRA 2024), and ToF+RGB depth exists (DELTAR, ECCV 2022; DEPTHOR, ICCV 2025). None measures a camera trained this way against an active depth camera on centimetre-scale urban lips. The outcome can go either way: the depth camera may resolve 2 cm steps fine, or dusk may kill the camera.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [How Does It Feel? Self-Supervised Costmap Learning for Off-Road Vehicle Traversability (ICRA 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra48891.2023.10160856); [DELTAR: Depth Estimation from a Light-Weight ToF Sensor and RGB Image (ECCV 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-19769-7_36); [DEPTHOR: Depth Enhancement from a Practical Light-Weight dToF Sensor and RGB Image (ICCV 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iccv51701.2025.00576); [WalkOCC / curb-detection literature (from prior_art.md gap map)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19122)
- **Build:** Mechanical: base robot (shared hoverboard platform) with a rigid sensor mast holding the Pi camera and OAK-D Pro side by side at about 30 cm, and an IMU on the front caster. Software: time-synchronized logging; a lip-event detector on IMU/current; odometry back-projection to auto-label image rows/patches; a small CNN (e.g. MobileNet-class segmentation, trained on a laptop GPU or Colab) running on a Pi 5; OAK-D Pro ground-plane + step detector baseline.
- **Cost:** $760: Shared base robot ~$270 (used hoverboard ~$60, Pi 5 ~$80, frame ~$80, wiring/ST-Link ~$50; all estimates); Pi Camera Module 3 $25 (verified); BNO085 $29.50 (verified); OAK-D Pro $429 (verified). Total ~$754, about $760. The sensor comparison is $25 vs $429.
- **Venue:** IROS 2027 (Mar 1): learning-based perception with a self-supervision mechanism and a strong hardware baseline is core IROS material.
- **Main risk:** Dusk images may be too dark, and some lips may be invisible to the camera, so the cheap sensor loses broadly. Fallback: add a $10 LED floodlight (still about 12x cheaper) and report the crossover lip height and lux level where the camera stops matching the depth camera, which is itself the 'where it breaks' result.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 6.5 / I 5.5 = 5.67. *Novelty:* Labelling camera images with the robot's own IMU, contact or traction signals and back-projecting through odometry is well established. BADGR (RA-L 2021) labels bumpiness from an IMU angular-velocity threshold on a wheeled robot in urban terrain. Wellhausen et al. (RA-L 2019), Howard et al. (JFR 2006), Brooks & Iagnemma (JFR 2012) and WayFAST (RA-L 2022) do the same with other robots and signals. So the 'insight' is not new. What is left is a defensible but thin new setting: discrete 1-3 cm lips, caliper ground truth, and a paired, height-stratified comparison against a specific active-stereo camera. Against the closest prior work I found, that is a known technique applied to a new setting with modest new measurements. *Feasibility:* The parts are off the shelf and the total cost (~$760, with OAK-D Pro US $429 confirmed on shop.luxonis.com) is fine. But the plan stacks several risky pieces in about 12 part-time weeks: time-synced dual-sensor logging, an IMU/current event detector, odometry back-projection, CNN training, a depth step-detector baseline, and 160 approaches at 10 sites in Dec-Feb weather and early dusk. The biggest risk is label quality: a 1 cm lip under hoverboard wheels (~6.5 in) may give an IMU/current impulse too weak to label reliably, which is exactly the regime the hypothesis depends on. Also, Luxonis gives the OAK-D Pro's 'ideal depth range' as ~80 cm-12 m at standard settings, so the 0.5-0.8 m part of the test range needs extended-disparity tuning, or the baseline is handicapped. *Impact:* An IROS main-track reviewer would see a single-robot study with 10 lips, about 80 small-lip approaches and a correlated-sample AUC comparison. The labelling mechanism has been published since 2006-2021. That makes main-track acceptance unlikely, but it is a respectable ARSO/SoutheastCon/ICRA-workshop paper. The 'cheap camera beats a $429 depth camera on small lips' headline, plus the crossover lip height and lux level, is a clear, citable result and a good application story if the comparison is clean. *Main criticism:* The claimed insight (proprioceptive self-labels back-projected to earlier camera frames) is already published, most directly in BADGR (IMU angular-velocity bumpiness labels for camera images on an urban wheeled robot), and the proposal does not cite it. The proposed head-to-head is also confounded: a learned CNN on the $25 camera competes against a hand-tuned ground-plane step detector on the $429 camera. A camera 'win' could then mean 'learning beats a heuristic' rather than 'the cheap sensor is as good', and IROS reviewers will spot this immediately. *Closest prior work:* [BADGR: An Autonomous Self-Supervised Learning-Based Navigation System (Kahn, Abbeel, Levine; IEEE RA-L 2021)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.05700); [Where Should I Walk? Predicting Terrain Properties From Images Via Self-Supervised Learning (Wellhausen et al., IEEE RA-L 2019)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2019.2895390); [Towards learned traversability for robot navigation: From underfoot to the far field (Howard et al., J. Field Robotics 2006)](https://doi.org/10.1002/rob.20168); [Self-supervised terrain classification for planetary surface exploration rovers (Brooks & Iagnemma, J. Field Robotics 2012)](https://doi.org/10.1002/rob.21408); [WayFAST: Navigation With Predictive Traversability in the Field (Gasparino et al., IEEE RA-L 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2022.3193464); [How Does It Feel? Self-Supervised Costmap Learning for Off-Road Vehicle Traversability (Castro et al., ICRA 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra48891.2023.10160856); [Lost and Found: detecting small road hazards for self-driving vehicles (Pinggera et al., IROS 2016)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.2016.7759186); [Curb detection for a pedestrian robot in urban environments (ICRA 2012)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2012.6224593)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 6 / I 5.5 = 5.50. *Novelty:* The proposal's headline insight, labeling the camera with the robot's own IMU bumps, is already published. BADGR (arXiv 2002.05700, verified in the PDF) labels 'bumpiness' with an IMU angular-velocity threshold and trains a camera model on those labels, and Stavens & Thrun 2006 trained forward sensors from accelerometer shock. The proposal cites neither. What is left is a new setting plus a new measurement: a paired, height-stratified A/B of a proprioception-trained $25 camera against an active stereo camera on 1-3 cm urban lips. That is real but thin differentiation. *Feasibility:* Every part is off-the-shelf and the total (~$754) is under budget. But the team has to build several risky pieces in about 12 part-time winter weeks:
- the hoverboard base itself;
- hardware time-sync between the Pi camera, OAK-D, IMU and wheel current;
- an IMU lip-event detector that must reject cracks, pebbles and expansion joints;
- odometry back-projection to image patches, which is sensitive to slip and to pitch at the moment of the bump;
- a CNN trained on only ~144 approaches per fold from 9 sites;
- 160 field approaches across 4+ properties in Dec-Feb weather (wet or snowy surfaces change appearance).
On the baseline side, the OAK-D's default MinZ is ~70 cm (Luxonis docs), so the 0.5 m end of the test band needs Extended Disparity, which adds another configuration risk. *Impact:* A clean answer to 'below what lip height does a cheap self-supervised camera beat a $429 active stereo camera' would be a nice result for ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA workshop, and it tells a good application story. IROS main track is unlikely: the mechanism is BADGR-era, the data is 10 sites and 160 approaches, and the baseline is a hand-tuned plane-fit detector rather than a learned depth model, which reviewers will call an unfair comparison. *Main criticism:* The self-supervision mechanism (IMU bump to camera label) is already established by BADGR (2020) and Stavens & Thrun (2006), and the proposal does not cite either. The actual contribution is therefore a sensor A/B on 10 lips, and that A/B is set up in ways that favor the camera:
- a hand-tuned geometric step detector is compared against a learned camera model;
- part of the range sits below the OAK-D's default ~70 cm minimum depth;
- the IR projector is likely ineffective in daylight.
A reviewer will say the camera 'won' because of an unequal baseline, not because of the cost insight. In addition, appearance cues such as seams and material changes may fire on flush seams with no lip, and the label pipeline (crack and pebble false bumps, odometry error at the bump) could produce noisy ground truth with only 160 approaches. *Closest prior work:* [BADGR: An Autonomous Self-Supervised Learning-Based Navigation System (Kahn, Abbeel, Levine; arXiv 2002.05700)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.05700); [A Self-Supervised Terrain Roughness Estimator for Off-Road Autonomous Driving (Stavens & Thrun, UAI 2006; arXiv 1206.6872)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1206.6872); [How Does It Feel? Self-Supervised Costmap Learning for Off-Road Vehicle Traversability (Castro, Triest, Wang, Gregory, Sanchez, Rogers, Scherer; ICRA 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra48891.2023.10160856); [STERLING: Self-Supervised Terrain Representation Learning from Unconstrained Robot Experience (Karnan et al., CoRL 2023; arXiv 2309.15302)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15302); [WalkOCC: Monocular 3D Occupancy Perception for Robots on Sidewalks via Hybrid 2D-3D Learning (arXiv 2606.19122, from prior_art.md)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19122); [Luxonis OAK-D Pro product docs (baseline sensor spec)](https://docs.luxonis.com/hardware/products/OAK-D%20Pro)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5 / F 6 / I 5.5 = 5.50. *Novelty:* The core insight, time-shifted proprioception supervising a monocular camera to predict upcoming curbs and steps, is already published as Cross-Modal Supervision (arXiv 2211.03785, curbs and steps up to 20 cm), and BADGR (2002.05700) did self-supervised image-based affordance learning on a wheeled robot. What is left is a real but thin axis: 1-3 cm lips from a low wheeled robot, compared paired against an active-stereo camera with cost on the axis. The proposal does not cite CMS, so its novelty claim is overstated. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and verified (OAK-D Pro $429 on the Luxonis shop page; the total of about $760 is fine). But the software stack is heavy for 12 part-time weeks: time-synced dual-sensor logging, an IMU/current event detector, odometry back-projection, CNN training, and a hand-built depth step detector. There are also circularities. The smallest lips (1 cm) are the ones a hoverboard wheel will feel least, so labels are noisiest exactly on the hard case. And 10 sites x 16 approaches is a small, correlated dataset for leave-one-site-out CNN generalization. Dec-Feb weather (snow, wet surfaces, early dusk) could also block outdoor sessions. *Impact:* An IROS reviewer who knows CMS/BADGR would read this as a known self-supervision recipe applied to a narrow task, with 160 approaches and a team-implemented depth baseline. That is plausible for an ARSO/SoutheastCon paper or an ICRA workshop, but a weak main-track IROS bet. It is a decent college-application story ('the robot labels its own data'). *Main criticism:* The headline insight (proprioception felt on crossing, back-projected in time to label a monocular camera's view of upcoming curbs and steps) is already published as Cross-Modal Supervision, and the 'cheap camera beats depth on small lips' premise rests on a noise-floor claim the OAK-D Pro spec does not support. The likely result is that a properly tuned active-stereo detector resolves 2-3 cm lips at 1 m, and the camera wins only where labels are noisiest. That leaves a narrow 160-approach benchmark against a self-built baseline, which an IROS reviewer will see as an incremental application. *Closest prior work:* [Learning Visual Locomotion with Cross-Modal Supervision (Loquercio, Kumar, Malik; arXiv 2211.03785)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.03785); [BADGR: An Autonomous Self-Supervised Learning-Based Navigation System (Kahn, Abbeel, Levine; arXiv 2002.05700)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.05700); [How Does It Feel? Self-Supervised Costmap Learning for Off-Road Vehicle Traversability (ICRA 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra48891.2023.10160856); [STERLING: Self-Supervised Terrain Representation Learning from Unconstrained Robot Experience (arXiv 2309.15302)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15302); [Wheelchair Behavior Recognition for Visualizing Sidewalk Accessibility by Deep Neural Networks (arXiv 2101.03724)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2101.03724); [Annotation-Free Detection of Drivable Areas and Curbs Leveraging LiDAR Point Cloud Maps (arXiv 2603.27553)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27553); [Monocular 3D Occupancy Perception for Robots on Sidewalks (WalkOCC, arXiv 2606.19122)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19122)

### R3.v1 (round 1, median 5.33): DRIVE slip-model learning on a $400 skid-steer: a $25 optical-flow sensor in place of lidar-ICP labels

- **Question:** Can DRIVE's automatic slip-model learning protocol (46 s of data, 34% / 38% median translational / rotational prediction-error reduction on Husky/Warthog with lidar-ICP localization) be reproduced on a hobby skid-steer robot when the body-velocity labels come from a downward optical-flow sensor instead of lidar ICP?
- **Hypothesis:** Using optical-flow body-velocity labels, the slip-based model will recover at least 80% of the error reduction obtained with labels from a calibrated overhead-camera tag tracker (the in-house 'expensive label' stand-in for ICP) on concrete and gravel. On grass the flow sensor will fall below 50% recovery, because varying ground height breaks its fixed focus. A second, competing hypothesis: a light 3 kg skid-steer slips proportionally more than a 75 kg Husky, so DRIVE's gain over the naive model will exceed the paper's 34%. Each claim is falsifiable separately.
- **Measurements:** 3 terrains (sealed concrete, gravel, short grass) x 10 independent DRIVE sessions each (about 3 min of logged data per session; models trained on the first 46 s and on the full set). Three model variants are trained per session: naive diff-drive, slip-BLR with flow labels, and slip-BLR with overhead-camera labels. Evaluation: DRIVE's 2-s-horizon multi-step translational and rotational RMSE on at least 200 held-out windows of random commands per session, with the overhead camera tracker (~1 cm accuracy, validated against a chalk grid) as the evaluation ground truth. Analysis: per-terrain paired comparisons across the 10 sessions (Wilcoxon) and a fraction-of-gain-recovered metric with bootstrap CIs. The paper's 46 s convergence curve is repeated with the flow labels.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Baril et al., 'DRIVE: Data-driven Robot Input Vector Exploration', ICRA 2024 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10718, code and data at github.com/norlab-ulaval/DRIVE). Confirmed details: Warthog (470 kg), Husky (75 kg), Superdroid HD2 (80 kg); ICP lidar localization; 2 s horizon at 20 Hz; 34% / 38% median error reduction; 46 s convergence. Reference cost: Husky $19-24k (third-party listing in prior_art.md) plus a 3D lidar (price UNVERIFIED). In-house baselines: the naive model, and the same learner trained on overhead-camera labels on the same robot.
- **Novelty claim:** The one insight: slip-model learning needs body velocity, not global position. A $25 mouse-type flow sensor measures body velocity directly at about 100 Hz, independent of wheel slip, so the expensive global-localization stack can be dropped for the learning step. The result is not foregone. Flow sensors drift with height changes, surface texture and lighting, and label noise may wipe out the slip signal on exactly the terrains where slip matters. Separately, whether DRIVE's gains grow or shrink on a light robot is untested (DRIVE's lightest platform is 75 kg). An arXiv abstract search for optical-flow-sensor slip learning on ground robots returned nothing. Optical-mouse odometry itself is old (pre-2010 literature, not linked here). Scholar/IEEE were not searched.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [DRIVE: Data-driven Robot Input Vector Exploration (Baril et al., ICRA 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10718); [Omobot: a low-cost mobile robot (IEEE AIM 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.05315); [Baltazar et al. low-cost RTK field robot (AgriEngineering 2024)](https://mdpi-res.com/d_attachment/agriengineering/agriengineering-06-00192/article_deploy/agriengineering-06-00192.pdf)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: hobby 4WD skid-steer with encoder gear motors, a rigid low mount for the PMW3901-class flow sensor plus a ToF height sensor, a top-plate AprilTag, and a ceiling or tall-tripod mount for a 4K webcam over a 4 x 6 m test patch on each terrain (garage floor, gravel strip, lawn). Software student: Pi 5 ROS 2 node logging encoders, IMU and flow at 100 Hz; port of DRIVE's released BLR code and command-generation protocol; overhead tag tracker with homography calibration; evaluation scripts reproducing DRIVE's 2-s RMSE.
- **Cost:** $420: Raspberry Pi 5 4GB $60 (per LeKiwi BOM). 4WD skid-steer chassis with encoder motors and driver ~$130 (estimate). PMW3901-class flow sensor ~$25 (estimate, UNVERIFIED). IMU ~$15 (estimate). 4K USB webcam for the overhead reference ~$70 (estimate). Tall tripod/ceiling mount ~$40 (estimate). Battery and charger ~$50 (estimate). Printed tags and chalk $10. Reference Husky + lidar not bought; at least 40x more expensive (Husky third-party price).
- **Venue:** IEEE/RSJ IROS 2027: learned vehicle models and field robotics. CASE is an alternative if framed as a low-cost automated model-identification procedure.
- **Main risk:** The flow sensor may fail outright on grass or in winter low light, and the robot's weather windows are limited. Fallback: add an LED ring for the flow sensor, restrict the headline to hard surfaces, and report grass as a failure envelope with the overhead-camera-label model still reproducing DRIVE there. Weather slips are absorbed by running concrete and gravel first.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 6 / I 5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* Each part is already published: DRIVE's protocol and code (arXiv 2309.10718, GitHub), optical-flow and mouse-sensor ground odometry and its height and focus problem (ICCAS 2015 afocal sensor; Automatika 2016), and slip learning on skid-steers (a 2026 1-D CNN preprint, 2024 multi-surface slip-compensation comparisons). What is new is the combination: cheap flow labels inside DRIVE's protocol, measured as a fraction of the gain recovered, on a 3 kg robot. That is a real but thin difference, essentially a cheaper re-implementation plus a label-source ablation. *Feasibility:* The software risk is low, because DRIVE's code and data are public and the PMW3901 has hobby drivers. The ground-truth rig is the weak point. Covering a 4 x 6 m patch outdoors on gravel and grass with one 4K webcam needs a camera mounted roughly 4-6 m high (my estimate), which a $40 tripod cannot provide. The plan also calls for 30 two-hour outdoor sessions on January-February weekends, a large matrix for winter weather and short daylight. Expect grass and possibly gravel to slip. *Impact:* At best this is a reproduction or label-source study on a toy-scale robot. It suits ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA workshop on field robotics or reproducibility, and is unlikely at IROS main track: a 3 kg hobby robot at low speed is not a platform that IROS field-robotics reviewers will see as relevant to DRIVE's large-UGV use case, and the paper never compares against real lidar-ICP labels. For college applications, it is a respectable quantitative replication story. *Main criticism:* The central claim, that cheap body-velocity labels can replace lidar ICP for slip learning, is never tested against lidar ICP or a real research platform. The reference label is a cheap overhead webcam, and the robot is a 3 kg toy whose slip behavior may be dominated by gear-motor deadband rather than terrain. A reviewer would see "a known sensor (optical-flow odometry, studied since before 2010) substituted into a released pipeline (DRIVE) on a robot outside the method's target class". The result is easy to foresee: it works on concrete and fails on grass. The rotational half also reduces to a gyro, which nobody doubts. *Closest prior work:* [DRIVE: Data-driven Robot Input Vector Exploration (Baril et al., ICRA 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10718); [norlab-ulaval/DRIVE code repository](https://github.com/norlab-ulaval/DRIVE); [Afocal optical flow sensor for mobile robot odometry (ICCAS 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iccas.2015.7364858); [Optical flow based odometry for mobile robots supported by multiple sensors and sensor fusion (Automatika 2016)](https://doi.org/10.7305/automatika.2016.07.886); [Mobile Robot Localization Using Optical Mouse Sensor and Encoder Based on Kalman Filter Algorithm (IJCA 2017)](https://doi.org/10.14257/ijca.2017.10.6.07); [Slip-Aware Wheel Odometry for 4-Wheeled Skid-Steer Mobile Robots Using a Compact 1-D CNN (Research Square preprint, 2026)](https://doi.org/10.21203/rs.3.rs-9959755/v1); [Comparison of slip-compensated odometry for various skid-steer suspensions on different ground surfaces (Research Square preprint, 2024)](https://doi.org/10.21203/rs.3.rs-4777944/v1); [Evaluation of Skid-Steering Kinematic Models for Subarctic Environments (Baril et al., CRV 2020)](https://doi.org/10.1109/crv50864.2020.00034); [A Computer Vision-Based Method for Collecting Ground Truth for Mobile Robot Odometry (ICEIS 2024)](https://doi.org/10.5220/0012622900003690)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 6 / I 5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* Neither the parts nor the question is new. Optical-mouse/flow body-velocity sensing on mobile robots has its own IEEE literature (ICRA 2010, ICARCV 2010 outdoor, AIM 2013, ICCAS 2015 afocal flow sensor), and DRIVE already supplies the learner and protocol. What is new is pairing flow labels with DRIVE's slip-BLR on a 3 kg robot. The grass-failure hypothesis is predictable, because the afocal-sensor and outdoor-compensation papers exist precisely because ground height breaks these sensors. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap. The DRIVE repo is ROS 2 Humble with Python/Pandas training for generic diff-drive UGVs, which makes porting plausible (repo page checked). Three things are risky. First, an overhead 4K webcam covering 4 x 6 m needs roughly a 4 m mount on gravel and grass outdoors in winter, where it is exposed to wind. Second, a fast hobby robot will hit the edge of a 4 x 6 m patch within seconds under random-step commands. Third, 30 two-hour sessions on winter weekends is a lot. Grass is the likely casualty. *Impact:* A careful 'cheap labels recover X% of the gain' study would fit ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA workshop. IROS reviewers would see a replication with a sensor swap that does not ask a new question. The headline baseline number (34%/38%) is also misattributed, and a reviewer who knows DRIVE would catch it immediately. *Main criticism:* The contribution is a sensor swap in a known pipeline, aimed at a misread baseline. The 34%/38% gain the angle wants to 'recover' belongs to DRIVE's powertrain-aware model versus naive, not to slip-BLR. Slip-BLR adds only 6% translation over acceleration-BLR, and nothing on easy terrain. On a 3 kg robot on concrete, the learnable slip signal may therefore be smaller than the flow-label noise, and the result could be null. Flow-sensor body-velocity odometry, and its height and texture failure modes, already have IEEE papers, so the grass envelope is predictable. A $70 overhead webcam is itself a cheap label source, which weakens the 'replace lidar ICP' story, because the expensive component the angle replaces never appears in its experiments. *Closest prior work:* [DRIVE: Data-driven Robot Input Vector Exploration (Baril et al., ICRA 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10718); [Afocal optical flow sensor for mobile robot odometry (ICCAS 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iccas.2015.7364858); [Analysis of real-time velocity compensation for outdoor optical mouse sensor odometry (ICARCV 2010)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icarcv.2010.5707970); [Location of optical mouse sensors on mobile robots for odometry (ICRA 2010)](https://doi.org/10.1109/robot.2010.5509317); [Planar Velocity Estimation for Fast-Moving Mobile Robots Using Event-Based Optical Flow (arXiv 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11116); [Trajectory Tracking Control of Skid-Steering Mobile Robots with Slip and Skid Compensation using Sliding-Mode Control and Deep Learning (arXiv 2023)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.08863)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5 / F 5.5 / I 5 = 5.17. *Novelty:* I found no paper that trains a DRIVE-style slip model from optical-flow labels on a robot of about 3 kg, so the exact combination looks open. But the core insight, that flow sensors measure body motion independently of wheel slip, dates to Lee & Song (ICRA 2004) and Nagai et al. (IROS 2010). DRIVE's own 2025 T-FR follow-up (arXiv 2506.16593) already covers grass, gravel and asphalt, so to a reviewer this reads as a cheaper-sensor reproduction study with modest new measurements. *Feasibility:* The concrete-only core is doable. Several pieces are risky:
- The sensor choice is confused. The PMW3901 needs at least 80 mm and outputs angular flow (Pimoroni lists '~80mm to infinity', 121 FPS), so metric velocity depends on a ToF height reading that is noisy on grass. True mouse-type sensors need 10-27 mm (SparkFun) and would scrape on gravel and grass.
- Covering 4 x 6 m outdoors needs a stable camera mast about 3.5-4 m high (my estimate for a ~78-degree FOV webcam), not a $40 tripod.
- 30 two-hour outdoor sessions between mid-January and mid-February, with grass in winter, is optimistic.
- Porting DRIVE's code and command protocol to a ROS 2 hobby robot is a lot for one software student. *Impact:* An IROS reviewer will see a reproduction study whose 'expensive' reference is a $70 webcam, not lidar-ICP. It never measures the head-to-head cost/performance claim against the 75-470 kg platforms in DRIVE and its T-FR follow-up. That puts it at the level of an ICRA/IROS workshop or a student conference, not the IROS/CASE main track. The college-application story is respectable but not standout. *Main criticism:* The experiment never tests the 'same capability, much cheaper' claim it is framed around. The expensive-label stand-in is itself a $70 overhead webcam, and nothing is compared against lidar-ICP or a Husky-class result. The study reduces to 'one cheap sensor vs another cheap sensor on a toy robot.' Meanwhile, the key insight (optical flow measures body velocity regardless of wheel slip) has been published since 2004 (Lee & Song, ICRA). The PMW3901-vs-mouse-sensor confusion also means the grass and gravel results may come down to sensor mounting rather than science. *Closest prior work:* [DRIVE: Data-driven Robot Input Vector Exploration (Baril et al., ICRA 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10718); [DRIVE Through the Unpredictability: From a Protocol Investigating Slip to a Metric Estimating Command Uncertainty (Samson et al., IEEE T-FR 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.16593); [Robust mobile robot localization using optical flow sensors and encoders (Lee & Song, ICRA 2004)](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/abstract/document/1307287/); [Noncontact position estimation device with optical sensor and laser sources for mobile robots traversing slippery terrains (Nagai, Watanabe, Nagatani, IROS 2010)](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/abstract/document/5650346/); [The optical mouse for indoor mobile robot odometry measurement (Palacin et al., Sensors & Actuators A 2006); Implementation and calibration of an odometry system for mobile robots based on optical computer mouse sensors (Paijens et al., 2020)](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0924424705005406); [Displacement estimation based on optical and inertial sensor fusion (Ursel & Olinski, Sensors 2021)](https://www.mdpi.com/1424-8220/21/4/1390); [Visual-based kinematics and pose estimation for skid-steering robots (Zuo et al., IEEE Trans., 2022)](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/abstract/document/9926038/); [SparkFun Optical Tracking Odometry Sensor PAA5160E1 ($84.95, marketed to FIRST Tech Challenge teams)](https://www.sparkfun.com/sparkfun-optical-tracking-odometry-sensor-paa5160e1-qwiic.html)

### R5.v1 (round 1, median 5.33): Lidar-grade bin pose from a $13 camera: a deliberate weave during approach in place of the 5-sensor trolley-docking stack

- **Question:** Xiao et al. (ICRA 2022) reach 0.03 m / 0.02 rad pose accuracy at short range for docking to an unmodified wheeled cart using lidar plane fitting on a 5-sensor stack. Can a single cheap monocular camera match that on an unmodified 64/96-gal trash cart by triangulating keypoints across frames while the robot weaves deliberately on approach?
- **Hypothesis:** Single-frame keypoint PnP will be roughly as accurate as the paper's long-range camera stage (on the order of 0.1-0.2 m / 0.1 rad). Multi-frame triangulation on a straight approach will barely help, because the target sits at the epipole (no parallax in the direction of travel). A +/-15 cm lateral weave over the last 2 m will reach <=3 cm / <=2 deg (0.035 rad) error at 0.5 m range, statistically non-inferior to an LD19 2D-lidar line fit on the same runs. Falsified if weave error at 0.5 m exceeds 5 cm, or does not beat the straight approach.
- **Measurements:** 80 approaches: 40 straight and 40 weave, randomized and interleaved, from start poses sampled over +/-0.5 m lateral and +/-30 deg yaw at 3 m. 3 carts (2 brands, 64 and 96 gal), with lighting (sun/overcast/dusk) logged as a covariate, not a crossed factor. Each run is recorded once, and single-frame PnP, triangulation and LD19 line-fit estimates are all computed from the same log, so the baselines are paired within each run. Ground truth: the cart is placed on chalk-grid marks (tape-measured to about 5 mm / 1 deg) and robot pose comes from wheel odometry, reset against a floor mark per run and checked against the final measured stop pose. Metrics: position and yaw error (cm, deg) at 2 m, 1 m and 0.5 m. Analysis: paired differences with bootstrap 95% CIs, and a TOST non-inferiority test vs LD19 with a pre-registered 1.5 cm margin. With error SD about 2 cm (estimate), 40 runs per arm gives a CI half-width of about 0.6 cm, so the test is powered.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Xiao et al., 'Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection with Progressive Perception and Nonlinear Model Predictive Control', ICRA 2022 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648). Sensors: a 3D lidar, two 2D lidars, a solid-state lidar and RGB-D. Long-range PnP 0.17 m / 0.11 rad, short-range lidar plane 0.03 m / 0.02 rad (verified in prior_art.md). Reference hardware cost is not stated; the team's estimate is several thousand USD in sensors (UNVERIFIED). Code release UNVERIFIED, so the protocol (pose error vs range) is replicated rather than the code. In-house strong baseline: a ~$96 LD19 2D lidar line/plane fit on every run, so the cheap camera is tested against a real range sensor, not a straw man.
- **Novelty claim:** The one insight: on a docking approach the target lies at the focus of expansion, which makes motion stereo degenerate. A small, deliberate lateral weave creates the parallax, giving a metric baseline from wheel odometry for free, with the most accuracy in the final metre where docking needs it. This contradicts the usual coarse-camera / fine-range-sensor pipeline (Xiao; Lei et al. 2024 with TF40 rangefinders and QR markers). The result is not foregone. Odometry error during the weave, keypoint jitter on the glossy HDPE carts, and brand-to-brand geometry differences could all keep error above the lidar's, and the weave costs time. This is a perception result with a published numeric target, not another hitch comparison (the criticism in rounds.md). An arXiv search for monocular cart-pose docking returned nothing; Scholar/IEEE/patents were NOT searched (budget exhausted).
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection with Progressive Perception and Nonlinear MPC (Xiao et al., ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [High-precision docking of wheelchair/beds through LIDAR and visual information (Lei et al., 2024)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11408198/); [A Systematic Evaluation of Indoor Localization Methods in Robotic Luggage Trolley Collection (Sun et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551); [StreetView-Waste multi-task dataset (WACV 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16440)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: reuse the hoverboard bin-robot base (or a LeKiwi base for a faster start), a rigid camera and LD19 mount at handle height, and a 3 x 4 m chalk grid on the driveway with cart placement jigs (no modification to the carts). Software student: label about 600 images of the team's carts with 6 keypoints (handle-bar ends, lid hinge ends, wheel hubs) and train a small YOLO-pose model; PnP plus odometry-baseline multi-view triangulation (bundle adjustment over a 2 m window); weave trajectory controller; LD19 line-fit baseline; evaluation scripts.
- **Cost:** $380: Raspberry Pi 5 4GB $60 (per LeKiwi BOM). USB camera ~$13 (LeKiwi BOM lists 2 for $25.96). LD19 2D lidar $95.99 (Waveshare listing in prior_art.md, now marked discontinued; LD06-class substitute assumed similar, UNVERIFIED). Used hoverboard base ~$80 (estimate, UNVERIFIED). Mounts, deck and caster ~$70 (estimate). Chalk, tape measures and jigs ~$25 (estimate). Carts: household-owned plus a borrowed neighbour's second brand ($0). Reference 5-sensor stack not bought; likely more than 10x more expensive (UNVERIFIED).
- **Venue:** IEEE CASE 2027 (Mar 1): an automation-oriented perception-for-docking result with a cost/accuracy framing fits CASE's engineering emphasis. IROS 2027 is the alternative if the active-perception angle is emphasized.
- **Main risk:** Keypoints may not transfer across cart brands, or the glossy plastic may give unstable detections, which would inflate triangulation error. Fallback: use the most consistent geometry (axle/wheel hubs and handle bar), report per-brand results, and keep LD19 as a co-primary method. A finding that weave-camera matches lidar only on one brand is still a quantified, publishable gap.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 4.5 / F 6 / I 5.5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* The 'one insight' is textbook. Motion stereo is degenerate at the focus of expansion, and bearing-only range is observable only when the observer maneuvers. Both are classic results (bearings-only target motion analysis, active structure-from-motion), so 'weave beats straight approach' is predictable from geometry. That is the kind of foreseeable comparison rounds.md penalized. The application to unmodified refuse carts, with a paired cheap-lidar baseline, is new as far as I could check. However, the search API budget ran out (WebSearch was capped at 200 calls, and arXiv, OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar all returned HTTP 429), so the trailer-hitch and active-SfM literature went unchecked and the novelty may be lower than scored. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and off the shelf, and 80 runs at under 3 minutes each fits the schedule. Two technical problems remain. (1) At 0.5 m a camera at handle height cannot see both the handle-bar ends and the wheel hubs (roughly 0.9 m of vertical extent against a vertical field of view of about 0.45 m), so the 6-keypoint set breaks down exactly where accuracy is scored. (2) Hoverboard hub-motor odometry is coarse (Hall-sensor resolution; exact tick count UNVERIFIED) and slips during the weave. That odometry is both the triangulation baseline and the ground-truth robot pose, so the 1.5 cm TOST margin is about the size of the ground-truth error. Flashing the hoverboard firmware adds a further risk. *Impact:* A clean result ('a $13 camera plus a 15 cm weave matches a $96 lidar to within 1.5 cm on unmodified carts') would suit ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA workshop and makes a good application story. A CASE or IROS main-track reviewer will likely see it as known active-SfM applied to a new object, with a strawman straight-approach baseline. The comparison with Xiao's 5-sensor stack is also not like-for-like: the 0.17 m camera figure is long-range, while this angle scores at 0.5 m. *Main criticism:* The headline finding (lateral parallax beats a straight approach) follows from textbook geometry: the focus-of-expansion degeneracy and the need for an observer maneuver. So the experiment mostly confirms a known result against a strawman baseline. The baseline that matters, single-frame PnP at close range with a known cart model, is mis-hypothesized as being decimetre-level. On top of that, the ground truth reuses the same wheel odometry the method depends on, and is not accurate enough to support a 1.5 cm non-inferiority claim against a lidar rated at +/-45 mm. *Closest prior work:* [Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection with Progressive Perception and Nonlinear MPC (Xiao et al., ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [High-precision docking of wheelchair/beds through LIDAR and visual information (Lei et al., 2024)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11408198/); [A Systematic Evaluation of Indoor Localization Methods in Robotic Luggage Trolley Collection (Sun et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551); [Active Structure from Motion / active depth estimation by optimizing camera motion (e.g., Spica, Robuffo Giordano and Chaumette, IEEE T-RO c. 2014)](UNVERIFIED - no link opened (search budget exhausted)); [Bearings-only target motion analysis: observability requires an observer maneuver (classic literature, e.g., Nardone & Aidala, IEEE TAES c. 1981)](UNVERIFIED - no link opened (search budget exhausted)); [Monocular trailer-coupler detection and localization for automated hitching (e.g., Atoum et al., ICCV 2017 workshops; OEM hitch-assist systems)](UNVERIFIED - no link opened (search budget exhausted))
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 6 / I 5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* The weave-for-parallax idea is the classic bearing-only observability maneuver (own-ship maneuvers in arXiv 2405.05937 and 2302.01729) and the deliberate self-induced motion of de Croon (arXiv 1506.01153). Keypoint+PnP pose of an unmodified cart is already in Xiao et al. (arXiv 2110.06648). What remains new is a measured camera-vs-cheap-lidar docking-pose comparison on outdoor refuse carts. That difference is real but thin, and the epipole premise is overstated. Only a point exactly at the focus of expansion lacks parallax. Handle ends about 0.3 m off-axis sit roughly 17 degrees from the FOE at 1 m (my estimate), so a straight approach still gives usable parallax. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and the software is standard (YOLO-pose, PnP, small bundle adjustment), and 80 runs fit in the schedule. The problem is ground truth. During motion, robot pose comes from wheel odometry, the same odometry that supplies the triangulation baseline. That makes the errors correlated (circular). Odometry drift of about 1-3% over 3 m with a weave (my estimate) is comparable to the 3 cm target, and the 1-degree placement accuracy is half of the 2-degree target. A second risk: at 0.5 m, a cart roughly 1.07 m tall (estimate) will not fit a cheap camera's vertical FOV, so wheel-hub and handle keypoints will be truncated exactly where the hypothesis is tested. *Impact:* A clean outcome either way ('PnP alone is already within X cm at 0.5 m' or 'the weave closes the gap to a $96 lidar') would suit SoutheastCon, ARSO or an ICRA workshop. CASE/IROS main track is unlikely. The method is a known observability maneuver, the in-house lidar comparator is a ±45 mm-spec hobby unit rather than the 'lidar-grade' stack in the title, and reviewers will attack the odometry-based ground truth. *Main criticism:* The central premise and the measurement both look weak. The claimed degeneracy (target at the epipole) applies only to points on the approach axis. A known-geometry PnP needs no parallax at all, and at 0.5-1 m single-frame PnP on a measured cart model will plausibly already reach a few cm, which would make the weave unnecessary. Meanwhile, ground truth during motion comes from the same wheel odometry that provides the triangulation baseline, and its drift is about the size of the 3 cm effect being tested. The experiment cannot cleanly tell 3 cm from 5 cm error, and the weave idea itself is the textbook bearing-only observability maneuver. *Closest prior work:* [Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection with Progressive Perception and Nonlinear MPC (Xiao et al., ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [Distance estimation with efference copies and optical flow maneuvers: a stability-based strategy (de Croon)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1506.01153); [Bearing-only tracking literature requiring own-ship maneuvers for observability (e.g., Singh, Kumar, Bhaumik; Das, Tiwari, Bhaumik)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.05937); [A Systematic Evaluation of Indoor Localization Methods in Robotic Luggage Trolley Collection (Sun et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551); [High-precision docking of wheelchair/beds through LIDAR and visual information (Lei et al., 2024)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11408198/)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 4.5 / F 6 / I 5 = 5.17. *Novelty:* The 'one insight' is textbook. Bearing-only target-motion analysis states 'the own-ship is required to perform a manoeuvre in order to make the system observable' (arXiv 2405.05937), and active SfM that controls camera motion for observability appeared at IROS 2018 (arXiv 1807.00753). What remains new is applying it to outdoor refuse-cart docking and a paired comparison against a cheap lidar. My arXiv searches for trash-bin pose estimation and for monocular active-perception docking returned zero hits, so the application gap is real but thin. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and verified (LeKiwi BOM: Pi 5 4GB $60, USB camera $12.98), and YOLO-pose, PnP and small bundle adjustment are well trodden. The evaluation design is the problem. Robot ground-truth pose comes from the same wheel odometry that supplies the triangulation baseline, so their errors are correlated and not independent. A hoverboard base that weaves over 3 m can easily drift several cm, which is the same size as the 3 cm target and well above the 1.5 cm TOST margin. Labelling 600 images and running 80 outdoor January-February runs is doable, but tight during the school term. *Impact:* This is a respectable student and workshop result (ARSO, SoutheastCon, an ICRA workshop). An IROS or CASE main-track reviewer would likely say the insight is classical observability, and that PnP on a known-geometry object already resolves scale at close range. The likely 'weave barely beats straight PnP at 0.5 m' outcome would weaken the headline further. *Main criticism:* The premise rests on a degeneracy that does not apply to this setup, and the insight itself is textbook. Motion stereo is degenerate at the focus of expansion only when the object's geometry is unknown. The team labels keypoints on a known cart model, so single-frame PnP already has metric scale, and its error shrinks roughly with range squared.
