That makes straight-approach PnP at 0.5 m likely already cm-level, and the weave's benefit may be null or within noise. Also, 'maneuver to make bearing-only range observable' is classical (bearing-only TMA; active SfM, IROS 2018). And ground truth for robot pose comes from the same wheel odometry the method uses, so the 1.5 cm non-inferiority test cannot be trusted. *Closest prior work:* [Active Structure-from-Motion for 3D Straight Lines (Mateus, Tahri, Miraldo, IROS 2018)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1807.00753); [Dynamics of a Towed Cable with Sensor-Array for Underwater Target Motion Analysis (arXiv 2405.05937)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.05937); [Resolving Left-Right Ambiguity During Bearing Only Tracking (arXiv 2302.01729)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.01729); [Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection with Progressive Perception and NMPC (Xiao et al., ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [A Systematic Evaluation of Indoor Localization Methods in Robotic Luggage Trolley Collection (Sun et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551); [High-precision docking of wheelchair/beds through LIDAR and visual information (Lei et al., 2024)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11408198/)

### S1.v1 (round 1, median 5.33): Feeling the driveway: concrete control joints as free landmarks for proprioceptive garage-to-curb localization (vs. RTK GNSS)

- **Question:** On a slow (0.3-0.8 m/s) hoverboard-based robot, can the impulse sequence that the driveway's saw-cut control joints, garage threshold and apron seam produce in a $30 IMU and the hub-motor phase current, matched against a taught joint map, give along-track position error at the curb comparable to a ZED-F9P RTK receiver, including inside the garage where RTK has no fix?
- **Hypothesis:** Joint-matched proprioceptive localization keeps along-track error at the curb stop within 5 cm (95th percentile) with and without a towed cart, matching or beating RTK-plus-odometry at the curb and clearly beating it from the garage interior through the garage mouth. It is falsified if joint detection recall drops below 90% at 0.3 m/s or when towing a full cart, or if curb-stop error exceeds RTK's by more than 3 cm.
- **Measurements:** Paired design: every run logs both stacks at once (IMU+current joint matcher, and ZED-F9P RTK + wheel odometry). Primary metric: along-track error (cm) at about 6 chalk-marked, tape-surveyed checkpoints and at the final curb stop. Secondary metrics: joint detection recall/precision and the impulse SNR (dB) as a function of speed. Conditions: 2 speeds (0.3, 0.7 m/s) x 2 loads (no cart, full 96-gal cart), 15 runs each, so 60 runs on the home driveway plus 20 runs on a held-out second driveway whose joint map is taught once. Power: the paired error difference with sd of about 5 cm over 15 runs gives a 95% CI of about +/-2.8 cm per cell and about +/-1.3 cm pooled, with roughly 6 checkpoints per run. Analysis: mixed model (run as random effect) of error by stack x zone (garage interior, mouth, open driveway, curb). RTK fix and float fraction is logged per zone.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** The expensive reference is ZED-F9P RTK, which robots and the team's earlier plan use for driveway localization: ArduSimple simpleRTK2B starter kit at $263.75 each, so about $527 for a rover+base pair (https://www.ardusimple.com/product/simplertk2b-basic-starter-kit-ip65/, from prior_art.md; SparkFun's ZED-F9P board is $259.95, https://www.sparkfun.com/sparkfun-gps-rtk2-board-zed-f9p-qwiic-gps-15136.html). The team mounts it on the same robot and logs it in every run. The substitute adds only a $29.50 BNO085 IMU (https://www.adafruit.com/product/4754, verified) and reads current already exposed by the EFeru hoverboard FOC firmware. That is about an 18x cost reduction on the localization sensor.
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: residential concrete is cut into slabs every few metres, and each saw-cut joint, the garage threshold and the apron-to-gutter seam deliver a sharp, repeatable impulse to a small rigid caster. A short sequence of these impulses is an absolute along-track fingerprint that needs no sky view, so it works exactly where RTK fails (garage interior and mouth). Prior terrain/road-profile localization (Dean/Brennan ACC 2008; Gim & Ahn 2018) is car-scale at road speed, with suspension-filtered continuous profiles. Whether a 6-10 mm joint is detectable at 0.3 m/s through soft hub-motor tyres, and whether a towed cart's jolts mask it, is genuinely open. A reviewer would be surprised if a $30 IMU matches RTK at the curb. A plausible negative outcome is that impulses vanish at low speed or under tow, and the paper would then report that detectability boundary (SNR vs speed and load).
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Terrain-based road vehicle localization using particle filters (ACC 2008)](https://doi.org/10.1109/acc.2008.4586497); [IMU-Based Virtual Road Profile Sensor for Vehicle Localization (Gim & Ahn, Sensors 2018)](https://doi.org/10.3390/s18103344); [Vehicle localization using its vibration caused by road surface roughness (ICVES 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icves.2015.7396909); [Janos & Kuras, RTK accuracy in obstructed environments (from prior_art.md)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8401267/)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: hoverboard-hub differential base with a rigid, hard-wheeled rear caster carrying the IMU (stiff mount to maximize impulse transfer), a simple passive cart hitch, and an RTK antenna mast. Software student: flash the EFeru FOC firmware to stream phase current and wheel ticks at 100+ Hz; BNO085 at 400 Hz; joint detector (band-pass + matched filter on vertical accel and current); a 1D particle filter matching the detected joint sequence to a map taught in one drive; ROS2 logging of RTK (RTKLIB or u-blox fix) for comparison.
- **Cost:** $780: Used hoverboard ~$60 (estimate, unverified); ST-Link + wiring ~$20 (estimate); Raspberry Pi 5 ~$80 (estimate; prior_art lists only the 16GB at $305); BNO085 $29.50 (Adafruit, verified); frame/hitch/caster materials ~$80 (estimate); 2x ArduSimple simpleRTK2B kits $527.50 (verified in prior_art). Total ~$797, about $780. Use a public NTRIP caster instead of a second kit if one is in range (UNVERIFIED) to drop it to ~$515. The substitute itself costs $29.50 against ~$527 for RTK.
- **Venue:** IEEE/RSJ IROS 2027 (Mar 1). A new proprioceptive localization mechanism with an on-robot head-to-head fits IROS better than CASE. CASE is the backup if results become mainly an engineering cost study.
- **Main risk:** Soft tyres and low speed may leave joint impulses below the noise floor, or the driveway may have filled joints. Fallback: put the sensing in the hard caster and add a $5 piezo disc contact mic on the caster fork. If that also fails, publish the detectability map (SNR vs speed, load and joint depth) together with the RTK garage-mouth failure data as the result.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5.5 / F 5.5 / I 5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* The setting is new: I found no paper using slab control joints for a small, slow robot. But the mechanism (discrete-irregularity and terrain-signature localization from IMU/acceleration, matched against a taught map) is established in Wheel-SLAM (RA-L 2023, wheel-mounted IMU on a ground vehicle), DLR's axle-box rail positioning (ITSC 2023, rail joints and welds as signal) and haptic MCL on quadrupeds. The proposal cites none of these. What remains is "known technique in a new domain, plus a detectability study." The RTK-fails-in-the-garage half is foreseeable, the same objection reviewers raised in rounds.md. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and the software is simple: band-pass, matched filter, 1D particle filter, and EFeru firmware that already streams current. The core physics is risky, though. By my own geometry estimate (not measured), a narrow saw-cut under an approximately 83 mm-radius hub tyre produces a vertical dip of only hundredths of a millimetre, and about 0.2 mm under a 25 mm caster. Detection would therefore rely on slab-edge faulting or spalling, and on joints not being filled with sealant. Both vary from driveway to driveway. Add an 80-run matrix with RTK base/NTRIP setup and towing a full 96-gal cart, all part-time during school, and the plan is tight. The October hand-push go/no-go is a good mitigation and should be done immediately. *Impact:* Even a positive result is a 1-D along-track localizer on one or two driveways, judged against a strawman baseline. That is ARSO, SoutheastCon or ICRA-workshop level, not an IROS main-track contribution, especially once Wheel-SLAM and the rail work are cited. The fallback "SNR vs speed/load" detectability map is a weak paper on its own. It is a good story for college applications ("$30 IMU feels the driveway"). *Main criticism:* The baseline is the wrong one. RTK cannot work in a garage, so "beats RTK inside the garage" is foreseeable, the same objection reviewers already raised. On a 10-20 m concrete driveway from a known parking start, plain hoverboard wheel odometry (free) may already reach a few cm along-track error without towing. The real question is whether joint impulses beat odometry-only (and odometry plus a $5 threshold switch or fiducial) under towing slip, and the plan does not test that. Meanwhile the mechanism itself (terrain or discrete-irregularity signatures from a cheap IMU matched against a map) is already published for wheeled ground vehicles (Wheel-SLAM, RA-L 2023) and rail (DLR ITSC 2023). And the physical signal from a narrow, possibly sealed saw-cut under a large soft tyre may be close to zero. *Closest prior work:* [Wheel-SLAM: Simultaneous Localization and Terrain Mapping Using One Wheel-Mounted IMU (Wu, Kuang, Niu, Behley, Klingbeil, Kuhlmann; IEEE RA-L 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2022.3226071); [On the Use of Axle-Box Acceleration Data for Rail Vehicle Positioning (Heusel, del Alamo, Baasch, Roth; DLR; IEEE ITSC 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/itsc57777.2023.10422565); [Haptic Sequential Monte Carlo Localization for Quadrupedal Locomotion in Vision-Denied Scenarios (arXiv 2005.01567) and Navigating by Touch: Haptic Monte Carlo Localization (arXiv 2108.08015)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2005.01567); [Terrain-based road vehicle localization using particle filters (ACC 2008), cited by the proposal](https://doi.org/10.1109/acc.2008.4586497); [MagLand: Magnetic Landmarks for Road Vehicle Localization (IEEE TVT 2020)](https://doi.org/10.1109/tvt.2020.2977599)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 4.5 / F 5.5 / I 5 = 5.00. *Novelty:* The central insight, that discrete impulses from expansion joints sensed by a low-cost IMU and matched to a map give absolute along-track position, is already published as 'Road-Pulse' (IEEE TVT 2024, doi 10.1109/TVT.2023.3318240), which the proposal misses. Gim & Ahn (ACC 2020) and Ahmed et al. (VTC 2016) are also missing. What is left is a scale-down to a slow small robot, plus a garage-mouth RTK comparison and a SNR-vs-speed/load map. That is a real but thin difference ("known technique, new setting"). *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and the 80-run matrix is manageable. However, the claim that current is "already exposed" is false. The EFeru FOC SerialFeedback struct, which I read from the repo source, carries cmd, speed, optional wheel counts (only with ENABLE_ODOMETRY), battery voltage and temperature, with no current. It is sent every 10 ms (100 Hz), so streaming current requires firmware changes, and at 100 Hz it is too coarse to resolve a joint crossing that lasts about 10-20 ms. The bigger physics risk is that saw-cut joints are narrow, roughly 3 mm (my estimate). By sagitta w^2/8R, a 38 mm-radius caster dips only about 0.03 mm into a 3 mm gap, so the impulse depends on slab offset or faulting, not on the "6-10 mm" depth the proposal assumes. Recall below 90% is a live possibility (my inference, not measured). *Impact:* This is a 1D along-track result on one or two driveways, it follows a TVT paper that already did joint-pulse map matching, and its main comparison (beating RTK inside a garage, where RTK obviously has no sky) is foreseeable. That points to ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA workshop rather than IROS main track. A clean detectability map (SNR vs speed, load, gap width and offset) would be modestly citable and makes a decent application story. *Main criticism:* The idea of using expansion-joint impulses from a low-cost IMU, map-matched for longitudinal localization, is already published (Road-Pulse, IEEE TVT 2024) and the proposal misses it. The remaining contribution is a scale-down whose headline comparison is a strawman. RTK predictably fails inside a garage, and the experiment omits the $0 competitor that matters: wheel odometry from a known garage start pose, optionally with one printed AprilTag. On a 10-20 m driveway that baseline may already hold about 5 cm along-track (my estimate). If it does, joint matching adds little, and the paper has neither a novelty claim nor a cost claim. *Closest prior work:* [Road-Pulse From IMU to Enhance HD Map Matching for Intelligent Vehicle Localization (Zhou, Hu, Tao, Xiao; IEEE Trans. Vehicular Technology 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/TVT.2023.3318240); [Ground Vehicle Localization using Road Profile Feature (Gim & Ahn, ACC 2020)](https://doi.org/10.23919/ACC45564.2020.9147664); [Terrain-based road vehicle localization using particle filters (Dean, Martini, Brennan; ACC 2008)](https://doi.org/10.1109/acc.2008.4586497); [Terrain Based GPS Independent Lane-Level Vehicle Localization Using Particle Filter and Dead Reckoning (Ahmed, Tahir, Ali; IEEE VTC-Fall 2016)](https://doi.org/10.1109/VTCFall.2016.7881249); [Vehicle localization using its vibration caused by road surface roughness (Tsurushiro & Nagaosa, ICVES 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icves.2015.7396909); [IMU-Based Virtual Road Profile Sensor for Vehicle Localization (Gim & Ahn, Sensors 2018)](https://doi.org/10.3390/s18103344); [Multiclass Terrain Classification using Sound and Vibration from Mobile Robot Terrain Interaction (IROS 2021)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros51168.2021.9636237)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5.5 / F 6 / I 5 = 5.50. *Novelty:* Localizing by matching vehicle IMU and vibration signals against a road-profile map is an established line. The Brennan group alone has at least 6 ACC/CDC papers from 2008-2013, including discrete 'Multi-scale Extrema Features' matching (doi 10.1109/acc.2011.5990976). Gim & Ahn 2018 already pitch a cheap IMU as a GPS-denied substitute, and Pei & Kleeman (AMC 2016) already fuse motor current and an accelerometer on a small robot to sense the floor. What is new is the setting: discrete saw-cut joints at 0.3-0.7 m/s on a small robot, under tow, through a garage. The SNR-vs-speed/load detectability map is a real but thin addition. *Feasibility:* The parts are hobbyist, and the October hand-push SNR pilot is a good early go/no-go. Several items are under-planned, though. (1) The EFeru firmware does NOT stream current: its UART feedback, sent every 10 ms, carries only cmd1/2, speedR/L, wheel counts (only if ENABLE_ODOMETRY is uncommented), batVoltage, boardTemp and cmdLed (checked in Src/main.c and Inc/config.h), so the firmware must be modified, and 100 Hz is probably too slow to resolve a joint impulse lasting tens of ms (my estimate). (2) RTK needs a base-to-rover correction link that is not budgeted. (3) Checkpoint ground truth on a moving robot is unspecified. (4) The full-cart towing condition depends on a working hitch and drive. 80 runs is manageable in about 4 weekends. *Impact:* This is 1D along-track localization over roughly 10-20 m on 2 driveways, against an RTK baseline that is known to fail in garages (the foreseeability that already sank A1.v1 in rounds.md). The missing odometry-only and odometry+threshold baselines would let an IROS reviewer argue that joints add little over 15 m. Main-track IROS acceptance is unlikely. It fits ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA workshop better. The 'feeling the driveway' story is memorable for applications. *Main criticism:* The head-to-head is against the wrong baseline. RTK failing in a garage is foreseeable, and terrain/vibration-map localization is an established ACC/CDC line (Dean/Brennan 2008-2013, Gim & Ahn 2018). The real question on a 10-20 m 1D route is whether joints beat wheel odometry plus a single absolute event, such as the garage threshold bump or a $2 downward IR reflectance sensor that sees the saw cuts. Neither cheap baseline is in the design, so a reviewer cannot tell whether the joint matcher adds anything. The 'IMU+current' sensing also depends on firmware changes the proposal assumes are already done. *Closest prior work:* [Terrain-based road vehicle localization using particle filters (ACC 2008), plus the same group's follow-ups: multi-lane highways (ACC 2009, doi 10.1109/acc.2009.5160078), UKF initialization (ACC 2010, doi 10.1109/acc.2010.5531121), real-time dynamical models (CDC 2012, doi 10.1109/cdc.2012.6426351) and robust map design (CDC 2013, doi 10.1109/cdc.2013.6760311)](https://doi.org/10.1109/acc.2008.4586497); [Pitch based vehicle localization using time series subsequence matching with Multi-scale Extrema Features (Vemulapalli, Dean, Brennan; ACC 2011)](https://doi.org/10.1109/acc.2011.5990976); [IMU-Based Virtual Road Profile Sensor for Vehicle Localization (Gim & Ahn, Sensors 2018)](https://doi.org/10.3390/s18103344); [Vehicle localization using its vibration caused by road surface roughness (ICVES 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icves.2015.7396909); [Measurement and analysis of train motion and railway track characteristics with inertial sensors (Heirich et al., ITSC 2011); also RailSLAM (ICRA 2013, doi 10.1109/icra.2013.6631322)](https://doi.org/10.1109/itsc.2011.6082908); [Dynamic characteristics of the pipeline inspection gauge under girth weld excitation in submarine pipeline (Petroleum Science 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.petsci.2021.09.044); [Mobile robot floor classification using motor current and accelerometer measurements (Pei & Kleeman, AMC 2016)](https://doi.org/10.1109/amc.2016.7496407); [Evaluation of Low-Cost GNSS Receiver under Demanding Conditions in RTK Network Mode (Janos & Kuras, Sensors 2021)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8401267/)

### S5.v1 (round 1, median 5.33): Borrow the expensive sensor once: depth-camera-to-$25-camera distillation for estimating the pose of unmodified trash carts

- **Question:** How many minutes of automatic labels from a $429 active-stereo depth camera does a $25 monocular camera need before its learned estimate of the cart's planar pose (x, y, yaw) matches the depth camera itself on held-out poses, and on which carts, ranges and lighting does the cheap student beat or lose to its teacher?
- **Hypothesis:** With 45 minutes or less of teacher-labelled driving, the student matches the OAK-D Pro to within 2 cm position and 3 deg yaw at 1-3 m, and beats it at 3-5 m and on sunlit faces, because a uniform HDPE cart face is textureless for stereo (and sunlight washes out the IR projector) while the student uses silhouette and lid/wheel keypoints whose geometry is fixed by the ANSI cart standard. Falsified if the student error stays more than 1.5x the teacher's at all ranges even after 90 minutes of labels.
- **Measurements:** Test set: 200 static cart poses on a taped floor/driveway grid with tape-and-protractor truth (x, y in cm; yaw in deg), using 2 carts (64 and 96 gal, a third brand held out), ranges 1-5 m, and 2 lighting conditions (shade, direct sun), with teacher and student evaluated on identical poses (paired). Learning curve: students trained on 5, 15, 45 and 90 minutes of teacher labels. Metrics: position and yaw error (continuous), failure rate (no estimate). Analysis: paired bootstrap CIs on error difference by range bin and lighting; learning-curve fit. Power: 100 poses per lighting condition with sd of about 3 cm gives a paired CI of about +/-0.6 cm.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Luxonis OAK-D Pro, $429 (https://shop.luxonis.com/products/oak-d-pro, verified, active IR stereo), running a standard plane-fit/point-cloud cart pose estimator. It serves both as the teacher and as the on-robot reference. Student: Raspberry Pi Camera Module 3, 'from $25' (https://www.raspberrypi.com/products/camera-module-3/, verified). That is about 17x cheaper per robot, and the teacher can be borrowed or shared across a fleet.
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: the expensive sensor is needed only for the hours spent labelling, not at run time. Because carts follow a standard geometry, a student can exploit shape priors that the teacher's stereo matching cannot, so the student can exceed its teacher exactly where stereo is weak (textureless HDPE, long range, sun). Cross-modal distillation exists for visual odometry (Li et al., RA-L 2022) and ToF/RGB depth (DELTAR), and the low-viewpoint US cart dataset gap is documented in prior_art (StreetView-Waste is truck-view). None measures 'minutes of teacher needed' or student-beats-teacher regions for a docking target. Not foregone: the student may inherit the teacher's errors and never exceed it, and the learning curve may plateau well above the teacher.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Cross-Modal Knowledge Distillation for Depth Privileged Monocular Visual Odometry (RA-L 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2022.3166457); [DELTAR (ECCV 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-19769-7_36); [StreetView-Waste dataset (from prior_art.md)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16440); [Xiao et al., cart docking (from prior_art.md)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648)
- **Build:** Mechanical: sensor mast holding both cameras rigidly with a calibrated extrinsic; a taped pose grid on the driveway; the shared base robot for collecting teacher data while driving around carts. Software: teacher pipeline (OAK-D point cloud, cart-face plane + top-lid fit giving x, y, yaw); auto-labelled dataset; student (small keypoint or direct-regression CNN, trained on Colab/laptop, deployed on a Pi 5); evaluation scripts. Carts stay unmodified (no tags).
- **Cost:** $730: Shared base robot ~$270 (estimates); Pi Camera Module 3 $25 (verified); OAK-D Pro $429 (verified); tape/protractor/mounts ~$10 (estimate). Total ~$734, about $730. Carts: the team's own and neighbours' (borrowed, unmodified).
- **Venue:** IROS 2027 (Mar 1): a learning-based cheap-perception result with a quantified teacher-cost curve fits IROS. CASE backup.
- **Main risk:** The teacher's own labels on textureless carts may be too noisy to train a good student. Fallback: filter teacher labels by depth-confidence and temporal consistency, report the student against tape-measured truth (not just the teacher), and present 'teacher quality limits student' as the measured breakdown point.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 4.5 / F 6.5 / I 5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* The two headline ideas are established. 'Expensive sensor only at training time' is cross-modal or privileged distillation: Self6D (ECCV 2020) trains monocular object pose from unlabelled RGB-D, and MonoDistill (ICLR 2022) distils LiDAR into a monocular detector. 'Student beats teacher at long range' is the classic near-to-far self-supervision result, which Nava et al. (RA-L 2019) already showed on a mobile robot. What is left is a cart-specific application plus a teacher-minutes learning curve, which is a known method in a new setting with a thin extra measurement. *Feasibility:* The parts are off the shelf and the prices are verified: OAK-D Pro US$429 on the Luxonis page, and Pi Camera Module 3 'from $25'. Training a small keypoint or regression CNN on Colab is well-trodden. Three things put the schedule at risk. (1) A robust plane-plus-lid-fit teacher for curved HDPE carts in sun is a research problem in itself, and the stated main risk is that its labels are bad. (2) Training and evaluating four student sizes on 200 hand-measured poses in two lighting conditions before Feb 20 is a lot for part-time students. (3) Tape-and-protractor yaw truth (roughly 1-2 deg resolution, my estimate) is close to the 3 deg hypothesis threshold, which weakens the paired comparison. *Impact:* An IROS reviewer would read this as a known self-supervision or distillation recipe applied to one object class with one teacher, which makes main-track acceptance unlikely. The teacher-minutes curve on unmodified carts is a respectable workshop, ARSO or SoutheastCon contribution and a decent application story. It also gives the team a useful low-viewpoint cart dataset. *Main criticism:* The premise that you have to buy a $429 depth camera to get labels is not the baseline a robotics reviewer would expect. The obvious cheaper teacher is a temporary AprilTag or ArUco marker stuck on the cart only during data collection (about $0, mm/sub-degree-level at close range), or a few hand labels plus synthetic CAD renders of an ANSI-standard cart. Either one removes the 'borrow the expensive sensor' story. Meanwhile, 'student beats teacher at long range or in sun' is the textbook near-to-far self-supervision result (Nava et al., RA-L 2019), so it is foreseeable rather than surprising. The paper would end up showing an established effect with a costlier teacher than necessary. *Closest prior work:* [Learning Long-Range Perception Using Self-Supervision from Short-Range Sensors and Odometry (Nava, Guzzi, Chavez-Garcia, Gambardella, Giusti; IEEE RA-L 2019)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1809.07207); [Self6D: Self-Supervised Monocular 6D Object Pose Estimation (Wang, Manhardt, Shao, Ji, Navab, Tombari; ECCV 2020)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.06468); [MonoDistill: Learning Spatial Features for Monocular 3D Object Detection (Chong et al., ICLR 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2201.10830); [Cross-Modal Knowledge Distillation for Depth Privileged Monocular Visual Odometry (RA-L 2022), cited by the proposal](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2022.3166457); [Xiao et al., cart docking (from prior_art.md)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 4.5 / F 7 / I 5.5 = 5.67. *Novelty:* Prior work already establishes training a camera with a range sensor and then dropping the range sensor at run time, including cases where the student outperforms the teacher at long range (Nava et al. RA-L 2019, arXiv 1809.07207; Self6D for monocular object pose from unlabelled RGB-D, arXiv 2004.06468; MonoDistill, arXiv 2201.10830). Monocular keypoint-PnP pose of carts is also done (Xiao et al., 2110.06648). What is new is only the application (residential carts) plus a 'minutes of teacher' learning curve, which is a thin axis. *Feasibility:* The hardware is minimal and verified: an OAK-D Pro at US$429 (Luxonis shop page, which confirms the IR dot projector and illumination) and a $25 Pi camera. Static test poses and hand-pushed data collection avoid the need for autonomy. The real risks are rigid extrinsic calibration and time synchronization, a small regressor overfitting to only 2 carts from highly correlated video frames (the held-out third brand will likely fail), and tape-and-protractor yaw truth (about 1-2 deg) being close to the 3 deg claim. These are manageable in 12 part-time weeks if the base robot is dropped. *Impact:* A careful learning curve plus paired student-vs-teacher error maps by range and lighting would be a solid student, ARSO or ICRA-workshop paper. IROS main-track reviewers would see a known self-supervision recipe applied to one object class with 2-3 instances, and no closed-loop docking result. For college applications it is a clean, quantitative ML+robotics story. *Main criticism:* The headline claim, that the student beats the teacher at 3-5 m and in sun, has no mechanism in the proposal. If the labels for far or sunlit frames come from the teacher, the student learns the teacher's errors exactly where the teacher is weak. Outperformance is possible only if far-range labels are propagated from near-range teacher measurements via odometry or temporal tracking, which is exactly Nava et al. 2019. The proposal does not specify this, so the result risks being negative or already known. The research question also lacks the obvious zero-teacher baselines: keypoint-PnP using the standard cart geometry with about an hour of hand-clicked labels, and zero-shot monocular metric depth such as UniDepth. Without them, 'minutes of an expensive teacher' is not shown to be the binding cost. *Closest prior work:* [Learning Long-Range Perception Using Self-Supervision from Short-Range Sensors and Odometry (Nava, Guzzi, Chavez-Garcia, Gambardella, Giusti; RA-L 2019)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1809.07207); [Self6D: Self-Supervised Monocular 6D Object Pose Estimation (Wang et al., ECCV 2020)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.06468); [MonoDistill: Learning Spatial Features for Monocular 3D Object Detection (ICLR 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2201.10830); [Xiao et al., airport trolley collection robot (ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [UniDepth: Universal Monocular Metric Depth Estimation (CVPR 2024) / UniDepthV2 (2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.18913)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 4.5 / F 6 / I 5 = 5.17. *Novelty:* The insight that 'the expensive sensor is needed only for labelling, and the camera can beat it at range' is the explicit premise of Nava et al. RA-L 2019 (doi 10.1109/lra.2019.2894849) and of LAGR near-to-far learning (Hadsell et al., JFR 2009, doi 10.1002/rob.20276). Robot-generated pose labels (Deng et al., ICRA 2020) and depth-to-camera distillation (Li RA-L 2022, BEV-LGKD) are also established. What is left is a known recipe applied to a new object (US carts) plus a standard learning curve over minutes of labels, which is thin differentiation. *Feasibility:* The parts are off-the-shelf and verified: OAK-D Pro is $429 for all variants (shop.luxonis.com) and the Pi Camera Module 3 is 'from $25' (raspberrypi.com). Colab training of a keypoint CNN is well-trodden. The hard parts carry real risk on a 12-week, part-time schedule:
- a robust teacher (cart-face plane plus lid fit) on outdoor HDPE;
- synchronized capture and extrinsic calibration;
- 90 minutes of labelled driving across 3 cart brands;
- 200 hand-measured test poses in both shade and direct sun, in Jan-Feb weather.
The 2 cm / 3 deg target is also near the resolution of tape-and-protractor truth: yaw by protractor is about +/-1-2 deg, and the cart's reference point is ambiguous. That could make the headline comparison unmeasurable. *Impact:* IROS reviewers will recognise this as self-supervised near-to-far learning (Nava 2019, Hadsell 2009) applied to one object class, with 200 static poses and no closed-loop docking result, so main-track acceptance is unlikely. It suits a workshop, ARSO or SoutheastCon. The college-application story ('a $25 camera taught by a $429 sensor') is respectable but not distinctive. *Main criticism:* The 'student beats teacher' claim is either already known or unlikely to hold:
- If the 3-5 m training labels come from the OAK-D itself, the student learns that range's noise and bias. Averaging can reduce variance, but it cannot remove systematic bias, so the student cannot reliably beat its teacher there.
- If the labels are instead taken when the robot is close and carried to distant frames by odometry, that is exactly the published near-to-far method (Nava RA-L 2019; Hadsell JFR 2009), so the result is foreseeable.
- A reviewer will also ask why a teacher is needed at all, when synthetic renders of a cart model or zero-shot model-based pose estimators could train the student with no expensive sensor. That alternative baseline is missing.
- The 2 cm / 3 deg monocular target is about 8x better than the published keypoint-PnP trolley result recorded in prior_art.md (0.17 m / 0.11 rad). *Closest prior work:* [Learning Long-Range Perception Using Self-Supervision From Short-Range Sensors and Odometry (Nava, Guzzi, Chavez-Garcia, Gambardella, Giusti; IEEE RA-L 2019)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2019.2894849); [Learning long-range vision for autonomous off-road driving (Hadsell et al., Journal of Field Robotics 2009)](https://doi.org/10.1002/rob.20276); [Self-supervised 6D Object Pose Estimation for Robot Manipulation (Deng, Xiang, Mousavian, Eppner, Bretl, Fox; ICRA 2020)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra40945.2020.9196714); [Cross-Modal Knowledge Distillation for Depth Privileged Monocular Visual Odometry (Li, Wang, Ye, Gong, Xiang; RA-L 2022), cited by the proposal](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2022.3166457); [BEV-LGKD: A Unified LiDAR-Guided Knowledge Distillation Framework for Multi-View BEV 3D Object Detection (IEEE T-IV 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/tiv.2023.3319430); [Trolley-collection robot with keypoint PnP and LiDAR plane fit (recorded in prior_art.md)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551)

### S3.v1 (round 1, median 5.17): Push-to-localize: blind hitching of unmodified trash carts by reading the cart's reaction in motor current and IMU (vs. depth-camera-guided docking)

- **Question:** When the robot makes a deliberate low-force probing push on an unmodified 64/96-gal cart, can the resulting yaw-rate, current and wheel-slip response alone estimate the cart's lateral offset and yaw relative to the hitch accurately enough to complete hitching at the same success rate as depth-camera-guided docking?
- **Hypothesis:** A two-push probing routine estimates lateral offset to within 2 cm and yaw to within 5 deg (RMS) for both empty and full carts, giving hitch success within 5 points of OAK-D Pro-guided docking over a +/-15 cm, +/-20 deg start envelope. Falsified if full carts (high inertia, caster friction) mask the response so that offset RMS exceeds 4 cm, or if success falls more than 10 points below the depth baseline.
- **Measurements:** Initial poses drawn randomly (shared seed list for both methods) from a +/-15 cm x +/-20 deg envelope marked on a floor grid. 2 methods x 2 fill levels (empty, about 40 kg full) x 40 trials = 160 trials. Metrics: offset and yaw estimation error after probing (cm, deg; continuous, tape/protractor truth), final hitch success (binary), time to hitch (s), and peak push force proxy (A). Analysis: paired continuous-error comparison with bootstrap CIs; logistic regression of success on |initial offset| per method to give a 'capture radius' with CI, which avoids underpowered success-rate equivalence claims. Power: 40 trials per cell gives a CI of about +/-0.5 cm on RMS offset error (estimate, sd about 1.5 cm).
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Depth-camera docking with the Luxonis OAK-D Pro ($429, https://shop.luxonis.com/products/oak-d-pro, verified), using a standard plane-fit cart pose estimator and the same hitch and controller. This is the typical sensor for cart pose in docking work (prior_art gap map: indoor docking with lidar/depth). The push method adds $0 of hardware: it reuses the hub-motor current (EFeru firmware) and a $29.50 IMU already on the robot. Coarse approach to within about 30 cm comes from odometry, because the cart's garage parking spot is known.
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: a two-wheeled cart pushed at a point off its axle centreline yaws in a direction and by an amount set by the lever arm, and the robot feels that as a differential current and yaw-rate signature. So pushing the cart turns the cart into its own pose sensor, the way manipulation research localizes objects by pushing. The CASE 2025 work by Hyland et al. estimates a payload's centre of mass by pushing, which shows the signal exists, but it does not use pushing to localize an object for docking or compare it against a depth camera. Not foregone: full carts may simply not rotate measurably under safe forces, and empty carts may skid instead of yaw. Either outcome is informative, and a 'sensorless docking beats a $429 camera on full carts' result would surprise reviewers.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Onboard Sensing and Pushing of Unknown Payload for CoM Estimation with a Holonomic Mobile Robot (Hyland, Xiao, Onal; CASE 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/case58245.2025.11163849); [Xiao et al., autonomous cart docking (from prior_art.md)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [MiR self-aligning pivot patent US12403591B2 (from prior_art.md)](https://patents.google.com/patent/US12403591B2/en); [EFeru hoverboard-firmware-hack-FOC (from prior_art.md)](https://github.com/EFeru/hoverboard-firmware-hack-FOC)
- **Build:** Mechanical: hoverboard base with a front contact plate/bumper (fixed geometry, known contact point) and a passive hitch for the cart's handle or ANSI lift bar (cart unmodified), plus a floor grid in the garage. Software: the probing routine (two short pushes at offset bumper points), a small model (physics-based least squares first, then optional regression on logged data) mapping current/yaw-rate/slip to offset and yaw, and the corrective approach. Baseline: OAK-D Pro point-cloud plane fit to cart face.
- **Cost:** $780: Shared base robot ~$270 (estimates as above); BNO085 $29.50 (verified); hitch/bumper/grid materials ~$50 (estimate); OAK-D Pro $429 (verified, baseline only). Total ~$780. The substitute adds about $0 against a $429 sensor (and against its compute load).
- **Venue:** IEEE CASE 2027 (Mar 1): an automation-oriented sensing-by-contact method with measured capture envelopes and cost. IROS is an alternative if the physics model is strong.
- **Main risk:** A full cart's inertia and caster/wheel friction may make the response too small or ambiguous, or the push may shove the cart off its spot. Fallback: limit the method to the final alignment stage (within +/-5 cm after a coarse odometry approach) and report the fill-dependent observability limit, which is still a clean, falsifiable result.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 5.5 / I 5 = 5.17. *Novelty:* The sensing principle is old. Yoshikawa & Kurisu (IROS 1991, doi 10.1109/iros.1991.174510) identified an object's friction centre by mobile-robot pushing, and Hyland et al. (CASE 2025) search for the zero-rotation line of action with a force sensor and camera. Touch-based pose estimation is mature (Petrovskaya T-RO 2011; Koval IJRR 2015). What remains new is applying proprioception-only (hoverboard current plus IMU) push probing to pre-hitch pose estimation of an unmodified refuse cart, with a head-to-head comparison. That counts as a known technique in a new setting with modest new measurements. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and the 160-trial matrix fits the time. The physics risk is serious, though. An upright 64/96-gal cart rests on two wheels and molded front feet, not casters (my inference from cart geometry), so its yaw response to a push is Coulomb stick-slip. The probe also moves the very pose it is trying to measure. Hitting 2 cm and 5 deg from noisy low-speed EFeru phase-current readings is ambitious. The Oct-Nov phone-IMU pilot is a sensible go/no-go, but the probable outcome is the fallback (an observability limit). *Impact:* A clean sensing-by-contact result with capture envelopes could reach a CASE submission or an ICRA workshop. But the 'same capability, cheaper' claim rests on a strawman baseline. Within about 30 cm, a passive self-aligning hitch with contact switches (MiR US12403591B2) or a cheap ToF/2D-lidar sensor already does the job without a $429 depth camera, and a CASE reviewer will ask for exactly those comparisons. The likely 'full carts don't rotate measurably' outcome is a modest negative result at workshop or student-conference level. *Main criticism:* The method's 'insight', that an off-axis push yaws the object and reveals its geometry, is the 1991 Yoshikawa-Kurisu and 2025 Hyland push-identification principle. The capability being made cheaper, closing the final ~30 cm of alignment, is already solved for about $0 by passive self-aligning hitches with contact switches (MiR) or cheap ToF sensing. Benchmarking only against a $429 depth camera is therefore a strawman. Separately, probing an unmodified cart that rests on high-friction front feet perturbs the pose being estimated and gives a stick-slip, mass-dependent signal. The most likely result is 'unobservable for full carts', which is weak as a main contribution. *Closest prior work:* [Onboard Sensing and Pushing of Unknown Payload for CoM Estimation with a Holonomic Mobile Robot (Hyland, Xiao, Onal; CASE 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/case58245.2025.11163849); [Identification of the center of friction from pushing an object by a mobile robot (Yoshikawa & Kurisu, IROS 1991)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.1991.174510); [On-line parameter identification of a cart by mobile manipulation pushing (Sun, Xi, Tan; Robotics and Autonomous Systems, 2003/2004)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.robot.2003.10.002); [Global Localization of Objects via Touch (Petrovskaya & Khatib, IEEE T-RO 2011)](https://doi.org/10.1109/tro.2011.2138450); [Pose estimation for planar contact manipulation with manifold particle filters (Koval, Pollard, Srinivasa; IJRR 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1177/0278364915571007); [Interactive Perception: Leveraging Action in Perception and Perception in Action (Bohg et al., IEEE T-RO 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/tro.2017.2721939); [MiR self-aligning gripper with pivot plus two contact switches (US12403591B2)](https://patents.google.com/patent/US12403591B2/en); [Yaw Rate Based Trailer Hitch Angle Estimation for Trailer Backup Assist (SAE 2017)](https://doi.org/10.4271/2017-01-0027)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 5.5 / I 5 = 5.17. *Novelty:* Using a push to infer object pose is a decades-old manipulation idea: 'Pose from pushing' (ICRA 1996), Yu/Leonard/Rodriguez (IROS 2015) and Koval et al. (IJRR 2015). Sun/Xi/Tan (RAS 2004) already identified cart parameters on-line by pushing with a mobile robot. The new part is applying it with wheel current and an IMU to hitch an unmodified refuse cart and benchmarking it against a depth camera. That is a known technique in a new setting with a narrow differentiation. The proposal also misframes Hyland 2025 as the only precedent. *Feasibility:* Parts are cheap and verified (OAK-D Pro is $429 with an IR projector on shop.luxonis.com), the 160-trial matrix is manageable, and the Oct-Nov hand-push go/no-go is a good de-risk. But the main physics risk is high. An upright cart rests on its wheels and front feet, so its yaw under a push is set by stick-slip friction whose distribution changes with the contents, and 2 cm / 5 deg from two pushes is optimistic. Separately, the EFeru firmware's default SerialFeedback struct (Src/main.c) sends only commands, speeds, voltage and temperature. Current is computed internally as DC-link current (left_dc_curr/right_dc_curr) and must be added to the UART packet via a firmware change, and it is not per-phase. Tape-and-protractor ground truth (~1 cm, ~2 deg) is too coarse for the claimed accuracy. *Impact:* A clean positive result (sensorless docking matches a depth camera on full carts) would be a nice CASE story, but the likely outcome is a fill-dependent observability limit or a hybrid. With a single cart type and a straw-man camera baseline that is ARSO, SoutheastCon or workshop level. The application story ('the robot feels where the bin is') is good for college applications. *Main criticism:* The 'cart as its own pose sensor' insight is the established pose-from-pushing and interactive-perception literature (Jia and Erdmann 1996, Yu et al. 2015, Koval et al. 2015, and Sun et al. 2004 on cart pushing by a mobile robot), which the proposal does not cite. The baseline is also chosen to flatter the method. In the final 15 cm the cheap competitor that industry actually uses is contact switches plus a passive self-aligning funnel or pivot (MiR US12403591B2), or a $20 ToF array, not a $429 depth camera. Beating a camera that is overkill for a big flat cart face at known range says little. Meanwhile, the friction-dominated yaw response of a full upright cart makes the 2 cm / 5 deg target unlikely. *Closest prior work:* [Pose from pushing (Y.-B. Jia, M. Erdmann, ICRA 1996)](https://doi.org/10.1109/robot.1996.503590); [Shape and pose recovery from planar pushing (K.-T. Yu, J. Leonard, A. Rodriguez, IROS 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.2015.7353523); [Pose estimation for planar contact manipulation with manifold particle filters (Koval et al., IJRR 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1177/0278364915571007); [On-line parameter identification of a cart by mobile manipulation pushing (Sun, Xi, Tan, Robotics and Autonomous Systems 2004)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.robot.2003.10.002); [Cart pushing with a mobile manipulation system: Towards navigation with moveable objects (Scholz, Chitta, Marthi, Likhachev, ICRA 2011)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2011.5980288); [Onboard Sensing and Pushing of Unknown Payload for CoM Estimation with a Holonomic Mobile Robot (Hyland, Xiao, Onal, CASE 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/case58245.2025.11163849); [Control of Cart-Like Nonholonomic Systems Using a Mobile Manipulator (Aguilera, Hutchinson, IROS 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros55552.2023.10342088); [MiR self-aligning gripper patent US12403591B2 (from prior_art.md)](https://patents.google.com/patent/US12403591B2/en)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5 / F 5.5 / I 5 = 5.17. *Novelty:* Pushing a wheeled cart with a mobile robot to estimate its non-holonomic parameters is already published (Scholz et al., ICRA 2015, doi 10.1109/icra.2015.7139972). Localizing objects by pushing is a mature manipulation topic (Yu et al. IROS 2015; Sipos & Fazeli IROS 2022), and Hyland et al. (CASE 2025) push payloads with a mobile base to estimate their properties. What remains new is applying this to hitching an unmodified refuse cart, using only hub-motor current and an IMU, and comparing head-to-head against a depth camera. That is a known technique in a new setting, not a new idea. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and verified: the OAK-D Pro is $429 (shop.luxonis.com, re-checked). The 160-trial matrix is about 10-15 hours of tape-and-protractor work, which fits. The risks are stacked, though. (1) An upright, untilted cart rests on its front lip/feet as well as its wheels, so its push response is governed by unknown floor friction and the support-pressure distribution, which changes with fill. That makes lateral offset confounded with the centre of friction, and the 2 cm target looks optimistic (my inference). (2) Hoverboard FOC phase-current resolution at low speed is unverified for detecting small differential torques. (3) A flat bumper on the flat cart face will passively square up the cart (classic sensorless push-alignment), so the yaw signal may disappear by construction. The two-week pilot with a phone IMU is a good go/no-go and makes the project survivable. *Impact:* A clean, falsifiable capture-radius study with bootstrap CIs is respectable for ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA workshop. At CASE/IROS main track, however, reviewers who know Scholz 2015 and Hyland 2025 would see an application paper. The baseline is also a strawman: a $429 depth camera rather than the cheap alternatives that compete here (two $10-20 ToF sensors, bumper switches, or a MiR-style passive pivot). That weakens the 'same capability, much cheaper' claim. *Main criticism:* The central insight (a pushed wheeled cart reveals its pose and kinematics through the reaction it produces) is already published for carts with a mobile manipulator (Scholz et al., ICRA 2015). The physics also works against the method. An untilted cart is friction-supported on its lip/feet as well as its wheels, so its response depends on fill-dependent friction, not on the lever arm alone. A flat bumper will tend to passively align the cart's yaw, which removes the very signal the method needs. As framed, the result is either 'it doesn't work on full carts' or a re-application of known interactive perception, benchmarked against an expensive camera that nobody would use for the last 30 cm. *Closest prior work:* [Learning Non-holonomic Object Models for Mobile Manipulation (Scholz, Levihn, Isbell, Christensen, Stilman; ICRA 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2015.7139972); [Shape and pose recovery from planar pushing (Yu et al., IROS 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.2015.7353523); [Simultaneous Contact Location and Object Pose Estimation Using Proprioception and Tactile Feedback (Sipos, Fazeli; IROS 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros47612.2022.9981762); [Onboard Sensing and Pushing of Unknown Payload for CoM Estimation with a Holonomic Mobile Robot (Hyland, Xiao, Onal; CASE 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/case58245.2025.11163849); [MiR self-aligning gripper patent US12403591B2](https://patents.google.com/patent/US12403591B2/en); [Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection (Xiao et al., ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648)

### P3.v1 (round 1, median 5.17): Stop before contact: a ~$60 60 GHz radar vs. camera-AI and bump-sensing robot mowers on hidden, hedgehog-size dummies

- **Question:** Can a ~$60 short-range mm-wave radar on a slow lawn robot detect a hedgehog-size, tissue-like dummy hidden in 4-10 cm grass, and stop before contact more often than a camera-AI wire-free mower (WORX Landroid Vision) and a conventional bump-sensing mower, by day and by night?
- **Hypothesis:** The radar robot will stop before contact in ≥90% of approaches, with a Wilson 95% lower bound ≥80% at n=60 per cell, in both light conditions. The camera mower will stop before contact in ≤50% of night or tall-grass approaches, and the bump mower in ~0% (consistent with Rasmussen et al.). The radar's false-stop rate will be ≤1 per 100 m on clear dry lawn. The hypothesis is falsified if dew- or sprinkler-wet grass clutter forces a threshold that misses the dummy, or if the camera mower matches the radar.
- **Measurements:** Radar robot: 2 grass heights (4, 10 cm) x 2 light levels (day, <5 lux) = 4 cells x 60 scripted approaches at 0.3 m/s = 240. Metrics: stop-before-contact (a soft bumper switch defines contact) and stop distance in cm (continuous). Products: the dummy is placed in the mower's straight-line lane, 30 encounters per cell, filmed with an IR-lit camera; metrics are touch / no-touch and push distance in cm. False alarms: 500 m of clear-lawn driving per moisture state (dry, dew/sprinkler-wet). Object control: a dry plastic toy of the same size, to test whether radar responds to water-rich targets or to any object. Analysis: Wilson CIs per cell, logistic regression on grass height, light and system, CDF of detection range, false stops per 100 m with Poisson CIs. No animals are used (gel dummies only) and no human subjects, so no IRB is needed.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** (1) Published benchmark: Rasmussen et al. 2023 tested 19 robotic mower models against hedgehog cadavers. 'Apart from one single incidence, all robotic lawn mowers had to physically touch' the hedgehogs to detect them (abstract, verified via Europe PMC). (2) Commercial camera-AI mower: WORX Landroid Vision, which advertises camera and lighting to 'detect and avoid nocturnal wildlife' (au.worx.com, fetched); AU$1,499-1,999, US price UNVERIFIED; borrowed or used. (3) A borrowed conventional wired or bump-sensing mower. Within-robot control: a Pi camera with a pretrained detector on the same rover.
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: water-rich bodies (tissue, gel) are strong mm-wave reflectors, while individual thin grass blades scatter weakly. Range-gating a downward-angled radar separates a near-ground target from the lawn behind it, so a cheap radar can 'see' an animal through grass and darkness, where cameras lose it to occlusion and low light. Why it is not foregone: dew-wet grass is also water-laden and may return clutter as strong as the dummy; low grazing angles cause ground multipath; and a hedgehog-size target has small radar cross-section. The radar could fail outright on wet mornings, or the AI camera product could do better than its peers in Rasmussen's test. A reviewer would be surprised if a $60 sensor cleared the bar that 18 of 19 commercial mowers failed. Searches: Crossref shows mower animal-safety testing (Rasmussen) and vision-based mower obstacle recognition, but no radar-based contactless small-animal detection for mowers. Patents and Scholar were not checked this session and must be before committing.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Testing the Impact of Robotic Lawn Mowers on European Hedgehogs and Designing a Safety Test (Rasmussen et al., Animals 2023)](https://doi.org/10.3390/ani14010122); [Obstacle Recognition System For Lawn Mower Based On Machine Vision (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/aihcir67580.2025.11405072); [WORX Landroid Vision product page (camera-based wildlife avoidance claim)](https://au.worx.com/landroid/landroid-vision/)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: a rover with the radar at 15-20 cm height tilted 20-30 degrees down; a soft foam bumper with a limit switch to define 'contact'; gel or water-filled dummies in 2 sizes (sized from Rasmussen's paper once read; dimensions UNVERIFIED); grass-height test plots; a camera rig with an IR lamp for night filming. Software student: radar SDK integration; range-gated detection with a clutter map/CFAR learned on clear lawn; stop controller; a camera-detector baseline on a Pi; event logging and video annotation.
- **Cost:** $1300: 60 GHz radar module or eval board (Acconeer A121-class, TI or Infineon kits) ~$50-80 (UNVERIFIED; check Digi-Key in October). Rover ~$260 (hoverboard $139 verified + ESP32 $19.95 verified + frame ~$100 estimate). Raspberry Pi 5 4GB ~$110 + Pi Camera Module 3 $25 (verified in A2). Ballistic gel and molds ~$60 (estimate). IR lamp and night camera ~$40 (estimate). Bumper and switches ~$15 (estimate). Subtotal ~$600. Products: $0 if borrowed; a used Landroid Vision is an estimated ~$600-900 (UNVERIFIED). FLAG: about $1,200-1,500 if bought. Cost ratio: ~$60 radar vs. camera + AI compute (~$274, verified parts) is about 4.5x, and vs. the ~$1,000+ product about 15x. The headline, though, is capability (contactless detection), not only price.
- **Venue:** IROS 2027 (Mar 1). The 'Sustainable Life' theme plus wildlife safety gives the paper a clear impact story. Backup: CASE 2027.
- **Main risk:** Wet-grass clutter may swamp the dummy return, and the radar SDK may eat build time. Fallback: restrict the claim to dry-lawn operation and report the moisture-dependent clutter characterization as a result. If the product loan fails, use Rasmussen's published benchmark plus the within-robot camera baseline.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 4.5 / F 5.5 / I 5.5 = 5.17. *Novelty:* The proposal's core insight is already published. Patrovsky & Biebl (2005, doi.org/10.5194/ars-3-211-2005) used a low-cost 24 GHz module to detect water-rich animals through covering pasture, and they reported dew-induced false alarms. Rasmussen et al. 2023 also already tested a radar-equipped commercial mower (Husqvarna Automower Nera). What is left is a modern 60 GHz range-gated sensor on a robot, compared head-to-head with a camera-AI mower on hedgehog dummies at night. That is a known technique in a new setting with a thin margin of difference. *Feasibility:* Acconeer-class 60 GHz radar SDKs (UNVERIFIED pricing) and a hoverboard rover are within reach. But the plan has several failure points. The experiments fall in Dec-Feb, when lawns in most of the US are dormant, frosty or snow-covered, so tests at 4 and 10 cm grass height and wet-vs-dry comparisons at night may simply be impossible. A Landroid Vision costing $1,000 or more has to be borrowed. The gel dummies need dielectric validation. And the plan calls for 480 approaches plus 1 km of false-alarm driving in about 6 weeks. *Impact:* Wildlife safety has a good story and fits ARSO, an ICRA/IROS workshop, or perhaps a sensors or agriculture venue. But IROS/CASE reviewers will find the 2005 radar-fawn work and the radar-equipped Nera. The result then reduces to "a newer radar also works, on dummies, versus one product". That is unlikely to make a main track without a model of the clutter vs. target RCS or a standardized-test contribution. *Main criticism:* The idea that cheap microwave radar can see water-rich animals hidden in grass, and that dew is the problem, was already published in 2005 (Patrovsky & Biebl, low-cost 24 GHz module, fawns covered by pasture). Radar-equipped commercial mowers such as the Husqvarna Automower Nera were already in Rasmussen's 19-model test. So the 'surprising $60 sensor' framing is not new. On top of that, the Dec-Feb test window probably rules out realistic lawn, grass-height and dew conditions. *Closest prior work:* [Microwave sensors for detection of wild animals during pasture mowing (Patrovsky & Biebl, Advances in Radio Science 3, 2005)](https://doi.org/10.5194/ars-3-211-2005); [Testing the Impact of Robotic Lawn Mowers on European Hedgehogs and Designing a Safety Test (Rasmussen et al., Animals 14(1):122, published 29 Dec 2023)](https://doi.org/10.3390/ani14010122); [Facing Danger: Exploring Personality and Reactions of European Hedgehogs towards Robotic Lawn Mowers (Animals 2023)](https://doi.org/10.3390/ani14010002); [Automatic Detection of Animals in Mowing Operations Using Thermal Cameras (Sensors 2012)](https://doi.org/10.3390/s120607587); [An active UHF RFID transponder for fawn saving during pasture mowing (GeMiC 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/gemic.2015.7107814); [Determine 24 GHz and 77 GHz Radar Characteristics of Surrogate Grass (SAE 2019-01-1012)](https://doi.org/10.4271/2019-01-1012); [Obstacle Recognition System For Lawn Mower Based On Machine Vision (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/aihcir67580.2025.11405072)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 5 / I 5.5 = 5.17. *Novelty:* The proposal's key insight was published 21 years ago. Patrovsky and Biebl (2005) used a low-cost 24 GHz radar module to detect fawn-size animals covered by grass, using the water-content contrast, and already reported dew false alarms (https://ars.copernicus.org/articles/3/211/2005/). What remains new is the smaller hedgehog-size target, range-gated 60 GHz radar on a domestic robot mower, and a quantified head-to-head against a shipping camera-AI mower. That is a known technique in a new setting with useful new measurements. The academic gap for mowers specifically does look real: Crossref and Europe PMC searches found no radar or camera hedgehog-detection engineering paper. *Feasibility:* The radar side is tractable (Acconeer-class modules ship an SDK with range-gated presence and distance examples). The main problem is the calendar. The test matrix needs live grass at 4 and 10 cm, dew-wet vs dry states and 500 m drives per moisture state, all run Dec–Feb. In most of the northern hemisphere lawns are then dormant, frosted or snow-covered, and robot mowers are put away for winter (team location unknown). On top of that, 240 product encounters with a randomly-pathing borrowed Landroid Vision, plus a second borrowed mower, adds heavy logistics and loan risk, and buying the products pushes the cost to about $1,200–1,500, over the ~$1,000 flag. *Impact:* A wildlife-safety benchmark of a commercial AI mower vs a $60 radar is a good story and fits ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA/IROS workshop. But an IROS/CASE reviewer who finds Patrovsky 2005 will see the sensing principle as known. One gel dummy type on one lawn, over one season, is a thin main-track contribution. For college applications it is strong (conservation angle, measured head-to-head against a product). *Main criticism:* The central idea is already published. A cheap radar sees water-rich animals through grass, with dew as the failure mode: Patrovsky and Biebl, Adv. Radio Sci. 2005, using a low-cost 24 GHz module on fawns. The data collection is also scheduled for Dec–Feb, when real lawns at 4/10 cm heights and robot mowers are mostly unavailable outdoors, so the core contribution is both less novel and harder to execute than the proposal claims. *Closest prior work:* [Microwave sensors for detection of wild animals during pasture mowing (Patrovsky and Biebl, Advances in Radio Science 3, 2005)](https://ars.copernicus.org/articles/3/211/2005/); [Testing the Impact of Robotic Lawn Mowers on European Hedgehogs and Designing a Safety Test (Rasmussen et al., Animals 2023/24)](https://doi.org/10.3390/ani14010122); [FieldSAFE: Dataset for Obstacle Detection in Agriculture (Kragh et al., Sensors 2017)](https://doi.org/10.3390/s17112579); [An active UHF RFID transponder for fawn saving during pasture mowing (Lehner, GeMiC 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/gemic.2015.7107814); [WORX Landroid Vision product page](https://au.worx.com/landroid/landroid-vision/)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 4.5 / F 5 / I 5.5 = 5.00. *Novelty:* Patrovsky & Biebl (2005) already used a low-cost 24 GHz radar to detect animals hidden under pasture grass by water-content RCS contrast, and already found dew-induced false alarms. Both the proposal's 'insight' and its 'why it is not foregone' risk are therefore published. What is left is an incremental update (60 GHz FMCW range-gating, hedgehog scale, a garden robot) plus a new benchmark of a camera-AI mower, which Rasmussen did not test because none of their 19 mowers had cameras. The proposal's statement that no radar-based contactless animal detection for mowing exists is false. *Feasibility:* Three large risks. (1) Season: the field matrix (4 and 10 cm growing grass, dew vs dry, 500 m of driving per moisture state, night runs) is scheduled for Dec-Feb, when lawns in most of the US are dormant, frosted or snow-covered, so 'dew-wet' becomes frost. Unless the team is in the Deep South, they need indoor sod trays, which weakens the realism claim. (2) Access: borrowing a Landroid Vision plus a bump mower and running 240 filmed product encounters partly at <5 lux depends on outside parties. (3) Validity: a spineless gel dummy may not match a real hedgehog's radar return, since the dry keratin spines cover the body, and no animal or cadaver validation is planned. Radar SDK work is modest (Acconeer XM125/XE125 are sold via Digi-Key/Mouser), but no price is shown on the vendor page, so the $50-80 figure is unconfirmed. *Impact:* A good wildlife-safety story with a clean dummy protocol borrowed from Rasmussen, and the first quantitative test of a camera-AI mower's wildlife claim would interest the hedgehog and ecology community (Animals/MDPI is a natural home). For IROS/CASE main track, though, it reads as a sensor characterization with a known physical principle (published in 2005) and a product benchmark, with no new algorithm or mechanism. ARSO, a workshop or SoutheastCon is the realistic level. It is a strong college-application story. *Main criticism:* The core physical insight (water-rich animal bodies vs weakly scattering grass, with dew as the failure mode) and the application (cheap microwave radar to find animals hidden in grass before mowing) were published in 2005. This turns the proposal into an incremental 60 GHz update plus a product benchmark. The Dec-Feb schedule also makes real growing-grass, dew and night field trials impractical in most of the US. *Closest prior work:* [Microwave sensors for detection of wild animals during pasture mowing (Patrovsky & Biebl, Advances in Radio Science 3, 2005)](https://ars.copernicus.org/articles/3/211/2005/); [Testing the Impact of Robotic Lawn Mowers on European Hedgehogs and Designing a Safety Test (Rasmussen et al., Animals 2023, PMC10777904)](https://doi.org/10.3390/ani14010122); [An active UHF RFID transponder for fawn saving during pasture mowing (German Microwave Conference 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/gemic.2015.7107814); [Boundary Recognition System for Intelligent Lawn Mowing Robot Based on mmWave Radar (IEEE PRAI 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/prai67447.2025.11412945); [WORX Landroid Vision product page](https://au.worx.com/landroid/landroid-vision/)

### S4.v1 (round 1, median 5.00): The used phone is the whole robot sensor suite: dual-frequency phone GNSS + on-device VIO vs. ZED-F9P RTK + 2D lidar on a garage-to-curb route

- **Question:** On a ground robot driving garage-to-curb, does a single used Android phone (on-device visual-inertial odometry anchored by its own dual-frequency GNSS) localize as accurately as a ZED-F9P RTK + LD19 lidar + odometry stack costing about 5-7x more, and where along the route does each fail?
- **Hypothesis:** The phone stack holds checkpoint error within 15 cm (95th percentile) along the whole route in daylight, beating RTK in the garage interior and mouth while losing to it by less than 10 cm at the open curb. Ground-robot motion (planar, near-constant velocity) is predicted to cause measurable VIO scale drift. If phone error at the curb exceeds 30 cm, or if VIO scale error exceeds 5% on constant-speed segments, the hypothesis fails.
- **Measurements:** Both stacks are mounted and logged on every run (paired). Primary metric: horizontal error (cm) at 6 tape-surveyed checkpoints; secondary metrics: VIO scale error (%) on 10 m constant-speed segments, tracking-loss events, and time with a valid estimate (%). Conditions: daylight vs night with only the garage light on (2) x 25 runs = 50 runs, plus 10 closed-loop runs per stack driving to the curb mark (final stop error, cm). Analysis: mixed model of error by stack x route zone x lighting; paired bootstrap CIs. Power: 25 paired runs with sd of about 8 cm gives a CI of about +/-3 cm per lighting condition.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Reference stack on the same robot: ZED-F9P RTK pair (2x ArduSimple kit, $527.50, from prior_art.md: https://www.ardusimple.com/product/simplertk2b-basic-starter-kit-ip65/), an LD19 2D lidar ($95.99-$99, https://www.waveshare.com/product/dtof-lidar-ld19.htm, from prior_art.md) and a Pi 5. That is about $700-$900 of localization hardware, against a used dual-frequency phone at roughly $100-$150 (estimate, UNVERIFIED) that also replaces the compute and camera.
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: the phone's VIO keeps metric scale only when the accelerometer is excited (VINS on Wheels, ICRA 2017, shows that ground-vehicle motion makes VIO scale unobservable). On a robot, the phone's own GNSS (noisy outdoors and absent in the garage) and the robot's periodic stops-and-turns at the garage mouth can supply exactly the excitation and anchoring that a handheld user would. Whether a stock phone holds scale on a ground robot is open. A 2026 preprint (Portugal & Venghaus) uses device-native VIO for robot displacement logging but adds axis constraints and does not compare against RTK and lidar on a GNSS-transition route. OpenBot uses the phone for compute and camera, not as the localization reference replacement. Not foregone: smartphone GNSS has known antenna and cycle-slip limits (Yun et al. 2022), and VIO may drift badly at night.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [VINS on Wheels (Wu, Guo, Georgiou, Roumeliotis; ICRA 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2017.7989603); [Constrained-Axis Visual-Inertial Odometry for Mobile Robot Displacement Logging Using Commodity Mobile Devices (Portugal & Venghaus, 2026 preprint)](https://doi.org/10.21203/rs.3.rs-10175959/v1); [Inherent Limitations of Smartphone GNSS Positioning ... Dual-Frequency Measurements (Yun, Lim, Park; Sensors 2022)](https://doi.org/10.3390/s22249879); [OpenBot (ICRA 2021, from prior_art.md)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2008.10631)
- **Build:** Mechanical: base robot with a vibration-isolated phone mount at the top of the mast (sky view plus a forward camera), RTK antenna and LD19 on the same mast. Software: Android app logging ARCore pose + raw GNSS (GnssLogger-style) and commanding the base over USB-serial/BLE (OpenBot-style); a laptop-side fusion of VIO with phone GNSS (and optionally RTKLIB processing of phone raw data); a reference stack of RTK + lidar scan-matching + odometry in ROS2.
- **Cost:** $1020: Shared base robot ~$270 (estimates); used dual-frequency Android phone ~$120 (estimate, UNVERIFIED; must confirm ARCore and dual-frequency support for the model); 2x ArduSimple RTK kits $527.50 (verified in prior_art); LD19 ~$99 (prior_art). Total ~$1,016. FLAG: about $1,000. Using a public NTRIP caster instead of a second RTK kit (UNVERIFIED availability) brings it to ~$750.
- **Venue:** IEEE CASE 2027 (Mar 1), as a cost-effectiveness automation study with an on-robot reference. IROS is possible if the scale-observability finding is strong.
- **Main risk:** Android GNSS duty cycling, thermal throttling or ARCore tracking loss in a dark garage may make the phone stack fail outright, and the build sits near the $1,000 limit. Fallback: report the failure map by zone and lighting (still the headline 'where it breaks' result), and drop the second RTK kit in favour of NTRIP or the LD19 to stay under budget.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 5 / I 5 = 5.00. *Novelty:* The two halves have each been studied. Phone GNSS inside a VIO factor graph has been compared against a ZED-F9P (Ocal et al. 2025). ARCore drift has been measured against optical ground truth at 14.4 cm/m (Feigl et al. 2020). ARCore is already a robot's pose source (OpenBot PointGoal; Portugal & Venghaus 2026, which also falls back to GPS). The new part is a paired on-robot comparison along a garage-to-curb route, which applies known techniques to a new setting. Part of the headline ('beats RTK inside the garage') was already judged foreseeable in rounds.md. *Feasibility:* This is two full localization systems built in about 12 part-time weeks: an Android app logging ARCore and raw GNSS while driving the base, plus custom VIO-GNSS fusion, plus a ROS2 RTK + lidar scan-matching + odometry stack. Ground truth for a moving robot at 6 checkpoints on each of 70 runs is extra labor. ARCore tracking loss in a dark garage and phone thermal throttling are real risks. The BOM leaves out the Pi 5 that the baseline lists ($305 for 16GB per prior_art.md), so the true total exceeds about $1,100, and the LD19 is marked discontinued at Waveshare. *Impact:* ARCore is a black box, so the paper cannot actually study scale observability. It ends up as a single-site benchmark of a consumer API, with a likely result of 'phone drifts by decimetres and its GNSS cannot correct below about 0.5-1.75 m; RTK fails in the garage'. That is workshop or SoutheastCon/ARSO level, not CASE/IROS main track. The 'used phone replaces the sensor suite' story still makes a good application narrative. *Main criticism:* The hypothesis contradicts published numbers, so the likely result is a foreseeable failure, not a surprising equivalence. Phone GNSS is 0.5 m (Pixel 5, open sky) to 1.75 m (best L5 DGNSS, Yun 2022) horizontally, so it cannot anchor VIO to 15 cm at the curb. ARCore's measured scale error reaches 14.4 cm/m (Feigl 2020), well beyond the 5% falsifier. The phone stack therefore reduces to 'ARCore dead-reckoning from a known garage start', and the RTK-in-garage comparison repeats the 'RTK fails in a garage' result that earlier reviewers already called foreseeable. Separately, the proposal's own novelty claims about OpenBot and Portugal & Venghaus are inaccurate. And because ARCore is a black box, the claimed VINS-on-Wheels scale-observability 'insight' cannot be tested, only observed. *Closest prior work:* [Assessment of Smartphone GNSS Measurements in Tightly Coupled Visual Inertial Navigation (Ocal, Durmaz, Tunali, Yildiz; Applied Sciences 2025)](https://doi.org/10.3390/app152312796); [Localization Limitations of ARCore, ARKit, and HoloLens in Dynamic Large-scale Industry Environments (Feigl et al., VISIGRAPP 2020)](https://doi.org/10.5220/0008989903070318); [OpenBot robot app, PointGoal navigation (isl-org/OpenBot README)](https://github.com/isl-org/OpenBot/blob/master/android/robot/README.md); [Constrained-Axis Visual-Inertial Odometry for Mobile Robot Displacement Logging Using Commodity Mobile Devices (Portugal & Venghaus, 2026 preprint)](https://doi.org/10.21203/rs.3.rs-10175959/v1); [GVI-Switch: A High-Precision GNSS-Visual-Inertial State Estimator for Indoor-Outdoor Environments (RA-L 2026)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2026.3678462); [GVIR: A GNSS-Visual-Inertial-Range Sensor Fusion Approach for Seamless Indoor-Outdoor Robot Localization (I2MTC 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/i2mtc62753.2025.11079147); [Demonstration of Fused RTK (Fixed) + Inertial Positioning Using Android Smartphone Sensors Only (Bochkati et al., IEEE/ION PLANS 2020)](https://doi.org/10.1109/plans46316.2020.9109865); [Inherent Limitations of Smartphone GNSS Positioning ... Dual-Frequency (Yun, Lim, Park; Sensors 2022)](https://doi.org/10.3390/s22249879)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 4.5 / F 5.5 / I 5 = 5.00. *Novelty:* Each piece is already published. Phone VIO as a robot odometry module, with GPS switch-over and indoor/outdoor validation, is in Portugal & Venghaus 2026 (doi 10.21203/rs.3.rs-10175959/v1). Phone GNSS inside VIO fusion performing 'comparable to' a ZED-F9P is in Ocal et al. 2025 (doi 10.3390/app152312796). What remains is a head-to-head on one short route. Its headline outcomes (RTK has no fix in the garage, ARCore degrades in the dark) are the kind of foreseeable comparison the 25-reviewer round already rejected. *Feasibility:* The team must build two full localization stacks: an Android ARCore + raw-GNSS logging and control app, and a ROS2 RTK + LD19 scan-matching + odometry reference. They must also run 70 paired runs in about 5 weeks during school. ARCore tracking loss on low-texture, forward-only ground motion in a dark garage is a real risk. The parts list is fragile: the LD19 is marked discontinued at Waveshare (prior_art.md), and the used phone must support dual-frequency raw GNSS and ARCore (model and price UNVERIFIED). The build also sits at the $1,000 cap. Ground truth from 6 tape checkpoints is workable but coarse. *Impact:* This is a single-route, single-device benchmark of a commercial black box (ARCore), with no new method. It reads as a student-conference, workshop or ARSO-level result, not CASE/IROS main track. CASE reviewers would also likely see the 'phone GNSS anchor' as decorative, given the documented ~0.5 m phone GNSS error. The 'old phone replaces a $900 sensor stack' story works well for college applications. *Main criticism:* The research question collapses into a foreseeable benchmark. Phone SPP GNSS is about 0.5 m even in open sky (Tomastik & Everett 2023), so it cannot 'anchor' anything at the 15 cm level. The phone result is therefore just ARCore dead-reckoning from a known start over a 15-20 m route. The rest of the story (RTK loses fix in the garage, VIO struggles at night, the phone's GNSS fused into VIO roughly matches an F9P) is either expected or already reported (Ocal et al. 2025; Portugal & Venghaus 2026). Industry already ships fused camera+RTK localization in $800-1,000 consumer mowers. That leaves no defensible new contribution for CASE/IROS. *Closest prior work:* [Assessment of Smartphone GNSS Measurements in Tightly Coupled Visual Inertial Navigation (Ocal, Durmaz, Tunali, Yildiz; Applied Sciences 2025)](https://doi.org/10.3390/app152312796); [Constrained-Axis Visual-Inertial Odometry for Mobile Robot Displacement Logging Using Commodity Mobile Devices (Portugal & Venghaus, Research Square preprint, Aug 2026)](https://doi.org/10.21203/rs.3.rs-10175959/v1); [Localization Limitations of ARCore, ARKit, and Hololens in Dynamic Large-scale Industry Environments (Feigl et al., VISAPP/GRAPP 2020)](https://doi.org/10.5220/0008989903070318); [Demonstration of Fused RTK (Fixed) + Inertial Positioning Using Android Smartphone Sensors Only (Bochkati, Sharma, Lichtenberger, Pany; IEEE/ION PLANS 2020)](https://doi.org/10.1109/plans46316.2020.9109865); [Static Positioning under Tree Canopy Using Low-Cost GNSS Receivers and Adapted RTKLIB (Tomastik & Everett, Sensors 2023; from prior_art.md)](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10056071/); [Segway Navimow i105N (consumer RTK + camera-vision mower, about $800-1,000; Yahoo Tech review, from prior_art.md)](https://tech.yahoo.com/home/articles/segway-navimow-i105n-review-cost-220012288.html); [OpenBot: Turning Smartphones into Robots (ICRA 2021)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2008.10631)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 4.5 / F 5 / I 4.5 = 4.67. *Novelty:* Each half has already been benchmarked. ARCore VIO accuracy for robotics has been compared head-to-head with T265 and ZED 2 (arXiv 2207.06780). Phone GNSS + phone inertial fusion across GNSS outages, compared against ARKit, exists (arXiv 1906.00360). Raw dual-frequency phone GNSS on moving vehicles against high-grade truth is the subject of the multi-year Google Smartphone Decimeter Challenge. What remains new is one ~20-30 m garage-to-curb route with an on-robot RTK+lidar reference: a new setting with thin differentiation. *Feasibility:* The team has to build three separate systems in about 12 part-time weeks: (1) a custom Android app that logs ARCore pose and raw GNSS together and also drives the base; (2) a full RTK base/rover + LD19 scan-matching + odometry reference stack in ROS2; (3) offline phone-GNSS/VIO fusion, possibly through RTKLIB. On top of that come 50 paired runs and 20 closed-loop runs. Ground truth is also underspecified. Nothing says how checkpoint error is measured on a moving robot. Stopping at checkpoints adds zero-velocity updates that help VIO, which confounds the result. The tape-surveyed local frame also has to be registered to the RTK and phone-GNSS frames to a few cm. The LD19 is listed as discontinued at Waveshare (per prior_art.md), so lead times are a risk. *Impact:* The likely outcomes are foreseeable and unlikely to excite CASE or IROS reviewers. ARCore works in daylight, loses tracking in a dark garage, and drifts roughly 1% over a short route. Phone GNSS is metre-level near a house (prior_art.md cites ~0.5 m open sky / ~1.5 m under canopy for a static Pixel 5), so it adds little at a 15 cm tolerance. The 'expensive' baseline is only $700-900, so the cost story is weak. A workshop or SoutheastCon paper is realistic. Note too that CASE 2027 is in Linz and needs a full (not student) registration, at least about $600 plus travel (venues.md). *Main criticism:* The research question is a re-run of existing benchmarks (ARCore vs other VIO in arXiv 2207.06780; phone GNSS on vehicles in the Smartphone Decimeter Challenge; phone GNSS+inertial across outages in arXiv 1906.00360) on one short driveway, and the answer is predictable. On a 20-30 m route, accuracy is set almost entirely by ARCore VIO, because phone GNSS (decimetre to metre level near buildings) cannot help at a 15 cm tolerance. ARCore will predictably fail in a dark garage. The proposed 'scale observability' insight is undercut by the checkpoint protocol: stopping at checkpoints itself supplies the excitation and zero-velocity updates.
