Meanwhile the build (custom Android app + RTK pair + lidar SLAM + fusion) is too much for 12 part-time weeks. *Closest prior work:* [An Empirical Evaluation of Four Off-the-Shelf Proprietary Visual-Inertial Odometry Systems (Kim, Song, Lee, Jung, Pyojin Kim; arXiv 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2207.06780); [Iterative Path Reconstruction for Large-Scale Inertial Navigation on Smartphones (arXiv 1906.00360, 2019)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.00360); [Google Smartphone Decimeter Challenge (summary paper, ION GNSS+ 2022) and 2024 winners, e.g. 'Optimized GNSS/INS Integration Approach for Smartphone Positioning'](https://doi.org/10.33012/2022.18379); [Constrained-Axis Visual-Inertial Odometry for Mobile Robot Displacement Logging Using Commodity Mobile Devices (2026 preprint, Research Square)](https://www.researchsquare.com/article/rs-10175959/v1); [Phone2Act: low-cost teleoperation using ARCore 6-DoF pose (arXiv 2605.01948, May 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01948); [OpenBot: Turning Smartphones into Robots (ICRA 2021)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2008.10631)

### P4.v1 (round 1, median 5.00): The lawn remembers: following your own cut edge to mow straight stripes without RTK

- **Question:** Can a robot mower with no GNSS hold uniform pass-to-pass spacing by sensing the cut/uncut height step left by its previous pass with a ~$25 multizone ToF sensor? Specifically, does its spacing consistency match an RTK mower (Segway Navimow i105N)?
- **Hypothesis:** With uncut grass ≥3 cm taller than the cut height, edge following will keep the inter-pass spacing SD ≤3 cm and the missed-strip area <1%. IMU-only heading hold on the same robot will reach SD ≥10 cm, and the Navimow's SD is expected within a factor of 1.5. The minimum reliably detectable step is predicted to be about 2 cm. The hypothesis is falsified if edge lock is lost at height differences ≥3 cm or on matted or bumpy lawns.
- **Measurements:** (1) Static edge-detection curve: the sensor's estimate of the edge's lateral position vs. tape ground truth at 300 locations (3 lawns x 3 height steps of 1.5, 3 and 5 cm x ~33). Continuous error (cm) is regressed on step height to give the minimum detectable step, the headline curve. (2) Closed loop: 3 methods (IMU-only, IMU + edge following, Navimow) x 6 runs x 8 passes of 10 m. Spacing is measured at 20 stations per adjacent pass pair, giving 140 measurements per run (~840 per method); missed-strip area is measured from overhead photos. Analysis: spacing SD and gap fraction with a mixed model (run as random effect), bootstrap CIs, and an F-test on variance ratios.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Commercial product: Segway Navimow i105N (RTK + vision, wire-free systematic mowing; $1,000 review price and $799 on Amazon at one point, both verified in prior_art.md); borrowed, or bought (FLAG). Open-source expensive reference: OpenMower (RTK retrofit, about €700 excluding donor mower and RTK base, per GitHub in prior_art.md). Within-robot control: IMU-only (BNO085) heading hold.
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: every pass writes a free physical landmark, the cut-height step, right beside the next pass. Lateral error is then measured relative to the previous pass rather than to a global frame, so drift cannot build up from one pass to the next, and centimeter global positioning becomes unnecessary for striping. Combine harvesters already steer by the crop edge (laser and vision work below), but crop edges are tens of cm high. Why it is not foregone: a lawn step is only 1-5 cm on a bumpy, matted, wind-moved surface, close to the noise floor of a hobby ToF. The method may simply not lock, or it may lock but snake. A reviewer would be surprised if a $25 sensor matched RTK striping. Crossref found agricultural cut-edge guidance only. Lawn-mower patents on following the cut edge are plausible, but Patents could not be searched this session, so this check is mandatory.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Cut Crop Edge Detection Using a Laser Sensor (2009)](https://doi.org/10.13031/2013.36205); [Machine Vision-based Guidance System for Agricultural Grain Harvesters using Cut-edge Detection (Biosystems Eng., 2003)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.biosystemseng.2003.07.002); [OpenMower (open-source RTK mower retrofit)](https://github.com/ClemensElflein/OpenMower); [Segway Navimow i105N review (RTK + vision, ~$1,000)](https://tech.yahoo.com/home/articles/segway-navimow-i105n-review-cost-220012288.html)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: a hoverboard rover pushing a manual reel mower (no powered blade, which is safer for students) through a rigid hitch; a side boom carrying the ToF sensor over the previous pass's edge; turnaround geometry; a photo mast for overhead gap measurement. Software student: VL53L5CX 8x8 height-profile acquisition and step-edge fitting (robust line fit across zones); lateral PD controller fused with IMU heading; U-turn sequencing; spacing-measurement tooling (tape stations plus photo-based gap segmentation).
- **Cost:** $1360: Rover ~$260 (hoverboard $139 verified + ESP32 $19.95 verified + frame ~$100 estimate). Manual reel mower ~$100 (estimate). VL53L5CX breakout ~$25 (UNVERIFIED). BNO085 IMU $29.50 (verified in A2). Raspberry Pi 5 4GB ~$110 (verified in A2). Hitch, boom, mast and tapes ~$40 (estimate). Subtotal ~$565. Navimow i105N: $0 if borrowed, $799 if bought (verified sale price). FLAG: ~$1,360 if bought. Cost ratio for the striping capability: ~$55 of sensing (ToF + IMU) vs. ~$580-640 for a hobby RTK pair (≈10x) or a $799-1,000 RTK mower (≈15x).
- **Venue:** IEEE CASE 2027 (Mar 1): automation without expensive infrastructure, measured cost-performance. Backup: IROS 2027.
- **Main risk:** Winter grass grows slowly (Dec-Feb), so there may not be enough fresh ≥3 cm steps for 18+ closed-loop runs. Fallback: use several lawns and the school field; prioritize the static detection curve (needs little lawn) plus fewer closed-loop runs; if needed, emulate steps with artificial-turf strips of different pile heights (weaker, and disclosed).
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 4 / I 5 = 4.67. *Novelty:* Steering a vehicle by the cut/uncut edge of its own previous pass is about 30 years old in IEEE robotics venues: Ollis & Stentz crop-line tracking (ICRA 1996, IROS 1997) and the Demeter harvester. There is also a 2017 IEEE RCAR lawn-mower 'grass boundary detection device' paper whose scope I could not check. What is left is a new setting (a 1-5 cm lawn step), a cheap sensor (multizone ToF) and a measured comparison against a consumer RTK mower. That is real but thin differentiation. Lawn-mower patents on cut-edge sensing are likely but remain UNVERIFIED: Google Patents and FreePatentsOnline both blocked access this session. *Feasibility:* The headline experiment needs fresh 1.5-5 cm cut/uncut steps on several lawns from Dec to Feb. In most of the US, grass is dormant then, and each of the 18+ closed-loop runs (8 passes x 10 m) uses up about 80 m² of regrown grass. The fallback (artificial turf) would gut the claim. Other risks: a VL53L5CX zone footprint is about 3 cm at 30 cm height (my estimate from its roughly 45-degree, 8x8 FoV), and returns from a porous grass canopy plus sunlight make a 1.5-2 cm step close to the noise floor. Measuring a Navimow's pass-to-pass spacing on dormant grass is also hard, and the bill exceeds $1,000 if the Navimow is bought. *Impact:* A clean 'minimum detectable step vs. sensor cost' curve plus a head-to-head against a commercial RTK mower fits CASE's automation-cost theme. But 'edge following beats IMU-only dead reckoning' is foreseeable, and the mechanism's precedent is well known. That makes this more likely a workshop, ARSO or SoutheastCon paper than a CASE/IROS main-track one. It is a decent application story, especially if the Navimow comparison gets done. *Main criticism:* The core insight ('each pass writes a landmark for the next, so no global positioning is needed') is the 1996-2002 CMU Demeter crop-line-tracking idea. An IROS/CASE reviewer will cite it immediately. The only new part is whether a $25 ToF can resolve a 1-5 cm lawn step, and that experiment depends on growing grass during Dec-Feb, when lawns are mostly dormant. So the likely paper is a turf-strip sensor test with a foreseeable closed-loop result. *Closest prior work:* [First results in vision-based crop line tracking (Ollis & Stentz, IEEE ICRA 1996)](https://doi.org/10.1109/robot.1996.503895); [Vision-based perception for an automated harvester (Ollis & Stentz, IROS 1997)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.1997.656612); [The Demeter System for Automated Harvesting (Pilarski et al., Autonomous Robots 2002)](https://doi.org/10.1023/a:1015622020131); [Vision-based uncut crop edge detection for automated guidance of head-feeding combine (Eng. in Agriculture, Environment and Food, 2014)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.eaef.2013.12.010); [Robust grass boundary detection for lawn mower with a novel design of detection device (Guo et al., IEEE RCAR 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/rcar.2017.8311863); [Estimation of Lawn Grass Lengths based on Random Forest Algorithm for Robotic Lawn Mower (SICE 2020)](https://doi.org/10.23919/sice48898.2020.9240338); [Cut Crop Edge Detection Using a Laser Sensor (2009, cited by proposal)](https://doi.org/10.13031/2013.36205)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 4.5 / I 5.5 = 5.00. *Novelty:* Following the cut/uncut edge left by the previous pass is an old idea in robotics, not just in agriculture. CMU's Demeter crop-line tracking appeared at ICRA 1996 and IROS 1997 (DOIs confirmed), and range-sensor and vision cut-edge guidance followed in 2003, 2009 and 2014. I could not search patents at all: Google Patents returned 503 and a rate-limit page, and Justia and FPO returned 403. Lawn-mower patents on following the mowed/unmowed boundary (I recall 1980s Japanese work vehicle patents) are likely but UNVERIFIED. What is new is lawn scale (1-5 cm steps), a hobby ToF, a minimum-detectable-step curve, and a head-to-head against a consumer RTK mower. That is a new evaluation of a known mechanism, so the differentiation is real but thin. *Feasibility:* The closed-loop experiment needs fresh, actively growing uncut grass in January and February. In most of the US, lawns are then dormant, frost-matted or under snow, and reel mowers cut dormant or wet grass poorly. Each closed-loop run destroys its own step, so 3 methods x 6 runs x 8 passes x 10 m needs several hundred m² of fresh ≥3 cm steps, about one reel width x 80 m per run. The Navimow also has to be borrowed or bought ($799, pushing the total to ~$1,360), and it has to be made to mow the same 10 m x 8-pass test plot. The static curve can be run in Oct-Nov, which is a real mitigation, but the headline head-to-head comparison is at high risk, and the artificial-turf fallback undercuts the claim. *Impact:* If it works, the cost story is clean (~$55 of sensing vs. a $799-1,000 RTK mower) and fits a CASE/ARSO-style 'automation without infrastructure' paper. Still, IROS/CASE reviewers will point to Demeter-era cut-line following. The chosen metric (pass-to-pass spacing SD) is the quantity edge following controls directly, while RTK optimizes global position, so the comparison will look stacked in its favor. Main-track acceptance is unlikely. This is more realistic for ARSO, SoutheastCon or a workshop, and it reads as a good, distinctive application story. *Main criticism:* The mechanism is old (Demeter's cut-line tracking at ICRA'96/IROS'97, plus later cut-edge guidance work, and probably mowed/unmowed-boundary patents that I could not check). The proposal's headline metric, inter-pass spacing SD, is exactly the relative quantity edge following controls by construction. A snaking or drifting first pass is copied faithfully into every later pass, so 'matches RTK' on spacing SD says little about global straightness or coverage. On top of that, the closed-loop data must be collected in Dec-Feb, when grass is dormant. *Closest prior work:* [Ollis & Stentz, 'First results in vision-based crop line tracking' (ICRA 1996)](https://doi.org/10.1109/robot.1996.503895); [Ollis & Stentz, 'Vision-based perception for an automated harvester' (IROS 1997); Pilarski et al., 'The Demeter System for Automated Harvesting' (Autonomous Robots 2002)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.1997.656612); [Cho et al., 'Vision-based uncut crop edge detection for automated guidance of head-feeding combine' (EAEF 2014)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.eaef.2013.12.010); [Pitla, Luck & Shearer, 'Cut Crop Edge Detection Using a Laser Sensor' (ASABE 2009), cited by the proposal](https://doi.org/10.13031/2013.36205); [Benson, Reid & Zhang, 'Machine Vision-based Guidance System for Agricultural Grain Harvesters using Cut-edge Detection' (Biosystems Eng. 2003), cited by the proposal](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.biosystemseng.2003.07.002); [Zushida et al., 'Estimation of Lawn Grass Lengths based on Random Forest Algorithm for Robotic Lawn Mower' (SICE 2020)](https://doi.org/10.23919/sice48898.2020.9240338); [Guo et al., 'Robust grass boundary detection for lawn mower with a novel design of detection device' (RCAR 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/rcar.2017.8311863)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5.5 / F 4.5 / I 5.5 = 5.17. *Novelty:* The central insight, steering by the cut/uncut line your own previous pass left, was already published by CMU's Demeter project at ICRA 1996 and IROS 1997 (doi 10.1109/robot.1996.503895, 10.1109/iros.1997.656612), and the proposal does not cite it. What remains new is real but thin: a 1-5 cm lawn-scale step, a ~$30 multizone ToF, a minimum-detectable-step curve, and the first measured stripe-spacing comparison against a consumer RTK mower. Lawn-mower patents on cut-edge following are still UNCHECKED: Google Patents returned 503 this session and the search budget was exhausted. Patents of this kind are likely enough that the score could drop to 3-4 once someone checks. *Feasibility:* The hardware is simple: a hoverboard rover pushing a reel mower, with a ToF sensor on a boom. The experiment, though, needs about 18 closed-loop runs x 8 passes x 10 m (roughly 500+ m² of fresh grass at least 3 cm above cut height) during Dec-Feb, when most US lawns are dormant or frost- or snow-covered and cut grass does not grow back. The Navimow arm adds a loan, mapping and RTK base setup, and a mower that may refuse to run on frosty or wet turf. There is also a measurement-design flaw: each pass cuts away the edge of the previous one, so inter-pass spacing cannot be read off the lawn afterward. Every method would need a separate track marker or a logged trajectory with ground truth, and the Navimow's overlapping passes may leave no measurable edges at all. *Impact:* A clean minimum-detectable-step curve plus a closed-loop result of the form 'a $30 ToF matches RTK stripe spacing' would make a decent CASE submission on cost and performance, and the college-application story is good. However, the concept's lineage (Demeter, IROS 1997) makes a main-track paper hinge on the RTK head-to-head being rigorous. A winter-degraded dataset (turf strips, few runs, a missing Navimow arm) would push it to workshop or SoutheastCon level. *Main criticism:* The idea of following your own previous cut edge is a 1996-97 ICRA/IROS idea (CMU Demeter), and the patent check is still undone. What is left is a scale-down to a lawn with a hobby ToF. Its core experiment, hundreds of square meters of fresh 3+ cm grass steps plus a borrowed RTK mower, is scheduled for Dec-Feb, when grass does not grow and robot mowers often will not run. The inter-pass spacing metric also cannot be measured as described, because each pass erases the edge of the previous one. *Closest prior work:* [First results in vision-based crop line tracking (Ollis & Stentz, ICRA 1996)](https://doi.org/10.1109/robot.1996.503895); [Vision-based perception for an automated harvester (Ollis & Stentz, IROS 1997)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.1997.656612); [Estimation of Lawn Grass Lengths based on Random Forest Algorithm for Robotic Lawn Mower (Zushida et al., SICE 2020)](https://doi.org/10.23919/sice48898.2020.9240338); [Robust grass boundary detection for lawn mower with a novel design of detection device (Guo et al., RCAR 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/rcar.2017.8311863); [Autonomous Boundary Detection Using Image Recognition for Robotic Lawn Mower (Wu, Chang, Yu, ICMT 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icmt56556.2022.9997764); [Cut Crop Edge Detection Using a Laser Sensor (2009, cited by the proposal)](https://doi.org/10.13031/2013.36205); [OpenMower (open-source RTK mower retrofit)](https://github.com/ClemensElflein/OpenMower)

### R1.v1 (round 1, median 4.83): Wild Visual Navigation on a $500 hoverboard robot: motor current, not velocity error, as the self-supervision signal

- **Question:** Can WVN's online self-supervised traversability learning, published on an ANYmal legged robot with a Jetson AGX Orin, reach the paper's <5-minute bootstrap on a hoverboard-drive robot with a $249 Orin Nano Super? Does it get there when the supervision label is normalized motor current instead of the paper's velocity-tracking error?
- **Hypothesis:** On a speed-controlled hoverboard FOC drive, velocity-tracking error stays near zero until the motors saturate, so WVN's original label is close to uninformative. A model trained on it will reach pixel AUROC <=0.65 against hand labels (grass / mulch / gravel / concrete / mud). A label built from normalized effort (phase current divided by commanded speed, sigmoid-scaled like WVN) will reach AUROC >=0.80 within 5 min of driving. Falsified if the two labels differ by less than 0.05 AUROC, or if the current label also stays below 0.70.
- **Measurements:** Design: 20 independent 5-min WVN training sessions in the same mixed-terrain yard: 10 with the robot unloaded and 10 towing an unmodified 96-gal cart (load is a known confound for current). Each session logs both signals, so the two labels are compared paired, on identical data, offline and online. Metrics: pixel AUROC and F1 on 50 hand-labeled held-out frames per session, and minutes until AUROC >=0.8. Then 2 x 20 closed-loop point-to-point runs (one model per label) on a fixed 30 m mixed-terrain course, recording interventions per 100 m and goals reached, which are compared with WVN's 8/8 goals and its intervention reports. Analysis: paired t-test or Wilcoxon on per-session AUROC difference. With 20 pairs and an assumed SD of differences of about 0.08, a 0.06 AUROC difference is detectable at roughly 80% power (estimate). Bootstrap CIs on interventions/100 m.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Reference result: Mattamala, Frey et al., 'Wild Visual Navigation' (arXiv 2404.07110, https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.07110; RSS 2023 version arXiv 2305.08510). The page confirms ANYmal C/D with a Jetson AGX Orin, DINO-ViT features at 224x224, velocity-tracking-error supervision, <5 min bootstrap, 8/8 goals and 0.5-1.4 km runs. Reference hardware cost: ANYmal price is not public (UNVERIFIED, generally understood as six figures), and the AGX Orin dev kit is about $2,000 (UNVERIFIED estimate). In-house baselines run on the same robot with the released code: (a) WVN's original velocity-error label, and (b) a team laptop GPU running the original-resolution pipeline, as an upper bound that isolates the compute downgrade.
- **Novelty claim:** The one insight: cheap high-gain speed-controlled drivetrains hide terrain difficulty in effort (current), not in tracking error, so the label must be moved. WVN was tested only on legged robots (confirmed: 'no wheeled platforms were evaluated'). The outcome is genuinely uncertain. Current may be swamped by the towed load, by temperature, or by FOC noise, and DINO features on a 6-8x weaker GPU may learn too slowly. Either finding is informative. A reviewer would be surprised if the paper's own label fails badly on a wheeled robot. An arXiv abstract search found drive-current-based traversability only in a non-visual sewer-pipe robot, and no low-cost WVN replication. Google Scholar/IEEE were NOT searched (search budget exhausted), so the team must re-check.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Wild Visual Navigation: Fast Traversability Learning via Pre-Trained Models and Online Self-Supervision (Mattamala, Frey et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.07110); [hoverboard-firmware-hack-FOC (EFeru), open FOC firmware with speed/torque modes and current readout](https://github.com/EFeru/hoverboard-firmware-hack-FOC); [arXiv 2606.14070 (sewer-pipe inspection robot; search listing says it uses drive-wheel current waveforms for traversability assessment)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14070); [ROMR hoverboard-motor ROS base (HardwareX 2023)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10197097)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: hoverboard hub-motor base (two motors, mainboard reflashed with EFeru FOC firmware, plywood/aluminum deck, rear caster, cart-handle hook from the earlier work) and a camera mast at about 0.8 m. Software student: ROS 2 on Orin Nano Super, released WVN code ported to ROS 2 or run in ROS 1 Docker, a UART bridge for current/speed telemetry, the current-based label (Kalman-smoothed, sigmoid), a hand-labeling tool for 1,000 evaluation frames, and a simple local planner driven by the traversability map.
- **Cost:** $520: Jetson Orin Nano Super dev kit $249 (NVIDIA blog, in prior_art.md). Used hoverboard ~$80 (estimate, UNVERIFIED). USB camera ~$13 (LeKiwi BOM lists 2 for $25.96). IMU ~$15 (estimate). DC-DC converters and wiring ~$40 (estimate). Deck, caster and fasteners ~$70 (estimate). Hook plate, already designed, ~$20. 64/96-gal carts owned by the household ($0). Team laptop for the upper-bound condition ($0). Reference ANYmal + AGX Orin not purchased; compute alone is about 8x cheaper (AGX price UNVERIFIED).
- **Venue:** IEEE/RSJ IROS 2027 (Mar 1, 2027). Learning-based field navigation fits IROS better than CASE, and a replication-gap result on a wheeled platform is an IROS-style contribution. CASE is the fallback if it ends up framed as a cost/automation study.
- **Main risk:** Hoverboard phase-current telemetry may be too coarse or noisy (or temperature-dependent), and DINO on an Orin Nano may update too slowly in winter light. Fallback: use battery power per unit speed from the mainboard voltage/current, or IMU-measured wheel-slip ratio, as the effort label. Run feature extraction at lower resolution. Even a negative result (neither label works on cheap wheels) is a publishable replication gap with a diagnosed cause.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 4 / F 5 / I 5 = 4.67. *Novelty:* The two halves of the claimed insight are already published in 2026. Current draw as the self-supervision label is in P-STEER (ICARSC 2026). WVN-style online DINO-ViT learning with the label replaced by a wheeled-robot proprioceptive signal is in Kawase et al. (AIM 2026, which cites WVN). A cheap-compute WVN alternative on a wheeled robot that compares label sources is in FLINT (arXiv 2609.26857). What remains is 'current label on a sub-$500 hoverboard with an Orin Nano', which is a thin re-combination. Also, the claim that velocity error is uninformative on a speed-controlled wheeled drive is predictable by construction when measured velocity comes from wheel odometry. *Feasibility:* There are several stacked risks. WVN is ROS 1 Noetic only per its README, so it has to be dockerized or ported onto JetPack 6 on an 8 GB Orin Nano that runs DINO extraction and online training together. The EFeru firmware does NOT send current over UART: the SerialFeedback struct has speed, battery voltage and temperature only, though left/right DC current is computed internally, so a firmware edit is needed. The workload is 20 sessions plus 40 closed-loop runs plus 1,000 hand-labeled frames, done Jan 16-Feb 14 outdoors in winter, when dormant or frozen ground may erase the grass/mud/mulch contrast. The cost (~$520) is fine. *Impact:* With P-STEER, Kawase et al. and FLINT all in 2026, an IROS reviewer would see an incremental replication on a toy platform, so main-track odds are low. A clean head-to-head of cheap labels on a $500 robot is a reasonable fit for ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA/IROS field-robotics workshop, and it makes a good application story ('ported an RSS/ETH system to a $500 robot'). *Main criticism:* The 'one insight' is already published. P-STEER (2026) uses motor current draw as the traversability self-supervision label. Kawase et al. (AIM 2026) already moved WVN's label to a wheeled-specific proprioceptive signal (slip, IMU, wheel-speed variance) with DINO-ViT online learning. FLINT (2026) already delivers a cheaper-than-WVN traversability learner on a wheeled robot and compares label sources. The chosen strawman, WVN's velocity-tracking error, fails by construction if measured velocity comes from wheel encoders on a stiff speed-controlled drive, so beating it is foreseeable. The binary-AUROC ground truth over surface classes (grass/gravel/concrete) is also ill-defined, because nearly all of those surfaces are traversable for the robot. *Closest prior work:* [P-STEER: Self-Supervised Learning for Energy-Efficient Traversability Prediction in Natural Terrain Using Proprioception (Carvalho, Portugal, Peixoto; IEEE ICARSC 2026)](https://doi.org/10.1109/ICARSC70216.2026.11523346); [Online Self-Supervised Traversability Estimation for Wheeled Robots on Unstructured Terrain (Kawase, Takahashi, Kuroda; IEEE/ASME AIM 2026)](https://doi.org/10.1109/AIM65483.2026.11658227); [FLINT: Fast Lightweight Inference for Traversability (Bonilla et al., arXiv Sept 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26857); [Wild Visual Navigation (Mattamala, Frey et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.07110); [Development of a 3 in Sewer Pipe Inspection Robot ... (Umemura, Taniguchi, Kakogawa; arXiv 2606.14070)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14070); [WayFAST: Navigation with Predictive Traversability in the Field (arXiv 2203.12071)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.12071)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 4 / F 5 / I 5.5 = 4.83. *Novelty:* 'WVN-style online self-supervised traversability on a wheeled robot' already appears as an AIM 2026 paper title (Kawase et al., doi 10.1109/aim65483.2026.11658227), and the official WVN repo ships a Jackal (wheeled) integration. 'Proprioceptive or energy signal as the self-supervision label' appears in ICARSC 2026 (Carvalho et al.) and in Castro et al. 2022 (wheeled off-road robots). The only thin new parts are the hoverboard FOC current label and the Orin Nano cost axis, and I could not read either 2026 abstract, so they may close even that gap. *Feasibility:* The software chain is the risk. The WVN code is ROS 1 Noetic only (README), so it needs Docker on JetPack 6. The Orin Nano Super has 8 GB shared memory and must run DINO features plus online training. On top of that come a live current-telemetry UART bridge, a local planner, and 1,000 hand-labeled frames. The test matrix is 20 sessions plus 40 closed-loop runs in Jan-Feb on dormant winter grass/mud, using a 6.5-in-wheel hoverboard base that may simply stall on mulch or mud, and an EFeru board-compatibility risk the earlier rounds already flagged. *Impact:* A clean paired label comparison with a diagnosed cause would make a solid ICRA-workshop, ARSO or SoutheastCon paper, and it is a strong application story (learning plus hardware). IROS main track is unlikely: the data is one yard and one robot, the AIM 2026 wheeled-WVN work already exists, and the headline 'velocity error is uninformative on a speed-controlled drive' is close to self-evident to reviewers. *Main criticism:* The core comparison is partly a strawman and is already crowded. WVN's label is mainly the robot's footprint from a short human demonstration, which marks wherever it drove as traversable, with velocity error only modulating that. On a teleoperated wheeled robot that never drives into truly untraversable terrain, both labels will be near 1 on almost every sample, so neither sees real negatives. AUROC against hand labels of 'grass/mulch/gravel/concrete/mud' (all drivable except perhaps mud) then measures terrain discrimination, not traversability. Meanwhile, wheeled WVN-style learning (AIM 2026) and proprioceptive/energy labels (ICARSC 2026, Castro 2022) are already published, so what remains is 'cheap hardware plus one more proprioceptive signal'. *Closest prior work:* [Online Self-Supervised Traversability Estimation for Wheeled Robots on Unstructured Terrain (Kawase, Takahashi, Kuroda, Meiji Univ.; IEEE/ASME AIM 2026)](https://doi.org/10.1109/aim65483.2026.11658227); [Self-Supervised Learning for Energy-Efficient Traversability Prediction in Natural Terrain Using Proprioception (Carvalho, Portugal, Peixoto, Univ. Coimbra; IEEE ICARSC 2026)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icarsc70216.2026.11523346); [leggedrobotics/wild_visual_navigation official code repository](https://github.com/leggedrobotics/wild_visual_navigation); [How Does It Feel? Self-Supervised Costmap Learning for Off-Road Vehicle Traversability (Castro, Triest, Wang, ... Scherer; arXiv 2209.10788)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.10788); [Wild Visual Navigation (Mattamala, Frey et al., arXiv 2404.07110)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.07110); [Umemura, Taniguchi, Kakogawa: 3-in sewer pipe inspection robot (arXiv 2606.14070)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14070); [Learning Off-Road Terrain Traversability With Self-Supervisions Only (RA-L 2023) and V-STRONG (ICRA 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2023.3284356)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5.5 / F 5 / I 5.5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* Two parts are not new. Self-supervised visual traversability on wheeled robots from a traction label already exists (WayFAST, arXiv 2203.12071, IROS 2022), and so do energy-consumption labels for terrain (arXiv 2104.01560). The new part is a paired comparison of WVN's velocity-error label against an effort label on a cheap speed-controlled drive. That axis is real but thin, and its expected outcome depends mostly on which velocity estimate is used. *Feasibility:* The plan stacks several risky pieces into 12 part-time winter weeks:
- Porting the ROS 1 WVN code to an 8 GB Orin Nano Super with online DINO training.
- Current telemetry that may need firmware changes.
- Hand-labeling 1,000 frames at pixel level.
- 20 sessions plus 40 closed-loop runs in a yard in Jan-Feb, when grass is dormant or snowy and mud may be frozen.
Each piece is doable alone, but the experiment matrix is likely to slip. Also, the EFeru firmware's default UART feedback is speed, battery voltage and temperature, and I recall it includes no current field (UNVERIFIED this session). If so, the team must modify the firmware, and what it measures is DC-link current, not phase current. *Impact:* A one-yard, one-robot comparison of two labels is unlikely to clear the IROS main track, where wheeled self-supervised traversability is crowded (WayFAST was itself IROS 2022). It fits an ICRA 2027 workshop or ARSO better, but ARSO and SoutheastCon close before Mar 1 according to venues.md. CASE fit is weak. It would still be a respectable, technically serious story for college applications. *Main criticism:* The central hypothesis is close to a strawman. WVN's label compares commanded velocity against the state-estimated body velocity, not wheel speed. On a hoverboard, that error is near zero only if body velocity comes from wheel odometry. If it comes from VIO or the IMU, wheel slip shows up directly in the error, and WayFAST already learned visual traversability on wheeled robots from this kind of traction signal. So the result mostly reflects how the team computes the label, which is foreseeable, much like the earlier reviewer complaints about predictable comparisons. A second problem is that the ground truth is ill-posed. Grass, mulch, gravel and concrete are all traversable for the robot. An effort label will learn 'grass costs more than concrete' (rolling resistance), which is a cost map. Scoring it with AUROC against hand-drawn binary traversability masks then measures whatever definition the labelers chose. Towing the cart adds a load confound that normalized current only partly removes. *Closest prior work:* [Wild Visual Navigation: Fast Traversability Learning via Pre-Trained Models and Online Self-Supervision (Mattamala, Frey et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.07110); [WayFAST: Navigation with Predictive Traversability in the Field (Gasparino et al., RA-L 2022 / IROS 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.12071); [EVORA: Deep Evidential Traversability Learning for Risk-Aware Off-Road Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.06234); [Conv1D Energy-Aware Path Planner for Mobile Robots in Unstructured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2104.01560); [STERLING: Self-Supervised Terrain Representation Learning from Unconstrained Robot Experience (Karnan et al., 2023)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15302); [Sewer-pipe inspection robot (arXiv 2606.14070, Umemura, Taniguchi, Kakogawa)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14070)

### P1.v1 (round 1, median 4.83): Red-edge lawn fence: a ~$20 active red/NIR grass sensor vs. a camera-AI wire-free mower (WORX Landroid Vision)

- **Question:** On the same lawn edges, does a ~$20 active two-wavelength (660 nm / 850 nm) reflectance-ratio sensor keep a robot inside the lawn, measured as overshoot past the grass edge in cm, at least as well as the camera-plus-AI WORX Landroid Vision in full sun, dappled shade and darkness (<10 lux)?
- **Hypothesis:** In full sun on grass/concrete and grass/mulch edges, the ratio sensor's median overshoot will be no more than 2 cm worse than the Landroid Vision's. In dappled shade and at <10 lux it will be at least 50% lower than the Vision's. Two failures are predicted in advance: on dormant (brown) warm-season grass the sensor treats the lawn as 'not grass' (contrast collapses), and it is blind to artificial turf. The hypothesis is falsified if the Vision mower beats the sensor in shade or darkness, or if the sensor overshoots by more than 2 cm beyond the Vision in sun.
- **Measurements:** (1) Perpendicular edge approaches at 0.3 m/s with stop-on-detect. Overshoot (cm) is the front contact point past a chalk-marked edge line (tape, about +/-0.5 cm). (2) Edge following: lateral offset every 0.5 m along 10 m edges. Conditions: 3 edge types (concrete, bark mulch, bare soil/weedy) x 3 lighting levels (noon sun, dappled shade, <10 lux) = 9 cells x 30 approaches = 270 sensor approaches (about 3 robot-days). The Landroid Vision is scored on 30 boundary events per cell, filmed by a tripod overhead camera over a chalk grid; peak excursion past the edge is the same continuous metric. Power: with an overshoot SD of about 3 cm, n=30 gives a 95% CI half-width of about +/-1.1 cm per cell. Analysis: Hodges-Lehmann shift with bootstrap CIs per cell, plus a mixed model (system x light, edge site random). Side tests at n=20 each: dew-wet grass, dormant grass, artificial turf. Bench test: the ratio contrast of grass vs. 8 non-grass surfaces under 0-100 klux ambient light (lux meter).
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Commercial product: the WORX Landroid Vision, a camera-only wire-free mower. WORX states it uses an HDR, full-HD wide-angle camera sampling every 0.05 s with AI to 'identify grass, obstacles and edges in real time', and it publishes no edge-accuracy figure (au.worx.com, fetched). Price: AU$1,499 (M600) to AU$1,999 (L1300), per a search snippet in prior_art.md. US price UNVERIFIED (estimate $1,000-1,500). The team borrows one from a neighbor or dealer, or buys used. Within-robot control: a Pi Camera Module 3 ($25) with a simple color/texture classifier on the same rover, so the same chassis and controller are compared.
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: living chlorophyll absorbs red and strongly reflects near-IR. If the sensor chops its own LEDs and demodulates them (lock-in), sunlight and shadow become a DC term that drops out. The red/NIR ratio then answers 'is this grass?' as a one-pixel measurement that does not depend on lighting. Cameras, by contrast, have to infer that from color and texture and struggle with hard shadows and darkness. Why it is not foregone: the 2-3 cm sensor footprint blurs the edge, mower bounce changes the sensing height, and wet grass gives specular glare. Weeds also count as 'grass', and dormant turf may defeat the ratio entirely, so the product could win in sun or on mixed edges. A reviewer would be surprised if a $20 sensor matched or beat a $1,000+ AI mower at the boundary, the mower's core selling point. Prior work found: camera-based mower boundary detection (ICMT 2022), and an RCAR 2017 'novel detection device' whose sensing principle could not be verified (the abstract is paywalled; the team must read it in October). arXiv returned 0 hits for 'lawn mower grass boundary', and Crossref showed no red/NIR mower-boundary paper. Google Patents and Scholar could not be searched in this session (search budget exhausted), and optical grass-sensor patents are plausible, so a patent check is required before committing.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [WORX Landroid Vision product page (camera + AI, no wire; no accuracy claims)](https://au.worx.com/landroid/landroid-vision/); [Robust grass boundary detection for lawn mower with a novel design of detection device (Guo et al., RCAR 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/RCAR.2017.8311863); [Autonomous Boundary Detection Using Image Recognition for Robotic Lawn Mower (ICMT 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icmt56556.2022.9997764); [Consumer yard robots publish no accuracy data (prior_art.md; Navimow i105N review)](https://tech.yahoo.com/home/articles/segway-navimow-i105n-review-cost-220012288.html)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: a hoverboard-motor rover with a front sensor boom at an adjustable 5-8 cm height and a small light shroud; a chalk-line jig and tripod mount for overhead video. Electronics, shared: 2x 660 nm and 2x 850 nm LEDs driven at 1-5 kHz; two photodiodes with transimpedance amplifiers; ESP32 ADC with software lock-in demodulation. Two laterally spaced sensor heads give an edge-following error signal. Software student: ratio thresholding with hysteresis, stop and edge-follow PID, logging; a Pi-camera classifier as the within-robot baseline; a video-annotation tool to measure the Landroid's boundary excursions; analysis in Python.
- **Cost:** $1150: Rover: Gotrax hoverboard $139 (verified in project file B1) + ESP32 Feather V2 $19.95 (verified) + frame/caster/wiring ~$100 (estimate). Sensor: LEDs, photodiodes, op-amps, perfboard ~$20-25 (estimate). Camera baseline: Pi Camera Module 3 $25 (verified in A2) + Raspberry Pi 5 4GB ~$110 (verified in A2). Lux meter, chalk, tape ~$30 (estimate). Subtotal ~$450. Product: $0 if borrowed; a used Landroid Vision is an estimated ~$600-900 (UNVERIFIED). FLAG: about $1,050-1,350 if the product must be bought. Cost ratio for the capability: about $25 of sensing vs. camera + AI compute (~$25 camera + $249 Jetson Orin Nano Super, verified) is about 11x; vs. the ~$1,000+ product it is about 40x.
- **Venue:** IROS 2027 (Mar 1). Service robots are a listed topic, and the 2027 theme 'Human-Centric Robots for Sustainable Life' fits. Backup: CASE 2027 (Mar 1) if framed as a cost-effectiveness automation study.
- **Main risk:** Getting a Landroid Vision (US availability and loan), and winter dormancy of warm-season lawns. Fallback: use a Segway Navimow i105N (VisionFence + RTK; $799-1,000, verified) as the product, plus the within-robot camera baseline; run on cool-season lawns or a school field. Even if the product comparison collapses, the light-invariance bench curve and the turf/dormancy failure map remain publishable.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 4.5 / I 5 = 4.83. *Novelty:* Neither the closest academic mower work (RCAR 2017 IR-triangulation device; IROS 2010 camera grass segmentation) nor any source I found reports a single-pixel red/NIR ratio edge sensor measured against a shipped vision mower in cm of overshoot. The sensing principle itself (active, modulated red/NIR vegetation detection) is very likely decades-old agricultural practice (UNVERIFIED here), so the contribution is a known sensor applied to a new setting plus new head-to-head measurements: real but thin. *Feasibility:* The sensor and rover are cheap and buildable (~$450 without the product), but the main trials fall in January. In most of the US, lawns are dormant, frosted or snow-covered then, and this angle itself predicts that dormant turf breaks the sensor. Commercial mowers also typically restrict cold, wet or night operation, so collecting 270 filmed Landroid boundary events (in darkness <10 lux, in winter) on a borrowed or used unit of uncertain US availability is unlikely by Feb 20. *Impact:* A clean light-invariance bench curve plus a cm-level edge comparison is a respectable SoutheastCon/ARSO/workshop paper and a good application story. An IROS/CASE reviewer will object that overshoot depends on the mower's boundary policy (how close it is programmed to cut to the edge, speed, turn margin), not only on sensing. Comparing a stop-on-detect research rover against a black-box product confounds the two, and the darkness cell may be trivially won if the product won't mow at night. *Main criticism:* The headline comparison is confounded and badly timed. 'Overshoot past the edge' for the Landroid Vision reflects WORX's boundary-control policy (it may deliberately stop short of the edge or refuse to run at night or in cold, wet conditions), not the accuracy of its grass perception, so a $20 sensor 'beating' it does not isolate sensing. Meanwhile the sensing idea (an ambient-rejecting active red/NIR vegetation detector) is likely standard agricultural technology, and the experiments must happen on winter lawns that the angle itself predicts will defeat the sensor. *Closest prior work:* [Robust grass boundary detection for lawn mower with a novel design of detection device (Guo, Wu, Sun, Ou, Feng; RCAR 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/RCAR.2017.8311863); [Visual segmentation of lawn grass for a mobile robotic lawnmower (Schepelmann, Hudson, Merat, Quinn; IROS 2010)](https://api.semanticscholar.org/graph/v1/paper/DOI:10.1109/IROS.2010.5650430); [Autonomous Boundary Detection Using Image Recognition for Robotic Lawn Mower (ICMT 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icmt56556.2022.9997764); [WORX Landroid Vision product page (camera + AI, no wire)](https://au.worx.com/landroid/landroid-vision/); [Active modulated red/NIR plant sensors in agriculture (GreenSeeker / WeedSeeker / Crop Circle class)](none verified (search budget exhausted; Crossref and OpenAlex rate-limited))
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 4.5 / F 4.5 / I 5 = 4.67. *Novelty:* The verified RCAR 2017 abstract (Guo et al.) already describes an active-IR, bandpass-filtered grass-boundary device for mowers that is robust across conditions. Active modulated red/NIR 'is it a green plant?' sensing is also decades-old agricultural practice (GreenSeeker/WeedSeeker-style sensors; not link-verified this session, so labeled UNVERIFIED). What remains new is the measured head-to-head against a shipping camera-AI mower, so this is a known technique in a new setting with a product benchmark. *Feasibility:* The sensor itself is easy: LEDs, photodiodes, TIA and an ESP32 lock-in. The experiment is the problem. It needs (a) a borrowed or bought Landroid Vision (US availability and price UNVERIFIED; about $1,050-1,350 total if bought, over budget), (b) green lawns and a mowing-season product during Dec-Feb, when much of the US has dormant or snow-covered grass and the proposal itself predicts the sensor fails on dormant turf, and (c) 270 sensor approaches plus 270 filmed and hand-annotated product boundary events, plus three side tests, all in about 6 winter weekends. *Impact:* A cheap single-pixel NDVI-style edge sensor with a product benchmark would be a decent ARSO/SoutheastCon/workshop paper and a nice application story. IROS main-track reviewers would likely see a re-application of a known optical principle with an unfair comparison: whole-product policy vs. a sensor on a stop-on-detect rover. Main-track odds are low. *Main criticism:* The comparison is foreseeable and apples-to-oranges, the same weakness the earlier round flagged. An active self-illuminated sensor beating a passive camera in darkness is predictable from physics. The proposal also measures the Landroid's boundary behavior: a product control policy that likely keeps a deliberate inside margin, may refuse to run in the dark, and may stop in wet conditions (UNVERIFIED). That is not its edge-detection accuracy. And active IR grass-boundary sensing for mowers already exists (RCAR 2017), so the headline result ('a $20 sensor beats a $1,000 AI mower in the dark') is neither surprising nor cleanly attributable to the sensor. *Closest prior work:* [Robust grass boundary detection for lawn mower with a novel design of detection device (Guo, Wu, Sun, Ou, Feng; RCAR 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/RCAR.2017.8311863); [WORX Landroid Vision product page (camera + AI, wire-free)](https://au.worx.com/landroid/landroid-vision/); [Autonomous Boundary Detection Using Image Recognition for Robotic Lawn Mower (ICMT 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icmt56556.2022.9997764); [Segway Navimow i105N review (Yahoo Tech) — consumer wire-free mower, no accuracy data](https://tech.yahoo.com/home/articles/segway-navimow-i105n-review-cost-220012288.html)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5 / F 4.5 / I 5 = 4.83. *Novelty:* The sensing idea is old: modulated red/NIR LEDs with demodulation give a sunlight-proof one-pixel 'is this a green plant?' test, which is how commercial plant sensors like WeedSeeker and GreenSeeker work. WeedSeeker was evaluated in 2003 (ASABE aid=13980), and low-cost active NDVI builds were published in 2019, 2021 (IEEE Sensors Letters) and 2024. Cheap point lawn sensors already exist in hobby mowers (Ardumower's capacitive lawnSensor), and RCAR 2017 already built an active-IR (triangulation) grass-boundary device. What is new is applying it to robot-mower edge keeping and measuring a shipped vision mower head-to-head. That is a real but thin difference, and I could not run a patent check (Google Patents and FreePatentsOnline blocked the queries), so mower patents on optical grass sensing remain a live risk. *Feasibility:* The sensor and rover are easy. The experiment is not, for three reasons. (1) A Landroid Vision may not be obtainable at all: US availability is UNVERIFIED, the AU page showed no products, and a borrowed or used unit ($600-900, UNVERIFIED) pushes the budget to about $1,050-1,350. (2) All trials fall in Dec-Feb, when US lawns are dormant, frosted or under snow, and robotic mowers are normally not run then. (3) The product cannot be commanded to make perpendicular 0.3 m/s approaches, so its 270 'boundary events' depend on its own behaviour and on mapping and setup on each test lawn. *Impact:* 'A $20 sensor matches a $1,000+ AI mower at the edge' is a good headline for college applications, and SoutheastCon, ARSO or an IEEE Sensors Letters-style outlet would fit. IROS reviewers would recognise the method as standard agricultural active NDVI and see a single-product, single-season comparison. Main-track acceptance is therefore unlikely, while CASE or a workshop is plausible. *Main criticism:* The head-to-head is not apples-to-apples, and the 'insight' is borrowed. A mower is designed to drive to or slightly over the edge so it cuts the last strip, so less overshoot is not better if the robot stops short and leaves uncut grass. The Landroid cannot be made to run the same controlled approaches (speed, angle, stop-on-detect) as the rover. It brings its own lighting at night, which defeats the darkness hypothesis. And it would have to be filmed on borrowed, mapped lawns in winter, when grass is dormant or frosted. On top of that, the light-invariant red/NIR lock-in principle has been commercial in WeedSeeker/GreenSeeker for 20+ years. The resulting paper is mainly 'agricultural NDVI sensor applied to a mower edge, compared against one product under uncontrolled conditions'. *Closest prior work:* [Robust grass boundary detection for lawn mower with a novel design of detection device (Guo, Wu, Sun, Ou, Feng; RCAR 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/RCAR.2017.8311863); [Performance Evaluation of an Optical Sensor for Weed Detection (Antuniassi et al., ASABE 2003): an evaluation of the commercial WeedSeeker](https://elibrary.asabe.org/abstract.asp?aid=13980); [Statistical Comparison of the GreenSeeker NDVI Scanner to NIR Camera System (ASABE AIM 2015)](https://doi.org/10.13031/aim.20152190495); [Low-Cost NDVI Platform for Land Operation: Passive and Active (Mazeh, El Sahili, Zaraket; IEEE Sensors Letters 2021)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lsens.2021.3112822); [Omnidirectional-vision-based estimation for containment detection of a robotic mower (Yang, Chung, Hutchinson, Johnson, Kise; ICRA 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2015.7140090); [Autonomous Boundary Detection Using Image Recognition for Robotic Lawn Mower (ICMT 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icmt56556.2022.9997764); [Ardumower open-source robotic mower firmware (capacitive front/back 'lawn sensor')](https://github.com/Ardumower/ardumower); [WORX Landroid Vision product page](https://au.worx.com/landroid/landroid-vision/)

### P5.v1 (round 1, median 4.83): Three Hall sensors vs. an AGV magnetic guide sensor: tape guidance on rebar-reinforced driveways

- **Question:** Can a ~$10 three-element gradiometric Hall array, fitted to an analytic model of the tape's field, track magnetic guide tape with lateral error equal to a commercial multi-channel AGV magnetic guide sensor on wood, asphalt and steel-reinforced concrete?
- **Hypothesis:** On non-magnetic floors both sensors will reach ≤2 mm RMS lateral error. On rebar- or mesh-reinforced concrete, the commercial sensor will show a site-dependent bias of ≥3 mm, while the model-fit gradiometer stays at ≤2 mm. The hypothesis is falsified if the commercial sensor is unaffected by rebar, or if the cheap array is worse by more than 1 mm RMS on any surface.
- **Measurements:** (1) Bench/static: a carriage on a ruler-indexed rail sweeps lateral offsets of -50 to +50 mm in 1 mm steps at 3 sensor heights, on 3 surfaces x 5 locations each (about 1,500 readings per sensor). Metrics: RMS error, bias and linear range (mm). (2) Closed loop: 20 runs per sensor along a 15 m driveway route laid on asphalt and on reinforced concrete. A downward marker traces the path, and its lateral offset from the tape is measured at 0.5 m stations (about 600 points per sensor). Analysis: Bland-Altman agreement, a mixed model (sensor x surface, location random) and bootstrap CIs. Rebar locations are mapped beforehand by scanning with the array itself.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** A commercial AGV magnetic guide sensor (Roboteq MGS1600-class), bought used or requested as a loan or academic sample. Its price and specs are UNVERIFIED (vendor pages were unreachable this session) and must be confirmed before committing. Also published: the PATH magnetic tape/marker characterization (ACC 2000).
- **Novelty claim:** Insight: the field of a magnetized strip is a known closed-form function of lateral offset and height. Three sensors are therefore enough to solve offset, height and background together, and the spatial gradient between sensors rejects the Earth's field and slowly varying rebar fields, which a dense threshold-and-centroid sensor misreads as offset. Why it is not foregone: rebar and mesh fields vary over the same few-cm scale as the tape's own field, so the gradiometer may not reject them, and the commercial sensor may already compensate. The result could favor either sensor, or show both biased. This is the weakest of the five on novelty: magnetic guidance and Hall-array position estimation are well established (see prior work). The defensible new contribution is the outdoor rebar head-to-head at about 1/30th of the cost (if the commercial price is confirmed). Patent and Scholar checks could not be run this session.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Characterization of magnetic tape and magnetic markers as a position sensing system for vehicle guidance and control (ACC 2000)](https://doi.org/10.1109/acc.2000.878780); [Indirect Measurement of a Bar Magnet Position Using a Hall Sensor Array (IEEE Trans. Magnetics, 2007)](https://doi.org/10.1109/tmag.2007.893632); [Boundary Setting Using Permanent Magnets for Robotic Lawn Mower (ISIE 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/isie62713.2025.11124814)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: a rail jig with ruler indexing and height shims; sensor mounts for both sensors on the existing hoverboard rover; a tape-laying fixture; a downward marker for path tracing. Software student: ESP32 sampling of 3 (optionally 8) linear Hall sensors; least-squares fit of the strip field model with a background term; a reader for the commercial sensor's serial/CAN output; a line-following controller usable with either sensor; analysis.
- **Cost:** $930: Linear Hall sensors x8 and a small PCB ~$15 (estimate). ESP32 $19.95 (verified). Magnetic guide tape, about 15 m, ~$60-150 (UNVERIFIED). Rail jig and mounts ~$40 (estimate). Rover ~$260 (hoverboard $139 verified + frame ~$100 estimate). Commercial guide sensor ~$400-600 (UNVERIFIED estimate; FLAG, confirm the price in October or obtain a loan). Total about $800-1,100. Cost ratio: about $35 of sensing vs. the commercial sensor, about 10-20x if the estimate holds.
- **Venue:** IEEE CASE 2027 (Mar 1). Guidance infrastructure and the cost of automation fit CASE's scope. It is less suited to IROS.
- **Main risk:** The commercial sensor may be unaffordable or unobtainable, and outdoor tape may not survive tires and weather. Fallback: compare against the vendor's published specs plus an in-house dense 8-channel 'commercial-style' threshold-and-centroid array, and test tape durability as a secondary result. Because of its weaker novelty, rank this idea last.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 4 / F 6.5 / I 4.5 = 5.00. *Novelty:* The building blocks are long established: magnetometer and Hall lateral sensing of tape and markers (ACC 2000), background-field cancellation for magnetometer-guided vehicles (Ryoo 2003, doi 10.1109/icsens.2003.1278894), and home-built AGV magnetic sensors (AMM 2015; CAC 2018; IAEAC 2025). Model fitting with three sensors is a textbook dipole/strip inverse problem. The only new piece is a rebar-surface head-to-head against one commercial unit, and that is a thin product benchmark, not a new mechanism or a new trade-off. *Feasibility:* The cheap side is easy: an ESP32, linear Hall sensors, a rail jig and a least-squares fit, all well within the team's skills. The critical path, though, is a commercial sensor whose price and availability I also could not verify (roboteq.com returned 502 twice). The total is already about $930, at the budget flag. The closed-loop metric (pen trace measured by hand at 0.5 m stations) has a measurement floor of about 1-2 mm, the same size as the 1 mm difference the hypothesis needs to resolve, and closed-loop error will be dominated by controller and rover dynamics rather than sensor accuracy. *Impact:* A CASE main-track reviewer would see a sensor benchmark of one vendor product on one family's driveway, with no compelling application: magnetic tape on a residential driveway is not a realistic deployment, given tires, snow, plowing and UV. Recent similar work sits at small venues (IAEAC 2025, CAC 2018), which points to student-conference or workshop level. A likely null result (the commercial sensor is unaffected by rebar) would weaken it further. *Main criticism:* The physics probably makes the headline hypothesis false or trivial. Tape fields at 20-30 mm height are tens of gauss, while induced rebar or mesh anomalies under 50+ mm of concrete are typically sub-gauss to a few gauss (my estimate, not measured), so a threshold-and-centroid commercial sensor is likely to show little bias. Residential driveways are often unreinforced or carry only wire mesh. The outcome is then either a null result or a benchmark of one product, both thin, and neither addresses an application anyone would deploy: outdoor tape on a home driveway. The novelty rests on a comparison, not on a new mechanism or a new question. *Closest prior work:* [Characterization of magnetic tape and magnetic markers as a position sensing system for vehicle guidance and control (ACC 2000), cited by the proposal](https://doi.org/10.1109/acc.2000.878780); [Cancellation of background field using magnetic compass sensor for magnetometer based autonomous vehicle (Ryoo, IEEE Sensors 2003)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icsens.2003.1278894); [Position Estimation of Vehicle Based on Magnetic Marker: Time-Division Position Correction (Byun, Sensors 2021)](https://doi.org/10.3390/s21248274); [Magnetic Locating AGV Navigation Based on Kalman Filter and PID Control (CAC 2018)](https://doi.org/10.1109/cac.2018.8623691); [Research on an Autonomous Navigation AGV Based on Magnetic Navigation Sensors (IEEE IAEAC 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iaeac65194.2025.11166451); [AGV Magnetic Sensor Data Acquisition System (Applied Mechanics and Materials, 2015)](https://doi.org/10.4028/www.scientific.net/amm.713-715.1056); [Using low cost magnetic sensors on magnetically hostile land vehicles (IEEE PLANS 1988)](https://doi.org/10.1109/plans.1988.195463); [Boundary Setting Using Permanent Magnets for Robotic Lawn Mower (Arai, Yokota, Matsumoto, Chugo, Hashimoto; ISIE 2025), cited by the proposal](https://doi.org/10.1109/isie62713.2025.11124814)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 4 / F 6.5 / I 4 = 4.83. *Novelty:* Magnetic-tape lateral sensing outdoors has been characterized since at least PATH/ACC 2000 (Chan et al., DOI confirmed via Crossref). Fitting a magnet's position from a Hall array is a known lab technique (the proposal's own TMAG 2007 citation). Differential rejection of background fields is, as I recall it (UNVERIFIED), already in deployed road-marker products. The only new part is a rebar head-to-head on a home driveway, and physics suggests that comparison will most likely come out null. *Feasibility:* The bench rail, the ESP32 with linear Hall sensors and a least-squares fit are well within the team's skills, and the closed-loop test (40 runs, about 600 marker points) is doable in a few weekends. The binding risks are four. (1) Getting the baseline: Roboteq pages were unreachable, so the price is still UNVERIFIED, and a roughly $500 sensor pushes the total to about $930, near the cap. (2) AGV tape is an indoor product and may not stay stuck on asphalt or survive tires through Jan-Feb weather. (3) Outdoor temperature swings cause ratiometric and offset drift in hobby linear Hall sensors. (4) The fallback (vendor specs plus a home-made 'commercial-style' array) is a strawman that would gut the paper. *Impact:* The most likely outcome is that neither sensor is biased by rebar. That leaves the paper as 'a $10 Hall array matches a commercial guide sensor on a driveway', which is a SoutheastCon or student-session result, not CASE main track. Magnetic tape on a residential driveway also has weak motivation: CASE reviewers will ask who would tape a driveway, and the earlier review round already criticized the GRAD tape-line bin robot as an infrastructure crutch. *Main criticism:* The central hypothesis is probably physically false, so the paper's one new contribution most likely disappears. AGV guide tape produces a field at the sensor (roughly 1 mT order at 20-30 mm, my estimate, UNVERIFIED) far larger than the slowly varying field from rebar or mesh buried 50-75 mm deep, and residential slabs often use mesh or fiber rather than rebar. Any decent commercial sensor will then show no rebar bias, and the result collapses to 'cheap Hall array ≈ commercial sensor on a flat surface'. That result is known from hobby and industrial practice and from PATH-era work, and the cost comparison is inflated by choosing the priciest baseline and ignoring the cheaper finished sensors that probably exist (UNVERIFIED). *Closest prior work:* [Chan, Bougler, Nelson, Kretz, Tan, Zhang: Characterization of magnetic tape and magnetic markers as a position sensing system for vehicle guidance and control (ACC 2000)](https://doi.org/10.1109/acc.2000.878780); [Roboteq MGS1600-class magnetic guide sensors (commercial product)](); [Low-cost Chinese 8/16-channel AGV magnetic navigation sensors (generic products)](); [Aichi Steel magnetic marker / GMPS road guidance system (product)]()
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 3.5 / F 6.5 / I 4 = 4.67. *Novelty:* Each piece has been done before. PATH characterized tape and marker fields for vehicle guidance (ACC 2000). Model-fitting a magnet's position from a Hall array is in IEEE TMAG 2007. A Crossref query turns up many DIY magnetic-sensor AGV papers (AMM 2015, CAC 2018, IAEAC 2025). That leaves the outdoor rebar head-to-head as the only new axis, and physics suggests it will be a null effect, not a finding. This is my inference: the tape field at 10-30 mm height is plausibly on the order of mT, while rebar under at least 50 mm of concrete cover disturbs the background field by roughly tens of µT. So 'cheap array equals commercial sensor' is the expected and foreseeable result, which is the kind of outcome the earlier 25-reviewer round penalized. *Feasibility:* The sensing hardware is trivial: about $15 of linear Hall sensors, an ESP32 (preferably with an external ADC such as an ADS1115, because the ESP32's own ADC is noisy and nonlinear), and a ruler-indexed rail jig. Bench sweeps can be done in a few weekends. The binding risk is getting the commercial baseline: its price is UNVERIFIED (my searches and the vendor page were rate-limited or returned 502), a loan is uncertain, and without it the head-to-head collapses. Smaller risks: rebar 'mapping with the array itself' is circular, many residential driveways are unreinforced or only mesh-reinforced, and tape on asphalt may not survive tires and heat. *Impact:* A CASE main-track acceptance is unlikely. The likely result is 'both sensors reach about 1-2 mm and rebar does not matter', which reads as a replication. The application (magnetic tape on a residential driveway) also has weak motivation next to a painted line or a camera follower. The realistic outlet is SoutheastCon, a student venue or a CASE WIP paper. It would be a respectable but common application story. *Main criticism:* The headline effect (a ≥3 mm rebar-induced bias in the commercial sensor) probably does not exist at a measurable size: the tape's mT-scale field should swamp µT-scale rebar perturbations under normal concrete cover (my estimate, not yet measured). That leaves a 'cheap Hall array equals commercial Hall array on a flat floor' result. Commercial guide sensors are themselves Hall arrays on a microcontroller, and DIY versions already appear in the AGV literature, so this is the foreseeable 'cheaper re-implementation' the earlier review round rejected, set in an application (tape on a home driveway) that few people need. *Closest prior work:* [Characterization of magnetic tape and magnetic markers as a position sensing system for vehicle guidance and control (Chan, Bougler, Nelson, Kretz, Tan, Zhang; ACC 2000)](https://doi.org/10.1109/acc.2000.878780); [AGV Magnetic Sensor Data Acquisition System (Applied Mechanics and Materials, 2015)](https://doi.org/10.4028/www.scientific.net/amm.713-715.1056); [Magnetic Locating AGV Navigation Based on Kalman Filter and PID Control (Chinese Automation Congress 2018)](https://doi.org/10.1109/cac.2018.8623691); [Research on an Autonomous Navigation AGV Based on Magnetic Navigation Sensors (IEEE IAEAC 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iaeac65194.2025.11166451); [Indirect Measurement of a Bar Magnet Position Using a Hall Sensor Array (IEEE Trans. Magnetics, 2007)](https://doi.org/10.1109/tmag.2007.893632); [Single-track magnetic tape absolute position sensor with self-adaptivity (Preprints.org, 2024)](https://doi.org/10.20944/preprints202406.1142.v1); [Boundary Setting Using Permanent Magnets for Robotic Lawn Mower (ISIE 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/isie62713.2025.11124814)

### R4.v1 (round 1, median 4.17): OK-Robot on a $482 LeKiwi: closed-loop wrist-camera grasp correction to offset a cheap arm

- **Question:** How much of OK-Robot's published open-vocabulary pick-and-drop success (58.5% across 10 homes, 82% in uncluttered homes, on a Hello Robot Stretch) survives on a $482 LeKiwi mobile manipulator? Does a closed-loop wrist-camera correction before closing the gripper recover the grasping share of that gap?
- **Hypothesis:** A faithful open-loop OK-Robot port on LeKiwi will reach <=40% end-to-end success in the team's homes, with most new failures from grasp misplacement caused by servo-arm inaccuracy rather than detection or navigation. Adding a final-approach correction that re-detects the queried object's mask in the wrist camera and servos its centroid to the gripper's centre will raise success by >=15 points and reach the paper's 58.5%. Falsified if the failures are dominated by navigation or semantic memory, or if the correction adds less than 5 points.
- **Measurements:** 80 pick-and-drop tasks (40 objects x 2 placements) across 2 homes, each attempted with both grasp modes in randomized order: a paired design analysed with McNemar's test. With about 25% discordant pairs, a 15-20 point gain is detectable at roughly 80% power (estimate). Failures are categorized with OK-Robot's taxonomy (semantic memory / navigation / grasp / drop). Continuous metric: lateral grasp offset in mm, measured from the wrist image at closure. Results are reported with Wilson CIs against the paper's 58.5% / 82% and broken down by clutter level. No human subjects.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Liu, Orru, Vakil, Paxton, Shafiullah, Pinto, 'OK-Robot: What Really Matters in Integrating Open-Knowledge Models for Robotics', RSS 2024 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.12202, code github.com/ok-robot/ok-robot): 58.5% in 10 homes, 82% in cleaner homes. Robot: Hello Robot Stretch (the current Stretch 4 lists at $29,950 on hello-robot.com; the model and price OK-Robot used are UNVERIFIED). In-house baseline: the faithful open-loop port on the same LeKiwi, which makes the comparison hardware-matched.
- **Novelty claim:** The one insight: on a cheap arm the error budget moves from perception to actuation. A last-centimetre visual correction, using the same open-vocabulary mask already computed, converts an expensive-arm assumption (open-loop grasp from a single pre-grasp view) into a cheap-arm-tolerant one. The outcome can go either way. LeKiwi's base odometry and low camera viewpoint may make navigation or memory the dominant failure instead, which would also be a useful finding about where cost cuts hurt. HomeRobot (arXiv 2306.11565) calls the Stretch 'low-cost' and reports 20% real-world success, so this study is about 60x cheaper again. An arXiv search for low-cost open-vocabulary mobile manipulation found only HomeRobot. LeKiwi's repo describes teleop and data collection, not OK-Robot. Scholar/GitHub forks of OK-Robot were NOT searched (budget exhausted), and the team must check them.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [OK-Robot: What Really Matters in Integrating Open-Knowledge Models for Robotics (RSS 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.12202); [HomeRobot: Open-Vocabulary Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.11565); [LeKiwi low-cost mobile manipulator (SIGRobotics UIUC), BOM $482 (12V)](https://github.com/SIGRobotics-UIUC/LeKiwi)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: build the LeKiwi 12V with an SO-101 arm, design compliant printed finger pads and a rigid wrist-camera mount, calibrate the arm, and set up the object set and drop bins. Software student: port the OK-Robot pipeline (navigation and VoxelMap on the laptop GPU, robot client on the Pi 5), replace Stretch-specific motion with LeKiwi kinematics, implement the wrist-mask servo (Lang-SAM/OWL mask in the wrist image, proportional correction, then close), and log failures by category.
- **Cost:** $560: LeKiwi 12V full BOM $482 (verified on its BOM page; excludes 3D printing). Filament ~$25 (estimate). Wrist camera spare and finger pads ~$20 (estimate). Object set ~$30 (household items). Laptop with NVIDIA GPU assumed owned ($0); FLAG: if not owned, a used GPU laptop (~$800+, estimate) would push the study over $1,000. The OK-Robot home scan used an iPhone (borrow one; model requirements UNVERIFIED). Reference Stretch 4 $29,950 not bought, about 60x.
- **Venue:** IEEE/RSJ IROS 2027: mobile manipulation and reproducibility of foundation-model systems are core IROS topics. An ICRA 2027 workshop on low-cost or open-source manipulation is a good companion.
- **Main risk:** Software integration load: OK-Robot depends on several large models and a commercially licensed grasp module (AnyGrasp, license terms UNVERIFIED), which is a lot for one software student. Fallback: replace AnyGrasp with a simple top-down grasp at the mask centroid in BOTH conditions, keeping the open-loop vs closed-loop comparison intact, and narrow the task to pick-and-drop of rigid objects into a bin.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 4.5 / F 3.5 / I 5 = 4.33. *Novelty:* The mechanism is not new. Hello Robot's stretch_ai, the direct successor of OK-Robot, already ships the exact proposed correction: re-detect the mask in the gripper camera, servo its centroid to the image centre, then close. It also keeps an open_loop alternative, as I verified in grasp_object.py. What remains is a cheaper re-implementation plus a failure-taxonomy breakdown on a $482 robot. I found no paper that measures OK-Robot-style success on sub-$1k hardware, so the differentiation is real but thin. The comparison against the published 58.5% is also confounded, because the homes, objects and queries all differ. *Feasibility:* The port is much harder than the proposal admits. OK-Robot's grasping (AnyGrasp) needs RGB-D point clouds, but per the LeRobot docs LeKiwi has only USB RGB cameras. AnyGrasp's 6-DOF grasps often cannot be reached by a 5-DOF SO-101. The navigation stack needs an iPhone Pro with LiDAR plus Record3D, and it needs robot localization against that scan, while LeKiwi has only servo-wheel odometry and no lidar. That is three large integration problems: depth, localization, and IK/grasp reachability. One part-time high-school software student would face them in about 12 weeks, before running 160 trials across 2 homes. *Impact:* A clean 'where does the error budget go when the robot costs 60x less' study would interest the LeRobot and low-cost-manipulation community and fits an ICRA workshop, ARSO or SoutheastCon. Main-track IROS is unlikely for three reasons: the correction already exists in stretch_ai, the gap to the published number is not controlled, and the likely result is a heavily modified pipeline (top-down grasps, a different localization) rather than a faithful OK-Robot. It is a good application story if it works end to end. *Main criticism:* The 'one insight' (wrist-camera mask-centroid servoing before closing) is already implemented, with an open-loop switch, in stretch_ai, the OK-Robot group's own follow-up code. Meanwhile the hard part the proposal hides, getting a faithful OK-Robot port running on a depth-less, lidar-less, 5-DOF $482 robot, is probably beyond one part-time student in 12 weeks. The likely outcome is a modified pipeline whose success rate cannot be fairly compared with the published 58.5%. *Closest prior work:* [Stretch AI (hello-robot/stretch_ai), the open-source successor to OK-Robot/DynaMem from the same group (Paxton is an OK-Robot author), grasp_object.py](https://github.com/hello-robot/stretch_ai/blob/main/src/stretch/agent/operations/grasp_object.py); [OK-Robot: What Really Matters in Integrating Open-Knowledge Models for Robotics (RSS 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.12202); [HomeRobot: Open-Vocabulary Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.11565); [XLeRobot: dual-arm mobile home robot built on LeKiwi/SO-101, from $660](https://github.com/Vector-Wangel/XLeRobot); [AhaRobot: A Low-Cost Open-Source Bimanual Mobile Manipulator for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10070); [LeKiwi (SIGRobotics-UIUC) repo and BOM; LeRobot LeKiwi docs](https://github.com/SIGRobotics-UIUC/LeKiwi/blob/main/BOM.md)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 4 / F 3.5 / I 5 = 4.17. *Novelty:* The 'one insight', re-detecting the queried object's mask in the wrist camera and servoing it to the gripper centre before closing, is already implemented with an open-loop/servo toggle in Hello Robot's stretch_ai (grasp_object.py visual_servo_to_object vs grasp_open_loop.py), which is the direct successor to OK-Robot. Sub-$1k LeKiwi/SO-101 home mobile manipulators (XLeRobot, $660) already exist. What is left is a modest new measurement: OK-Robot-style success and a failure breakdown on a ~$500 robot. I found nothing published that reports this (no LeKiwi or SO-101 OK-Robot fork among the 44 forks; arXiv returned only HomeRobot). *Feasibility:* A 'faithful port' is not possible on this hardware. The OK-Robot README requires an iPhone Pro LiDAR scan through Record3D, lidar-based Hector SLAM on the Stretch, and an AnyGrasp license. AnyGrasp is a point-cloud grasp model (that it needs depth input is my inference). The LeKiwi README lists only RGB cameras, with no depth and no lidar. The team would therefore be building new localization and grasp-pose estimation for a servo-odometry omni base, not porting code. On top of that comes 160 real trials by mid-February, done part-time by one software student. Also an inference I did not measure: an SO-101 on a low base probably cannot reach the table and counter heights used in OK-Robot's homes, so the task set would quietly change. *Impact:* An honest 'what survives on a 60x cheaper robot' error budget would fit ARSO, SoutheastCon or an ICRA low-cost-manipulation workshop, and it makes a good application story. IROS main track is unlikely for three reasons: the correction method already exists in stretch_ai; comparing against the paper's 58.5%/82% is not like for like (different homes, objects, reachable heights and sensors); and the expected result, that cheap servos make grasping worse and closed-loop helps, is foreseeable, the same 'predictable comparison' criticism raised in earlier rounds. *Main criticism:* The claimed contribution, closed-loop wrist-mask servoing to offset open-loop grasp error, is already shipped by the OK-Robot authors' own successor stack (hello-robot/stretch_ai, with both servo and open-loop grasp modes). The 'faithful port' baseline also cannot exist on LeKiwi, which has no depth camera or lidar while OK-Robot depends on both. The study would end up comparing a homemade stack, on different tasks and in different homes, against the paper's 58.5%, and then 'discovering' a technique that already exists. *Closest prior work:* [Hello Robot stretch_ai (the official successor codebase built on OK-Robot's robot controller): src/stretch/agent/operations/grasp_object.py, with servo_to_grasp, plus grasp_open_loop.py](https://github.com/hello-robot/stretch_ai); [OK-Robot code repository (hardware and software requirements)](https://github.com/ok-robot/ok-robot); [OK-Robot: What Really Matters in Integrating Open-Knowledge Models for Robotics (arXiv 2401.12202)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.12202); [XLeRobot: Practical Dual-Arm Mobile Home Robot for $660 (GitHub, Rice student project, cites itself as a 2025 misc report)](https://github.com/Vector-Wangel/XLeRobot); [HomeRobot: Open-Vocabulary Mobile Manipulation (arXiv 2306.11565)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.11565); [slurm-lab-usc/ok-robot fork: 'adapted version of OK-ROBOT for the Hello Robot Stretch 3'](https://github.com/slurm-lab-usc/ok-robot); [RoboCrew (LLM-agent autonomy for LeKiwi/XLeRobot)](https://github.com/Grigorij-Dudnik/RoboCrew); [LeKiwi low-cost mobile manipulator (SIGRobotics UIUC)](https://github.com/SIGRobotics-UIUC/LeKiwi)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 4 / F 3.5 / I 5 = 4.17. *Novelty:* The 'one insight' is already the default grasp in Hello Robot's open-source Stretch AI, the OK-Robot successor. Its grasp_object.py servos the object mask centre in the end-effector camera under align_x/y thresholds before calling _grasp(), with servo_to_grasp=True. That leaves a cheaper re-implementation plus a gap measurement. No published LeKiwi or SO-101 open-vocabulary pick-and-drop study turned up (arXiv API, Semantic Scholar citations of OK-Robot, GitHub repo search), so a thin but real data-point contribution remains. *Feasibility:* A 'faithful' port is not physically possible as specified. OK-Robot needs AnyGrasp (licensed, and it works on RGB-D point clouds), an iPhone Pro with LiDAR, and a Stretch Dex Wrist (README). The LeKiwi BOM lists RGB USB cameras only. The SO-101 sits on a low base and cannot reach the table and counter objects in OK-Robot's protocol. Adding depth or LiDAR breaks the $482 premise, and a GPU laptop may also be needed. Then one part-time high-school programmer has to integrate VoxelMap, OWL/Lang-SAM, navigation without a lidar, and 160 trials between Jan 16 and Feb 14. My estimate is that this will slip. *Impact:* If it works, an honest 'how much of OK-Robot survives on $500 hardware, and where it breaks' reproducibility study is a good fit for an ICRA workshop on low-cost or open-source manipulation, ARSO or SoutheastCon. IROS main track is unlikely. The fix is prior art (Stretch AI), the task set has to shrink to floor-level objects, and a comparison against a published number from different homes and objects is not a controlled experiment. The application story is solid, since foundation-model robotics is topical. *Main criticism:* The headline contribution, a closed-loop wrist-camera mask-centroid servo before closing the gripper, is already the default grasp routine in Hello Robot's open-source Stretch AI, the direct successor to OK-Robot. Meanwhile the 'faithful open-loop OK-Robot port' cannot exist on LeKiwi: it has no depth for AnyGrasp, no lidar or Dex Wrist, and the arm reach is too low for table and counter objects. So the 58.5%/82% comparison is apples to oranges and the fix is not new. *Closest prior work:* [Hello Robot Stretch AI: open-source successor stack to OK-Robot, with language-directed floor pick-and-place (grasp_object.py)](https://github.com/hello-robot/stretch_ai); [OK-Robot code repository (requirements and roadmap)](https://github.com/ok-robot/ok-robot); [XLeRobot: practical low-cost household dual-arm mobile robot (SO-100/101 arms), from $660](https://github.com/Vector-Wangel/XLeRobot); [AhaRobot: A Low-Cost Open-Source Bimanual Mobile Manipulator for Embodied AI (2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10070); [HomeRobot: Open-Vocabulary Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.11565); [LeKiwi low-cost mobile manipulator (SIGRobotics-UIUC)](https://github.com/SIGRobotics-UIUC/LeKiwi); [trail-club/lekiwi_challenge_2026 (LeKiwi + SO-101 ROS 2 stack with LiDAR, RealSense, navigation and arm control)](https://github.com/trail-club/lekiwi_challenge_2026)

### R2.v2 (round 2, median 5.67): Meters per decision: putting the NoMaD navigation policy on a $15-$78 computer by turning its discarded diffusion samples into a speed governor
