- **Question:** On one low-cost robot with one camera, running fully onboard, is the collision rate of the released NoMaD checkpoint governed by decision spacing d = v/f (meters traveled per policy decision)? And can scaling the robot's speed by the disagreement among NoMaD's own 8 diffusion samples (which the official deployment code computes and then discards) let a much cheaper, slower computer match the collision rate at the authors' 4 Hz / 0.2 m/s operating point without lowering mean speed as much as uniform slowing would?
- **Hypothesis:** H1 (decision-spacing law): with the official executor, collisions per 10 m rise monotonically with d. Speed/rate pairs that share the same d (e.g. 2 Hz at 0.2 m/s and 4 Hz at 0.4 m/s) have collision rates within a factor of 1.5. In a negative-binomial model, adding separate log f and log v terms to log d does not significantly improve fit (likelihood-ratio p > 0.05). Falsified if rate matters beyond d, for example through latency effects. That would also be reported, because it would mean compute must be sized by latency and not by meters per decision. H2 (governor): at f = 1 Hz and 0.5 Hz with v_max = 0.4 m/s, the dispersion governor cuts collisions per 10 m by at least 50% versus the official executor at the same f. It also beats uniform slow-down set to the same mean speed (rate ratio <= 0.7) while keeping mean speed >= 60% of v_max. Falsified if it does not beat matched uniform slow-down. That would mean sample dispersion carries no information about where collisions happen. A Feb 1 offline check tests this first: does dispersion predict a bump within the next 1 m (AUROC)? H3 (cost): with the governor, the cheapest board whose measured rate gives collisions within 1.5x of the 4 Hz reference is a Pi 4 2GB ($67.50) or a Pi Zero 2 W. Without the governor, it is a Pi 5 or better. This is an estimate: the board latencies are unmeasured until the October benchmark.
- **Measurements:** Platform: one LeKiwi base with the fisheye camera named in the NoMaD README, on a raised mast. Collisions are counted by a foam bumper on 4 microswitches. Obstacles are cardboard boxes and household clutter. Courses: (1) a home hallway/room loop and (2) a cluttered garage loop. Both are fixed, photographed and reset before each session. Each run is NoMaD exploration mode for 30 m of odometry distance or 4 min, whichever comes first. A manual reset after a stuck/wedged state counts as an intervention.

Rate emulation: the Pi 5 runs NoMaD (ONNX) in a loop throttled to rate f. It always uses the latest frame from a camera thread running at the camera rate, as the official code does. Each command is applied only after a latency of 1/f, emulating a single-threaded slower board.

Part 1 (law; official executor; 8 cells): f in {4, 2, 1, 0.5} Hz x v in {0.2, 0.4} m/s, or the base's maximum if lower. That gives d from 0.05 to 0.8 m, with three overlapping d values for the collapse test. 2 courses x 4 runs per cell = 64 runs.

Part 2 (fixes; v_max = 0.4; f in {1, 0.5} Hz): (a) odometry-realigned chunk execution: the full 8-waypoint chunk is transformed by the odometry change accumulated during inference, and expired waypoints are dropped (Mobile UMI/RTC-style baseline); (b) uniform slow-down set to the governor's measured mean speed; (c) the dispersion governor, v = v_max x clip(1 - k x s), where s is the spread of waypoints 1-3 across the 8 samples and k is tuned once on Part-1 logs; (d) governor plus realignment. 4 executors x 2 rates x 2 courses x 4 runs = 64 runs. The official-executor cells at these rates are reused from Part 1.

Part 3 (real latency): the governor running natively on a Pi Zero 2 W (or Pi 4, if the Zero cannot hold the model) mounted on the robot, 2 courses x 4 runs = 8 runs. This checks that the emulation matches real hardware.

Bench: per-inference latency (median/p95 over 500 recorded frames) on Pi Zero 2 W, Pi 4 2GB and Pi 5 2GB, at samples {1, 4, 8} x denoising steps {10, 5}, fp32 vs int8.

Metrics: collisions per 10 m (primary), interventions per 10 m, mean speed, and distance before the first collision. Total about 136 runs at about 4 min each, plus resets: roughly 12-15 robot-hours.

Analysis: negative-binomial GLM of collision counts with a log-distance offset and course as a fixed effect. For H1, compare log d alone against log f + log v by likelihood ratio and AIC. For H2, report rate ratios with 95% CIs and a Pareto plot of collisions per 10 m against mean speed. For the Feb 1 gate, AUROC of dispersion predicting a bump within the next 1 m on Part-1 logs. The final figure, collisions per 10 m against the board price needed to reach each rate, comes from combining the bench latencies with the Part 1/2 curves.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** (1) The within-study reference is the authors' own operating point: 4 Hz, max_v 0.2 m/s, waypoint index 2 scaled by MAX_V/RATE, as set in the official robot.yaml and explore.py (both fetched). It runs on the same robot and camera, so camera and embodiment shift do not affect the comparison. (2) Mitigation baselines on the robot: odometry-realigned chunk execution (the latency fix from Mobile UMI, arXiv 2605.20894, and RTC, arXiv 2506.07339, adapted to waypoints) and uniform slow-down at matched mean speed. (3) Hardware reference for the cost table: the README names a LoCoBot with a Jetson Orin Nano (prices UNVERIFIED; not bought). The official config caps the loop at 4 Hz, so an Orin buys nothing beyond what a 4 Hz-capable board provides. The paper's reported results (98% exploration success, 0.2 collisions per experiment) appear only as context. They were measured with different metrics, robot and environments, so they are not a target. Guerrier et al. (arXiv 2603.25937) are cited for the known off-platform collision problem.
- **Novelty claim:** (a) No published decision-rate or decision-spacing sweep for a navigation foundation model exists. Guerrier et al. 2026 re-evaluate GNM/ViNT/NoMaD on Jetson-class hardware without studying rate. DevGRU (2608.18470) reports only inference time for a new model. NoMaD itself reports no rate study. (b) The single technical idea: the official NoMaD deployment computes 8 diffusion trajectories and uses only naction[0] (verified in explore.py). The spread among those trajectories is a free, deployment-time uncertainty signal. It can turn low compute rate into locally lower speed, keeping meters per decision small only where the policy is unsure. Closest prior uses of uncertainty differ:
- Kahn et al. 2017 slow an RL robot using bootstrap/dropout ensembles, which add compute and serve safe training, not a compute trade.
- Punyamoorty et al. 2024 use diffusion uncertainty to change how often replanning happens, not speed, and not on cheap hardware.
- Mobile UMI 2026 and RTC 2025 handle latency by realigning or asynchronously executing chunks, without uncertainty. Both are tested here as baselines.
(c) The test against uniform slow-down at matched mean speed is the control that separates 'uncertainty is informative' from 'slower is safer'.
Searches run: arXiv API queries for NoMaD + navigation (12 hits, none on rate or embedded compute), diffusion + navigation + uncertainty + samples, diffusion + navigation + velocity + variance/disagreement (no paper scaling velocity by sample disagreement), and navigation + inference latency + policy. Google Scholar, IEEE Xplore and GitHub were NOT searched in this revision (web-search budget exhausted). The team should run those searches before writing, especially for 'uncertainty-aware speed' together with 'diffusion policy'.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [NoMaD: Goal Masked Diffusion Policies for Navigation and Exploration (Sridhar, Shah, Glossop, Levine)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07896); [visualnav-transformer official deployment code (explore.py) and config (robot.yaml)](https://github.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer); [Can Vision Foundation Models Navigate? Zero-Shot Real-World Evaluation and Lessons Learned (Guerrier, Soma, Pavlasek, Beltrame, 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25937); [Real-Time Execution of Action Chunking Flow Policies (RTC, Black et al., 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07339); [Mobile UMI: Cross-View Diffusion Policy with Decoupled Kinematics for Mobile Manipulation (2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20894); [Dynamic Obstacle Avoidance through Uncertainty-Based Adaptive Planning with Diffusion (Punyamoorty et al., 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.16950); [Uncertainty-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Collision Avoidance (Kahn, Villaflor, Pong, Abbeel, Levine, 2017)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1702.01182); [DevGRU: Depth-guided Visual Navigation using a Collision-aware Recurrent Model (2026; arXiv listing summary only)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18470); [LeKiwi low-cost mobile base BOM (SIGRobotics-UIUC)](https://github.com/SIGRobotics-UIUC/LeKiwi/blob/main/BOM.md); [RaGNNarok (IROS 2025; listed in venues.md)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.00937)
- **Build:** Mechanical student: build the LeKiwi base-only (3D-printed shell, 3 omni wheels, STS3215 servos). Add a raised camera mast carrying the README-specified fisheye camera and a foam bumper ring on 4 microswitches wired to Pi GPIO, as an objective collision counter. Add swappable mounts so the Pi 5, Pi 4 or Pi Zero 2 W can ride onboard. Set up and photograph two reproducible obstacle courses using cardboard boxes, and mark the start poses on the floor.

Software student: export the released NoMaD checkpoint to ONNX (encoder and denoiser separately) and write a ROS-free Python runner. The camera thread fills the context queue at the camera rate, as in explore.py. The inference loop throttles to f and injects latency. Implement the executors: official, odometry-realigned chunk, uniform slow-down, dispersion governor, and governor + realign. Odometry comes from servo encoders. Also write the latency bench harness, a per-run logger (frames, 8 samples, commands, bumper events, odometry) and the GLM/AUROC analysis notebooks.
- **Cost:** $580: - LeKiwi base-only 12V BOM: $251.50 (verified on BOM.md; excludes Pi, cameras and printing).
- PLA filament for printed parts: ~$25 (estimate).
- Raspberry Pi 5 2GB: $77.50 (verified on the Raspberry Pi Oct 1, 2026 price-increase post; 2GB should hold the 19M-parameter model, but that is an estimate).
- Raspberry Pi 4 2GB (bench and possible onboard tier): $67.50 (verified, same post).
- Raspberry Pi Zero 2 W: ~$15-20 (UNVERIFIED; the product page shows no price).
- 3 microSD cards: ~$30 (estimate).
- ELP 170-degree fisheye USB camera (model named in the NoMaD README): ~$50 (UNVERIFIED estimate).
- Pi power supply (5V/5A buck or USB-C power bank): ~$40 (estimate; check whether the LeKiwi base wiring already powers the Pi).
- Foam bumper, 4 microswitches and wiring: ~$15 (estimate).
- Cardboard and household obstacles: $0.
- Laptop for ONNX export and analysis: owned, $0.
Total about $575-$580, under the $1,000 flag. Not bought: the reference LoCoBot + Jetson Orin Nano (prices UNVERIFIED). Memory-driven Pi price rises in 2026 could move the Pi lines, so re-check prices at order time.
- **Venue:** IEEE/RSJ IROS 2027 (deadline Mar 1, 2027; fit 8/10 per venues.md). Real-robot learned navigation and deployment are core IROS scope. IROS 2025 accepted a Raspberry Pi 5 navigation paper (RaGNNarok). The result is a figure of collisions per 10 m against board price, with a controlled mechanism test, rather than a deployment report. Companion venue: an ICRA 2027 workshop on foundation models, efficient or edge robot learning, or low-cost robotics (CFPs Feb-Mar 2027), reusing the same paper.
- **Main risk:** The main risk is a floor effect from embodiment shift. NoMaD may collide often on the LeKiwi even at the 4 Hz reference, because of the low base, holonomic servo base and different scenes. Guerrier et al. already report frequent NoMaD collisions on new platforms. Every condition would then look bad and rate effects would be hidden. Mitigations: use the README's own fisheye camera on a raised mast, check with the winter-break pilot gate, and adjust course clutter. If the gate still fails, switch checkpoints to ViNT or GNM in the same repo and executor framework. The question stays the same, only the model changes.

Secondary risks:
1. Sample dispersion may not predict collisions. The Feb 1 AUROC gate catches this. Uniform slow-down and realignment results still give a complete rate-sweep paper, with the governor reported as a negative result.
2. Pi 5 latency may be far above or below expectations. The October bench sets the rate band before any hardware is built.
3. LeKiwi may not reach 0.4 m/s. In that case use v in {0.1, 0.2} m/s, since the design is indexed by d, not by v.
- **Changes from previous version:** 1) Dropped distance-indexed context. The official explore.py already decouples the context buffer from inference, so that premise was a strawman.
2) New research question: a controlled sweep of decision rate f and speed v, run entirely onboard the same robot and camera. It tests whether collisions are governed by decision spacing d = v/f, the meters traveled per decision.
3) New single technical idea: a dispersion speed governor. The robot's speed is scaled by how much the 8 diffusion samples disagree. The official code computes all 8 and discards 7.
4) Added honest baselines tested head-to-head on the robot: the official executor, odometry-realigned chunk execution (Mobile UMI/RTC-style), and uniform slow-down matched on mean speed. The last one is the key control: it shows whether the governor beats simply driving slower.
5) Removed the Wi-Fi laptop condition. Lower rates are emulated by throttling the Pi 5 with a matching injected latency. A real-latency confirmation runs onboard a Pi Zero 2 W.
6) Added a bench latency benchmark across compute tiers (Pi Zero 2 W, Pi 4 2GB, Pi 5 2GB) at 1/4/8 samples and 10/5 denoising steps, so each rate becomes a dollar point.
7) Reduced embodiment shift: the camera is the fisheye model named in the official README, on a raised mast. A winter-break pilot gate at the authors' operating point (4 Hz, 0.2 m/s) catches floor effects. The paper's numbers are now context only, and the within-study 4 Hz condition is the reference.
8) Corrected costs. The LeKiwi base-only total excludes the Pi (verified). The Pi 5 2GB is $77.50 after the Oct 2026 price rise (verified). The reference is the LoCoBot + Jetson Orin Nano per the README, price UNVERIFIED. Total about $580.
9) Primary metric is now collisions per 10 m from bumper switches, analyzed with a negative-binomial GLM. A collapse test checks whether d alone explains collisions, and a Pareto comparison plots collisions against mean speed.
10) New timeline gates: an October latency benchmark, a pilot floor-effect gate in winter break, and a Feb 1 check on whether dispersion predicts collisions (AUROC).
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 6 / I 5.5 = 5.50. *Novelty:* H1 is weak as a contribution. The official executor multiplies the waypoint by MAX_V/RATE, so per-decision step = d is built into the code. Latency-vs-safe-speed is classical (from memory, UNVERIFIED here: Falanga, Kim and Scaramuzza, 'How Fast is Too Fast?', RA-L 2019). LNN-Fly (arXiv 2606.28827, IROS 2026) already tests a learned navigation policy under reduced control frequency on a real robot. The governor (using the spread of NoMaD's already-computed 8 samples to set speed, tested against uniform slow-down at matched mean speed) is a real but thin twist on Kahn 2017 and Punyamoorty 2024. I found no paper doing exactly this, but my academic search was limited: the web-search budget was exhausted and arXiv/Semantic Scholar APIs returned 429 errors. *Feasibility:* The parts are off the shelf and cost is about $580, but there are several stacked risks: a ROS-free ONNX export of NoMaD's EfficientNet encoder plus diffusion UNet, a LeKiwi build plus bumper, a likely floor effect (Guerrier et al. already report frequent off-platform collisions), and an uncertain 0.4 m/s top speed on STS3215 servos. A Pi Zero 2 W has 512 MB RAM and will probably not hold the model plus onnxruntime at any useful rate. On top of that come 136 four-minute runs (12-15 robot-hours plus resets) squeezed into January-February weekends of a school term. The emulation design and the Feb 1 gate are good mitigations, but slip is likely. *Impact:* A clean negative-binomial rate sweep plus a matched-speed governor control would make a respectable ICRA-workshop or ARSO-level paper and a good application story. IROS main track is unlikely: H1 is partly true by construction, LNN-Fly already covers rate-robustness at IROS, and the cost story is weak. The 4 Hz reference likely already runs on a $77.50 Pi 5, so the 'much cheaper' delta is Pi 5 vs Pi 4, not Jetson vs Pi. *Main criticism:* Both hypotheses are compromised before any data is collected.
- H1 is largely true by construction. The official executor converts the chosen waypoint to motion by scaling with MAX_V/RATE, so 'meters per decision' is literally the executor's step size. 'Slower reaction means a larger collision radius' is the classical latency/stopping-distance result, and LNN-Fly (IROS 2026) already sweeps control rate for a learned navigation policy on a real robot.
- H2's signal is confounded. In exploration mode NoMaD is goal-masked and trained to emit deliberately diverse samples (e.g., left vs right at a junction or in open space). Spread across the 8 samples will therefore mostly measure route multimodality, not collision risk. The governor may slow the robot in open areas and run full speed in tight corridors where samples agree, making a low AUROC and a failed comparison against uniform slow-down the most likely outcome. *Closest prior work:* [LNN-Fly: Continuous-Time UAV Navigation for Robust Obstacle Avoidance under Timing Mismatch (Huang et al., accepted IROS 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28827); [NoMaD: Goal Masked Diffusion Policies for Navigation and Exploration (Sridhar et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07896); [visualnav-transformer official deployment code (explore.py, robot.yaml)](https://github.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer); [Mobile UMI: Cross-View Diffusion Policy with Decoupled Kinematics for Mobile Manipulation (Huang, Dong, Dong, 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20894); [Real-Time Execution of Action Chunking Flow Policies (Black et al., 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07339); [Dynamic Obstacle Avoidance through Uncertainty-Based Adaptive Planning with Diffusion (Punyamoorty et al., 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.16950); [Uncertainty-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Collision Avoidance (Kahn et al., 2017)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1702.01182); [Can Vision Foundation Models Navigate? Zero-Shot Real-World Evaluation and Lessons Learned (Guerrier et al., 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25937)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 6 / F 6 / I 5.5 = 5.83. *Novelty:* I found no published decision-rate sweep for NoMaD and no paper that scales speed by diffusion-sample spread, but my search was cut short (search budget exhausted; arXiv and Semantic Scholar returned 429). The governor is close to the speed version of Punyamoorty et al. 2024, which uses diffusion uncertainty to change replanning frequency (arXiv 2409.16950), and Kahn et al. 2017 already slowed a robot under uncertainty. H1 is close to built in, because explore.py scales each command by MAX_V/RATE. That leaves a known idea moved to a cheap-hardware setting, with real but thin differentiation. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and documented: the LeKiwi BOM ($251.50, verified by earlier reviewers), Pis, a bumper and cardboard. The software path is also open: ONNX export of a released 19M-parameter checkpoint. But the plan stacks several unmeasured risks that all have to land before Feb 17: Pi 5 latency with 8 samples x 10 steps, whether LeKiwi can reach 0.4 m/s, NoMaD's documented off-platform collision floor (Guerrier et al.), and an exploration-mode dispersion signal that may not track obstacles. It also needs 136 four-minute runs plus resets over about six weekends. *Impact:* A clean plot of collisions per 10 m against board price for a famous navigation model would suit an ICRA edge/low-cost-robotics workshop, ARSO or SoutheastCon, and it makes a good application story. It is unlikely to get into the IROS main track: it is one robot and two home courses, H1 is close to built in, the governor may come out null, and the 'cheaper' saving is the gap between a Pi and a Jetson-class board on a robot whose base alone is $251. A Jetson price is not verified, but it is a small share of system cost. *Main criticism:* Both hypotheses are weak tests. H1 is close to true by construction: explore.py already scales commands by MAX_V/RATE, and the emulated latency is exactly 1/f, so any deviation would come from speed dynamics and from context-frame spacing (which scales with v) rather than from latency. And with 4 runs per cell, a non-significant likelihood-ratio test is low-power 'accept the null' reasoning, the same equivalence-by-non-significance flaw earlier rounds penalized. The governor's signal is also doubtful in exactly the mode tested. NoMaD's goal-masked exploration samples are deliberately multimodal, so the 8 samples should disagree most at junctions and in open space, and may agree in a narrow, cluttered corridor where only one direction is free. Dispersion could then track openness, not collision risk. In the d = v/f framing it is also the speed version of Punyamoorty et al.'s uncertainty-adaptive replanning frequency. *Closest prior work:* [Dynamic Obstacle Avoidance through Uncertainty-Based Adaptive Planning with Diffusion (Punyamoorty, Jutras-Dube, Zhang, Aggarwal, Conover, Bera, 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.16950); [CARE: Enhancing Safety of Visual Navigation through Collision Avoidance via Repulsive Estimation (Kim, Sim, Kim, Sycara, Nam, 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03834); [visualnav-transformer official deployment code, explore.py](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer/main/deployment/src/explore.py); [NoMaD: Goal Masked Diffusion Policies for Navigation and Exploration (Sridhar, Shah, Glossop, Levine)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07896); [Real-Time Execution of Action Chunking Flow Policies (Black et al., 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07339); [Can Vision Foundation Models Navigate? Zero-Shot Real-World Evaluation (Guerrier, Soma, Pavlasek, Beltrame, 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25937)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 6 / F 5.5 / I 5.5 = 5.67. *Novelty:* I found no paper that sweeps NoMaD's decision rate against speed or turns its discarded 8 diffusion samples into a speed governor on Pi-class boards. arXiv searches for diffusion + navigation + uncertainty + speed and for NoMaD + embedded returned nothing on point. Still, every component is known: uncertainty-scaled caution (Kahn 2017), diffusion uncertainty used to trade compute for safety (Punyamoorty 2024, verified), adaptive speed plus latency scheduling for chunked policies (SAIL 2506.11948), and plug-in NoMaD collision fixes (CARE 2506.03834). H1, that collisions rise with meters per decision, is the kind of foreseeable result round 1 penalized. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and the software path (ONNX export, a ROS-free runner) is plausible. But the experiment matrix rests on v = 0.4 m/s, and the LeKiwi base very likely cannot reach it. LeRobot's lekiwi.py clamps wheel commands at max_raw = 3000 ticks/s with a 0.05 m wheel radius, which is about 0.23 m/s at the wheel by my calculation, and the fastest teleop level is 0.3 m/s. On top of that: 136 manually reset runs, a high prior chance of the embodiment floor effect, and Part 1 starting only days after a winter-break pilot. All of this is tight for two part-time high-school students. *Impact:* An ICRA 2027 workshop on edge or low-cost robot learning is a good fit, and a plot of collisions per 10 m against board price is a memorable result for applications. IROS main track is unlikely. H1 would be 'confirmed' by a likelihood-ratio test failing to reject with only 8 runs per cell, which reviewers will call underpowered acceptance of the null. Without a CARE-style baseline the safety contribution looks incomplete, and a NoMaD floor effect on the LeKiwi could wash out every condition. *Main criticism:* The paper's headline 'law' (H1) is close to foreseeable: blind travel per decision grows as v/f, so collisions rise. It is also tested in a way that cannot support the claim: 'log f + log v does not beat log d at p > 0.05' with 4 runs per cell on 2 courses is absence of evidence, not a collapse. Meanwhile the one new idea (the dispersion governor) gets only a single tuning pass and no comparison with existing NoMaD collision fixes such as CARE. All of this runs on a base that probably cannot reach the 0.4 m/s the design assumes. *Closest prior work:* [CARE: Enhancing Safety of Visual Navigation through Collision Avoidance via Repulsive Estimation (Kim, Sim, Kim, Sycara, Nam, 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03834); [SAIL: Faster-than-Demonstration Execution of Imitation Learning Policies (arXiv 2506.11948)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11948); [Dynamic Obstacle Avoidance through Uncertainty-Based Adaptive Planning with Diffusion (Punyamoorty et al., 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.16950); [Uncertainty-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Collision Avoidance (Kahn et al., 2017)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1702.01182); [FlowNav: Combining Flow Matching and Depth Priors for Efficient Navigation (arXiv 2411.09524)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.09524); [Can Vision Foundation Models Navigate? (Guerrier et al., 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25937); [Mobile UMI (arXiv 2605.20894) and RTC (arXiv 2506.07339)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20894); [visualnav-transformer issues (Jetson Orin deployment questions)](https://github.com/robodhruv/visualnav-transformer/issues?q=raspberry+OR+jetson+OR+latency+OR+fps)

### S2.v2 (round 2, median 5.67): Only see what the wheel can feel: the label floor of a proprioceptively trained $25 camera, and where it reaches parity with a $429 active-stereo camera on 3-25 mm ground steps

- **Question:** Is the smallest ground step that a self-supervised $25 monocular camera learns to detect set by its proprioceptive labeller, with a floor that scales with wheel radius over speed squared, rather than by its optics? And above what step height, range and lighting does that camera match a $429 active-stereo depth camera (OAK-D Pro) on the same 30 cm-high robot, when both use the same learned or geometric method?
- **Hypothesis:** H1 (label-floor mechanism). A wheel of radius r that hits a step of height h at speed v gets a vertical velocity jump of about v*sqrt(2h/r). The detectable floor is therefore h_min ~ (r/2)*(dv_min/v)^2: linear in r, inverse-square in v until tyre compliance dominates.
Prediction: the IMU on the front caster (r about 3-4 cm, estimate) has a 50%-detection floor at least 1.5x lower than hub-motor current on the 6.5-in drive wheel (r about 8.3 cm). For each labeller, the floor falls with speed, with a log-log slope steeper than -1 across 0.3/0.6/1.0 m/s.
Falsified if the caster floor is not lower than the hub floor, or if the slope is shallower than -1. A shallow slope would mean compliance, not the impact geometry, sets the floor.

H2 (inheritance). The same CNN trained on the sensitive labeller (caster IMU at 1.0 m/s) has a camera detection floor (recall >= 0.8 at precision 0.9) at least one height bin lower than when it is trained on the insensitive labeller (hub current at 0.3 m/s). In both cases the camera floor sits within one bin of its labeller's floor.
If the camera floor is far below the labeller floor, appearance generalises below the labels. That would be the more surprising result, and it would be reported either way.

H3 (cost-parity boundary). In shade or overcast at 0.8-1.6 m, the OAK-D Pro plane-fit detector resolves steps of 6 mm or more. Our estimate of per-pixel height noise is sigma_h ~ sigma_d*H*Z/(f*b), about 1-2.5 mm. So the $25 camera does NOT beat it on small steps. It reaches parity (AUC within 0.05, cluster-bootstrap 95% CI) only above a crossover height h*, which we predict at 10-20 mm. Below h*, the depth camera wins.
H3 is falsified in the camera's favour if parity holds at 6 mm or below. It is falsified against the camera if no parity is reached even at 25 mm.
Exploratory: in direct sun on uniform concrete at grazing angles, stereo holes may lower h* (our expectation, UNVERIFIED).
- **Measurements:** Exp 1: label floor and depth floor, calibrated steps. Run in the garage or on the driveway, so weather-proof.
- Steps: stacked 3 mm hardboard sheets, 1.2 m wide, giving 0 (flush control), 3, 6, 9, 12, 18 and 25 mm, each caliper-measured to 0.1 mm.
- Crossings: 7 heights x 3 speeds (0.3, 0.6, 1.0 m/s, closed-loop on hoverboard FOC speed mode) x 8 crossings = 168, step-up and step-down each.
- Logged on every crossing: caster-fork IMU (BNO085), chassis IMU, and hub phase current.
- Metrics per labeller x speed: detection rate vs height, a fitted logistic curve, h50 and h90 in mm with bootstrap CIs, and the log-log slope of h50 vs v. Also the caster/hub floor ratio compared against the measured r ratio.
- Same session, static depth captures: the OAK-D Pro is parked at 0.8, 1.2, 1.6 and 2.0 m from each step, with 60 frames per placement, in shade and in sun (and dusk with IR on/off as a stretch). Metric: OAK step-height estimate error and its SD (mm) vs range, giving the depth floor.

Exp 2: real sites, paired sensors.
- Sites: 6 lip sites (driveway aprons, gutter seams, garage thresholds, lifted slabs; 3-40 mm, caliper-measured) plus 4 negative sites with no height change (expansion joints, painted lines, cracks, a cast shadow).
- Approaches: 12 daytime approaches per site = 120. Stretch goal: dusk, 3 sites x 6 approaches, IR projector on/off.
- Each approach ends in a traversal that auto-labels it. 50 labels are hand-checked against caliper heights.

Five arms, scored on the same frames in the 0.8-2.0 m band:
- (a) Pi Cam 3 + small CNN trained on the self-labels;
- (b) Pi Cam 3 + zero-shot Depth Anything V2 metric depth + the same plane-fit step detector;
- (c) OAK-D Pro depth + plane-fit detector (extended disparity off, 800P);
- (d) OAK depth + the same CNN and labels;
- (e) OAK RGB + the same CNN and labels.
Comparing (a) with (e) isolates optics, (c) with (d) isolates method, and (a) with (b) isolates self-labels vs a foundation model.

Metrics:
- AUC per height bin (<6, 6-12, 12-25, >25 mm, plus negatives);
- detection range at 95% precision (m);
- height-estimation MAE (mm);
- false-alarm rate on negative sites;
- Pi 5 latency per frame (ms).

Analysis:
- leave-one-site-out evaluation;
- cluster bootstrap that resamples approaches within sites, never frames;
- paired AUC differences for each arm vs (c);
- the crossover height h* with CI, plotted as a cost-parity boundary over (height, range, lighting).
Power note (estimate): with about 20 approaches per height bin, the AUC-difference CIs will be roughly +/-0.07-0.10. This is why the claims are framed as boundaries with CIs, not as small-difference tests.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** The expensive sensor is a Luxonis OAK-D Pro active-stereo depth camera, US $429 (https://shop.luxonis.com/products/oak-d-pro, price confirmed by reviewers). Docs (https://docs.luxonis.com/hardware/products/OAK-D%20Pro, opened 2026-10-04) give 1280x800 OV9282 mono sensors, 80 deg HFOV, ideal range ~80 cm-12 m, MinZ ~80 cm at 800P, and below 2% absolute depth error under 4 m. Evaluation is therefore limited to 0.8-2.0 m, so the baseline runs in its rated envelope.

The baseline is run two ways:
- the standard RANSAC ground-plane fit + height-residual step detector;
- the same CNN trained on the same self-labels using OAK depth as input.
The learned-vs-heuristic confound is thereby measured, not hidden. The OAK's own RGB stream also runs the same CNN, which controls for camera optics.

The substitute is a $25 Raspberry Pi Camera Module 3 (https://www.raspberrypi.com/products/camera-module-3/, 'from $25'). It runs either the self-labelled CNN or zero-shot Depth Anything V2 metric depth (NeurIPS 2024, arXiv 2406.09414, metric models released).

The cost ratio of about 17x counts sensors only, and the paper will say so: the OAK computes depth on board, while the camera arms use the shared Pi 5.
- **Novelty claim:** The labelling technique is NOT claimed as new. Back-projecting proprioceptive events to earlier camera frames is established:
- Howard et al. JFR 2006 (near-to-far);
- Stavens & Thrun UAI 2006 (accelerometers train the lasers);
- Wellhausen et al. RA-L 2019 (contact labels to images, legged robot);
- BADGR RA-L 2021 (IMU angular-velocity bumpiness labels on a wheeled robot);
- WayFAST RA-L 2022 (traction self-labels);
- Cross-Modal Supervision 2022 (time-shifted proprioception supervises a monocular camera on curbs and steps up to 20 cm, quadruped).

None of these works, as far as their abstracts and our checks show, does any of the following:
(1) Measure the labeller's own detection floor as a function of the labelling wheel's radius and speed, or test the predicted h_min ~ r/v^2 law. The practical design rule that follows: instrument the smallest, fastest wheel, not the drive wheel.
(2) Test whether a self-supervised camera's detection floor inherits its labeller's floor.
(3) Compare a proprioceptively trained $25 camera against an active-stereo depth camera on the same robot in a design that separates sensor from method (5 arms, including a zero-shot monocular-depth foundation model), on caliper-measured 3-25 mm steps from a 30 cm viewpoint, with flush negative controls.

Related measurements come from different settings:
- Lost and Found (IROS 2016) shows stereo detecting small road hazards from a car.
- Watanabe et al. (2021) detects curbs and gaps from a wheelchair accelerometer, but with no camera and no look-ahead.
- WalkOCC (2026) reports monocular curb IoU of 14.59 on sidewalk robots, with LiDAR-derived labels.

Outcomes can go either way. The camera may generalise below its label floor, and stereo may fail in sun on uniform concrete.

SEARCH LIMIT: the session's WebSearch budget was used up and the arXiv, OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar APIs were rate-limited during this revision. The absence of a label-floor-vs-wheel-radius study is therefore bounded by an incomplete search.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [BADGR: An Autonomous Self-Supervised Learning-Based Navigation System (Kahn, Abbeel, Levine; IEEE RA-L 2021)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.05700); [Learning Visual Locomotion with Cross-Modal Supervision (Loquercio, Kumar, Malik; arXiv 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.03785); [A Self-Supervised Terrain Roughness Estimator for Off-Road Autonomous Driving (Stavens & Thrun; UAI 2006)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1206.6872); [Where Should I Walk? Predicting Terrain Properties From Images Via Self-Supervised Learning (Wellhausen et al.; IEEE RA-L 2019)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2019.2895390); [Towards learned traversability for robot navigation: From underfoot to the far field (Howard et al.; J. Field Robotics 2006)](https://doi.org/10.1002/rob.20168); [WayFAST: Navigation With Predictive Traversability in the Field (Gasparino et al.; IEEE RA-L 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2022.3193464); [Lost and Found: detecting small road hazards for self-driving vehicles (Pinggera et al.; IROS 2016)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.2016.7759186); [Wheelchair Behavior Recognition for Visualizing Sidewalk Accessibility by Deep Neural Networks (Watanabe et al.; IJCAI-PRICAI 2020 workshop)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2101.03724); [Depth Anything V2 (Yang et al.; NeurIPS 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.09414); [Monocular 3D Occupancy Perception for Robots on Sidewalks via Hybrid 2D-3D Learning (WalkOCC; arXiv 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19122)
- **Build:** Mechanical (hardware student):
- Shared hoverboard base robot.
- Rigid mast holding the Pi Cam 3 and OAK-D Pro side by side at about 30 cm, tilted about 15-20 deg down.
- A BNO085 IMU clamped to the front-caster fork, plus a second BNO085 on the chassis.
- A calibrated step set: stacked 3 mm hardboard sheets, 1.2 m wide, with chamfer-free edges, giving 3-25 mm steps.
- A wheel-radius record (caster and drive wheel measured under load).

Software (perception student):
- A single-clock logger on the Pi 5: Pi Cam 3 via libcamera, OAK-D Pro via DepthAI over USB, both IMUs over I2C/SPI, and hoverboard FOC phase current and wheel speed over UART. Everything is timestamped on the Pi clock to avoid a separate hardware-sync problem.
- An event detector: a threshold on caster vertical-accel jerk and on the current spike, tuned on Exp 1 with flush (0 mm) crossings as negatives.
- Odometry back-projection of each event to image rows in frames from 0.8-2.0 m earlier.
- A MobileNet-class patch/row classifier and height regressor, trained on Colab, reused unchanged on the Pi RGB, OAK RGB and OAK depth inputs.
- Depth Anything V2 Small metric, run offline on a laptop plus one timed Pi 5 run.
- A RANSAC ground-plane + height-residual step detector shared by the OAK depth and DA-V2 arms.
- Cluster-bootstrap analysis scripts.
- **Cost:** $860: - Shared base robot: about $270, all estimates (used hoverboard ~$60, Pi 5 ~$80, frame ~$80, wiring/ST-Link ~$50).
- OAK-D Pro: $429 (Luxonis shop, verified by reviewers).
- Raspberry Pi Camera Module 3: $25 ('from $25', Raspberry Pi product page).
- 2x BNO085 IMU: about $59 ($29.50 each, verified in the v1 round).
- Hardboard sheets and clamps for the step set: about $40 (estimate).
- Lux meter: about $20 (estimate).
- Digital calipers: about $20 (estimate).
Total: about $863, rounded to $860, under the $1,000 flag. The sensor comparison is $25 (+$30 IMU, which also serves as the labeller) vs $429.
- **Venue:** Primary: IEEE/RSJ IROS 2027 (Mar 1, 2027 deadline, 8 pages; venues.md fit 8/10). The paper offers a physical law for a self-supervision limit (the label floor), a falsifiable inheritance test, and a fair multi-arm sensor-by-method comparison against active stereo. That is perception-and-learning content suited to IROS, even with a small dataset, because the claims are boundaries with CIs rather than state-of-the-art accuracy.

IEEE CASE 2027 (also Mar 1) fits worse, because it leans to automation and industry.

Fallback: the same paper goes to an ICRA 2027 workshop on field or low-cost robotics (CFPs Feb-Mar 2027, per venues.md). Exp 1 alone can become a CASE WIP paper (Apr 1, 2027).
- **Main risk:** The biggest risk is that tyre and caster compliance plus IMU noise make every labeller floor high (above about 10 mm). Few real lips would then get labels, so H2 and the camera arms have little small-step data. Dec-Feb weather and early dusk add schedule risk for Exp 2.

Fallbacks:
(1) Exp 1 runs in a garage over winter break and produces the label-floor-vs-r/v^2 curves and the OAK depth-floor curves no matter what happens outdoors. That is a self-contained, publishable measurement.
(2) If compliance dominates (H1 slope shallower than -1), that is itself the reported answer, and we add a hard polyurethane caster (about $10, estimate) as a third labeller.
(3) If outdoor sessions are lost, Exp 2 shrinks to 4 lip sites + 2 negative sites, and the camera arms are also tested leave-one-height-out on calibrated steps.
(4) If the November pilot shows the OAK-D Pro resolving 3 mm everywhere, H3 is settled against the camera early. The paper then reports the parity height h* and the label-floor result, and makes no camera-wins claim.
- **Changes from previous version:** 1. Novelty reframed. "Proprioceptive self-labelling is new" is gone. The angle now cites BADGR, Cross-Modal Supervision, Stavens & Thrun, Wellhausen, Howard and WayFAST (all checked) as the source of the labelling technique. The new contributions are: a stated, falsifiable physics mechanism for the label floor (h_min proportional to r/v^2); an inheritance test (does the camera's floor track its labeller's floor?); and a fair cost-parity boundary.

2. Hypothesis made honest. It now predicts the OAK-D Pro WILL resolve small steps in shade (with an explicit noise estimate), and that the $25 camera reaches parity only above a crossover height h* and possibly in direct sun. The claim that the cheap camera beats depth on small lips is gone.

3. The confound is removed with a 5-arm design on the same logged approaches:
   - Pi camera with a self-labelled CNN;
   - Pi camera with zero-shot Depth Anything V2 metric depth plus the same plane-fit step detector;
   - OAK depth with the plane-fit detector;
   - OAK depth with the same CNN and the same labels;
   - OAK RGB with the same CNN and the same labels (isolates the optics).

4. A new Experiment 1 runs on calibrated hardboard steps (0, 3, 6, 9, 12, 18 and 25 mm), with caster IMU, chassis IMU and hub current logged at 3 speeds. It measures the label floor directly, and both reviewers asked for that number. It can run in a garage, so weather cannot block it.

5. The evaluation range is now 0.8-2.0 m, which matches the OAK MinZ of about 80 cm at 800P (verified). Dusk is now a stretch goal, with the IR projector on and off.

6. Flush negatives (expansion joints, painted lines, cracks, shadows) were added so the camera cannot win just by spotting seams. Labels are hand-checked against caliper heights.

7. Statistics now use a cluster bootstrap over approaches and sites.

8. Scope was cut: 6 lip sites plus 4 negative sites, 120 day approaches. A November pilot was added (OAK-D Pro on 6, 12 and 25 mm steps).

9. The paper will state that the cost ratio counts sensors only, with the Pi 5 compute shared.

10. Cost rose from about $760 to about $860, for the step set, a second IMU, a lux meter and calipers.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 6 / F 5.5 / I 6 = 5.83. *Novelty:* The labelling mechanism is admittedly old (Stavens & Thrun 2006, BADGR, and Cross-Modal Supervision, whose abstract I opened: 'time-shifted proprioception to supervise vision', curbs up to 20 cm). The new parts are measuring the labeller's floor against wheel radius and speed, and testing whether the camera inherits that floor. I found no paper that does either, but my search was badly incomplete: WebSearch was exhausted, Crossref, OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar returned 429, and arXiv keyword search returned nothing. The h_min ~ (r/2)(dv/v)^2 'law' is basic rigid-wheel kinematics, so a reviewer may see it as a foreseeable confirmation rather than a discovery. Novelty is real but thin. *Feasibility:* Every part is hobbyist-grade and under $1k. But the build stacks several risky pieces into about 12 part-time weeks during winter: hoverboard FOC speed-mode firmware over UART, a single-clock logger for Pi Cam, OAK, two IMUs and the UART stream, odometry back-projection, five trained or zero-shot arms, 336 calibrated crossings, and 120 outdoor approaches. There is also a physical risk. A 3-4 cm caster pushed at 1.0 m/s into an 18-25 mm step (h/r about 0.5-0.8) may stall, shimmy or break its IMU mount. At that h/r the small-angle formula also stops holding. *Impact:* The design is clean: the five arms separate sensor from method, the bootstrap resamples whole sites, and outcomes are falsifiable. That makes it a strong ICRA-workshop or ARSO paper and a good application story. IROS main track is a stretch, though. The datasets are small (power note: CIs of +/-0.07-0.10), the depth-camera-wins result is predicted by Lost and Found-style stereo work, and the H1 law, as tested with only two labellers, cannot cleanly establish the r-scaling. *Main criticism:* H1 does not test what it claims. The two labellers differ in both wheel radius (caster about 3.5 cm vs hub about 8.3 cm) and sensing modality (fork IMU jerk vs hub phase current). Phase current responds to climbing torque, not the vertical velocity jump, so it is not governed by v*sqrt(2h/r) at all. A caster/hub floor ratio of 1.5x or more therefore cannot isolate the r-dependence, and the 'linear in r' half of the law is untested with only two points. In addition, the derivation assumes a rigid wheel and a velocity-jump detector, but the event detector thresholds jerk or acceleration. Jerk depends on impact duration, which is set by tyre and caster compliance, and surface-texture vibration also grows with speed. The predicted -2 slope is closer to an assumption than a mechanism, and a reviewer could call the confirmation trivial or call the test confounded. *Closest prior work:* [Learning Visual Locomotion with Cross-Modal Supervision (Loquercio, Kumar, Malik, 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.03785); [BADGR: An Autonomous Self-Supervised Learning-Based Navigation System (Kahn, Abbeel, Levine, RA-L 2021)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.05700); [A Self-Supervised Terrain Roughness Estimator for Off-Road Autonomous Driving (Stavens & Thrun, UAI 2006)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1206.6872); [Where Should I Walk? Predicting Terrain Properties From Images Via Self-Supervised Learning (Wellhausen et al., RA-L 2019)](https://doi.org/10.1109/lra.2019.2895390); [Lost and Found: detecting small road hazards for self-driving vehicles (Pinggera et al., IROS 2016)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.2016.7759186); [Learning to Predict Mobile Robot Stability in Off-Road Environments (arXiv 2507.12731)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12731)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5.5 / F 5.5 / I 6 = 5.67. *Novelty:* The labelling pipeline is openly prior work: Cross-Modal Supervision (arXiv 2211.03785) already uses time-shifted proprioception to supervise a monocular camera on curbs and steps, and BADGR, WVN and Stavens & Thrun cover the rest. The 'label floor' law, h_min ~ (r/2)(dv_min/v)^2, is first-order rigid-wheel impact kinematics, and speed-dependent accelerometer bump detection goes back to Pothole Patrol (2008). The genuinely new parts are the floor-inheritance test and the mm-scale sensor-by-method comparison against active stereo at 30 cm height, but both outcomes are fairly predictable (depth wins below ~1 cm; floor falls with speed), which is the same 'foreseeable comparison' criticism the earlier rounds raised. *Feasibility:* Exp 1 (168 garage crossings plus static OAK captures) is realistic over winter break with hobbyist parts. The total (~$860) and the OAK-D Pro $429, MinZ ~80 cm at 800P and Pi Cam 3 'from $25' figures all check out against the vendor pages. Exp 2 is where it breaks: 6 positive sites spread over 4 height bins means about 1-2 sites per bin under leave-one-site-out, so the camera floor (H2) and crossover height h* (H3) are not statistically identifiable. H2 also needs traversals at both 0.3 and 1.0 m/s per site, which the 120-approach budget does not include. Driving a 3-4 cm caster into 25 mm steps at 1 m/s (h/r up to ~0.8) also risks jamming and shimmy. *Impact:* A measured self-supervision limit with a design rule ('instrument the smallest, fastest wheel') and a fair 5-arm sensor-vs-method design is a clean ICRA-workshop or ARSO paper and a strong application story. For IROS main track, the small number of sites, the predictable direction of H3 (the proposal itself predicts the $429 camera wins on small steps) and a simple-kinematics law make acceptance unlikely. *Main criticism:* The headline claims (H2 inheritance and the H3 cost-parity height h*) rest on 6 positive real sites across 4 height bins. Under leave-one-site-out, each bin's AUC is effectively one or two sites, so 'camera floor within one bin of labeller floor' and 'h* = 10-20 mm with a CI' cannot be resolved. That leaves Exp 1 as the real paper, and its law is close to textbook impact kinematics. It also covers only step-ups and is thresholded on acceleration rather than velocity. On top of that, the hardboard step set has a distinctive texture, so a CNN trained on it can learn 'brown board' rather than height. *Closest prior work:* [Learning Visual Locomotion with Cross-Modal Supervision (Loquercio, Kumar, Malik, 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.03785); [BADGR: An Autonomous Self-Supervised Learning-Based Navigation System (Kahn, Abbeel, Levine)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.05700); [Fast Traversability Estimation for Wild Visual Navigation (Frey et al., WVN)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.08510); [STERLING: Self-Supervised Terrain Representation Learning from Unconstrained Robot Experience (Karnan et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15302); [The Pothole Patrol: using a mobile sensor network for road surface monitoring (Eriksson et al., MobiSys 2008)](https://doi.org/10.1145/1378600.1378605); [A Self-Supervised Terrain Roughness Estimator for Off-Road Autonomous Driving (Stavens & Thrun, UAI 2006)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1206.6872); [Arducam Time of Flight Camera for Raspberry Pi](https://blog.arducam.com/time-of-flight-camera-raspberry-pi/)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 6 / F 5.5 / I 5 = 5.50. *Novelty:* The labelling idea is about 20 years old, and the proposal correctly concedes this. Cross-Modal Supervision (arXiv 2211.03785, abstract opened) already uses time-shifted proprioception to supervise a monocular RGB camera on curbs and steps. The h_min ~ r/v^2 law is first-order wheel-over-step kinematics, which a vehicle-dynamics reviewer will see as textbook (UNVERIFIED: I could not search the tyre-enveloping or accelerometer pothole-detection literature). What remains new is the measured label floor, the inheritance test, and the 5-arm sensor-by-method parity boundary. That is real but thin, and only two wheel radii are tested. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and in stock: the Luxonis shop shows US $429, arriving Oct 6-9. Exp 1 (168 crossings) is a winter-break-sized task. But one perception student must build a hoverboard-FOC base, a single-clock multi-sensor logger, odometry back-projection, a CNN, DA-V2, RANSAC and cluster-bootstrap tooling, while running 120 outdoor approaches over 8 sessions in Jan-Feb weather. Several of these components carry risk on their own (FOC firmware flashing, impact transients from the BNO085 under Linux I2C jitter), so slippage is likely. *Impact:* IROS main track is a long shot. The headline H3 outcome, that active stereo wins below about 10-20 mm, is the one the proposal itself predicts, and the floor law is simple kinematics. The single-site, small-n dataset has CIs of +/-0.07-0.10 AUC. An ICRA 2027 workshop or a CASE WIP paper is the realistic venue. For a high-school application the story is good, though not exceptional. *Main criticism:* Neither headline mechanism is cleanly identifiable with this design.

(1) H1 claims a law in wheel radius r, but r takes only two values, and those are confounded with sensing modality and mounting: a caster-fork IMU versus hub phase current on a different, loaded, compliant wheel. A caster/hub floor ratio cannot separate r from IMU-vs-current sensitivity. The law is also derived for a velocity jump (v*sqrt(2h/r)), while the event detector thresholds jerk or acceleration. For a rigid wheel, peak acceleration depends on contact compliance and impact duration, not simply on h. Step-down events are free-fall physics, not r/v^2.

(2) The calibrated hardboard steps carry a visual edge or material cue that the flush 0 mm control (bare floor) lacks. A camera CNN can learn 'hardboard edge' and not height, so H2 and the leave-one-height-out fallback are confounded by appearance. *Closest prior work:* [Learning Visual Locomotion with Cross-Modal Supervision (Loquercio, Kumar, Malik, arXiv 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.03785); [BADGR (Kahn, Abbeel, Levine, RA-L 2021)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.05700); [A Self-Supervised Terrain Roughness Estimator for Off-Road Autonomous Driving (Stavens & Thrun, UAI 2006)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1206.6872); [Curb recognition for robotic wheelchairs with a RealSense D455 (Sivakanthan et al. 2021), from link-verified prior_art.md](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8659845/); [WalkOCC: monocular 3D occupancy on sidewalk robots (arXiv 2026), from prior_art.md](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19122); [OAK-D Pro product docs (baseline spec, not prior work)](https://docs.luxonis.com/hardware/products/OAK-D%20Pro)

### R3.v2 (round 2, median 5.67): How good must slip labels be? Label-error structure vs. learned slip-model gain, from DRIVE's lidar-ICP data to a $40 optical-flow label

- **Question:** In DRIVE-style slip-model learning, how much of the slip-BLR model's prediction gain survives when the lidar-ICP body-velocity labels are replaced by lower-quality labels, and is the loss governed by label noise magnitude or by input-correlated label bias (so that a cheap downward optical-flow label with an in-protocol zero-slip self-calibration recovers the ICP-label gain)?
- **Hypothesis:** H1 (offline, on DRIVE's own Husky/Warthog lidar-ICP data): at equal label RMS error (0.05 and 0.10 m/s), zero-mean white noise retains at least 75% of slip-BLR's ICP-label gain over the powertrain-aware model and mainly lengthens convergence (DRIVE's 46 s rises by at most 2x). A 10% speed-proportional scale bias or yaw-to-lateral leakage of the same RMS retains at most 50%. Falsified if the two error structures differ by less than 15 percentage points in retained gain.
H2 (on robot): raw PMW3901+ToF translational labels with gyro yaw retain less than 60% of the tracker-label gain on the gravel bed. With the in-protocol zero-slip self-calibration (scale and lever arm), they retain at least 80% on both concrete and gravel.
H3 (transfer): the offline H1 curve, given the cheap sensor's measured bias/noise decomposition, predicts the on-robot retained gain within ±15 percentage points.
H4 (secondary, two-sided): tripling robot mass (4 kg to 12 kg) changes slip-BLR's gain over the powertrain-aware model on gravel by more than 5 percentage points.
Each hypothesis is tested and reported separately.
- **Measurements:** OFFLINE (no hardware; done by end of November):
- Data: DRIVE's in-repo Husky and Warthog data (github.com/norlab-ulaval/DRIVE calib_data, verified to exist). The six published datasets (Husky snow/tile, HD2 snow/tile, Warthog gravel/ice) will be added if Norlab restores bucket access; it returned 403 on 2026-10-04.
- Label degradations, applied to the 20 Hz body-velocity labels:
  - white noise, 6 levels from 0.01 to 0.3 m/s
  - random-walk drift
  - speed-proportional scale bias of ±2-20%
  - yaw-to-lateral leakage from an off-center sensor
  - dropouts
  - an 'error transplant': block-bootstrapped residuals of the team's real sensors vs. the tracker, scaled to the robot's speed
- Each degradation is run with 20 random seeds. Each run retrains naive, powertrain-aware, acceleration-BLR and slip-BLR with DRIVE's code and evaluates them with DRIVE's 2-s multi-step RMSE against the ORIGINAL ICP labels on DRIVE's held-out half.
- Outputs: retained-gain fraction vs. label RMS, broken down by error structure; and convergence time (DRIVE's gradient criterion of 0.01 m/s and 0.01 rad/s) vs. label noise.

ON-ROBOT (indoor garage, about 3 x 4 m patch):
- 2 surfaces (sealed concrete; pea-gravel bed about 2-3 cm deep on a tarp) x 2 masses (4 kg; 12 kg with ballast) x 5 sessions = 20 sessions.
- Each session: about 20 s of zero-slip calibration passes, then about 6 min of DRIVE random-command driving (6-s intervals with 1 transient and 2 steady windows), split into train and evaluation halves as in DRIVE.
- Label sources, logged at the same time:
  - (a) encoders only (naive/powertrain)
  - (b) gyro yaw + PMW3901/ToF translation, raw
  - (c) source (b) with the self-calibration applied
  - (d) gyro + SparkFun OTOS (concrete only)
  - (e) ceiling-camera AprilTag tracker (reference), validated against a chalk grid to at most 1.5 cm
- Every model is evaluated against the tracker with 2-s MRMSE on at least 200 held-out windows per session.
- Sensor error decomposition per surface: scale slope by speed bin, lever-arm leakage, residual PSD and dropout rate.

ANALYSIS:
- Paired per-session Wilcoxon tests between label sources.
- Retained-gain fraction with bootstrap 95% CIs, computed only where the reference gain's CI excludes zero; otherwise absolute MRMSE is reported.
- H3: predicted vs. observed retained gain across the 2 surfaces x 2 masses x 2-3 label sources.
- H4: mass effect with a CI.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** 1) Expensive-stack baseline: DRIVE's own lidar-ICP labels on the 75 kg Husky and 470 kg Warthog. The team reproduces the paper's model comparison from the released code/data and checks against the reported numbers (verified in the PDF text):
   - 34%/38% median translation/rotation error reduction of powertrain-aware vs. naive
   - 6%/22% for slip-BLR vs. acceleration-BLR
   - 23%/71% on Warthog-gravel
   - 46 s convergence
   ICP labels are the 'clean' reference into which every degradation is injected, so cheap-vs-expensive labels are compared directly on the same robots and data.
2) DRIVE's full model ladder (naive, powertrain-aware, acceleration-BLR, slip-BLR) as model baselines.
3) On the robot, an overhead AprilTag tracker as the reference label and evaluation ground truth. It plays the role ICP plays in DRIVE, and is validated against a chalk grid.
4) A T-FR-style label configuration (IMU yaw + external translational velocity) on the same robot.
- **Novelty claim:** DRIVE (ICRA 2024) requires 'an accurate localization system, estimating the robot position and velocity in a global frame'. Its T-FR 2025 follow-up says it will 'assume that we can access directly the state vector x and its noise is reasonable' (both quotes verified in the paper text). Neither paper, and nothing I found, measures how much label error slip-model learning tolerates, or whether the error's structure matters more than its size.

The optical-mouse/flow odometry literature (Cimino & Pagilla ICRA 2010; Ross & Devlin ICARCV 2010; Yi et al. ICCAS 2015) characterizes these sensors as odometers, not as training labels for a predictive slip model. Song et al. (IROS 2008) estimate skid-steer slip online from optical flow but learn no predictive model and use no protocol. Pentzer et al. (JFR 2014) estimate track ICRs online. SlipOdometryNet (2026 preprint; abstract read) learns slip-aware odometry in Gazebo only, with simulator ground truth.

The contributions:
(1) A label-quality vs. retained-gain curve computed on real lidar-ICP data from 75-470 kg robots. It applies to any platform choosing a label sensor.
(2) The hypothesis, with a test, that bias rather than noise sets the label requirement for a linear-Gaussian slip learner.
(3) One non-obvious fix that follows from it: a zero-slip self-calibration of the cheap sensor folded into DRIVE's existing wheel-limit step.
(4) A transfer test showing that the offline curve predicts on-robot results.
(5) A controlled mass variable that DRIVE could not vary.

I do not claim the sensor is new: flow/mouse odometry dates to 2004-2015 and FTC teams use the OTOS.

Search limits: arXiv API (DRIVE, slip/skid-steer learning, optical-flow slip, label noise) and Crossref bibliographic queries. The WebSearch budget was exhausted, Semantic Scholar and OpenAlex were rate-limited, and Google Scholar and IEEE Xplore were not searched directly. The team must re-check Scholar for 'label noise' + 'learned vehicle model' before submission.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [DRIVE: Data-driven Robot Input Vector Exploration (Baril et al., ICRA 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10718); [DRIVE Through the Unpredictability (Samson et al., IEEE T-FR 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.16593); [norlab-ulaval/DRIVE code and data repository (BSD-3)](https://github.com/norlab-ulaval/DRIVE); [Optical Flow-Based Slip and Velocity Estimation Technique for Unmanned Skid-Steered Vehicles (Song, Song, Seneviratne, Althoefer, IROS 2008)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iros.2008.4651025); [Analysis of real-time velocity compensation for outdoor optical mouse sensor odometry (Ross & Devlin, ICARCV 2010)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icarcv.2010.5707970); [Location of optical mouse sensors on mobile robots for odometry (Cimino & Pagilla, ICRA 2010)](https://doi.org/10.1109/robot.2010.5509317); [Afocal optical flow sensor for mobile robot odometry (Yi, Lee, Cho, ICCAS 2015)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iccas.2015.7364858); [Model-based Prediction of Skid-steer Robot Kinematics Using Online Estimation of Track ICRs (Pentzer, Brennan, Reichard, JFR 2014)](https://doi.org/10.1002/rob.21509); [SlipOdometryNet: Slip-Aware Wheel Odometry for 4-Wheeled Skid-Steer Robots Using a Compact 1-D CNN (Prabhu & Dsouza, Research Square preprint 2026)](https://doi.org/10.21203/rs.3.rs-9959755/v1); [Planar Velocity Estimation for Fast-Moving Mobile Robots Using Event-Based Optical Flow (arXiv 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11116); [SparkFun Optical Tracking Odometry Sensor PAA5160E1 (product page)](https://www.sparkfun.com/sparkfun-optical-tracking-odometry-sensor-paa5160e1-qwiic.html)
- **Build:** Mechanical student:
- 4WD skid-steer about 4 kg (aluminum chassis, 12 V encoder gear motors), with a ballast tray taking +8 kg of steel plates or sandbags.
- Rigid mount holding a PMW3901 at about 90-100 mm with a VL53L1X ToF beside it, and a spring-loaded OTOS sled at 10 mm (concrete only).
- Top-plate AprilTag.
- Ceiling bracket for a wide-angle USB camera over a 3 x 4 m patch.
- Gravel bed: tarp with 2x4 edging, about 10-12 bags of pea gravel, raked to a fixed depth before each session.

Software/perception student:
- Offline (Oct-Nov): load DRIVE's in-repo data, reproduce the four-model comparison, and build the label-degradation harness (noise, drift, scale bias, leakage, dropout, and the error-transplant block bootstrap).
- On robot: Pi 5 ROS 2 Humble node logging encoders, IMU, PMW3901+ToF, OTOS and commands at 20-100 Hz; port of DRIVE's command sampler with geofenced rejection sampling (commands whose naive-model 6-s endpoint leaves the patch are resampled) and logged input-space coverage.
- Zero-slip self-calibration routine: slow straight passes plus in-place turns that fit flow scale and lever arm.
- Fisheye-calibrated AprilTag tracker with chalk-grid validation.
- Evaluation scripts reproducing DRIVE's 2-s MRMSE.
- **Cost:** $660: - Raspberry Pi 5 4GB: $60 (per the LeKiwi BOM in prior_art.md)
- 4WD skid-steer chassis with 12 V encoder gear motors and driver: ~$150 (estimate)
- PMW3901 breakout: ~$30 (price UNVERIFIED; specs verified on Pimoroni)
- VL53L1X ToF: ~$15 (estimate)
- SparkFun OTOS PAA5160E1: $84.95 (verified on SparkFun)
- IMU (BNO085/ICM-20948 class): ~$25 (estimate)
- Wide-angle USB camera: ~$70 (estimate), plus ceiling bracket ~$20 (estimate)
- Pea gravel, 10-12 bags: ~$80 (estimate); tarp and 2x4 edging: ~$40 (estimate)
- Ballast plates/sandbags: ~$25 (estimate)
- Battery and charger: ~$50 (estimate)
- Tags, chalk, tape: ~$10

Total ≈ $660, well under $1,000.

The expensive comparison stack (Husky $19-24k per a third-party listing in prior_art.md, plus 3D lidar, price UNVERIFIED) is not bought. Its labels come free from DRIVE's released data.
- **Venue:** IEEE/RSJ IROS 2027 (deadline Mar 1, 2027, 8 pages; fit 8/10 per venues.md). The result is a transferable, quantitative finding about learned vehicle models, a label-quality requirement measured on real lidar-ICP data from 75-470 kg UGVs and validated on hardware. That suits IROS field-robotics and model-learning reviewers better than a toy-robot reproduction would. IEEE CASE 2027 (same deadline) is the alternative, framed as low-cost automated model identification. A shortened version can go to an ICRA 2027 workshop (CFPs Feb-Mar).
- **Main risk:** The main risk is that the 4-12 kg robot's slip signal on concrete and gravel is smaller than the label noise, so the on-robot retained-gain fraction is undefined (as DRIVE itself saw on HD2-tile). Mitigations:
- A gravel bed and ballast are chosen specifically to create slip.
- Speed limits are raised and the gravel deepened if the Jan 15 checkpoint fails.
- The fraction is reported only where the reference gain's CI excludes zero.

Fallback: the offline H1 study on DRIVE's ICP data plus the measured cheap-sensor error decomposition (H3 run open-loop as a prediction) still makes a complete paper.

Secondary risks:
- (a) DRIVE's six-dataset bucket stays inaccessible. The study then runs on the in-repo Husky/Warthog data, whose terrain labels must be confirmed from the files.
- (b) A low garage ceiling limits tracker coverage. Shrink the patch to about 2.5 x 3.5 m and lower the speed caps, or use two cameras (+$70).
- **Changes from previous version:** 1. Research question reframed from "reproduce DRIVE with a flow sensor on a 3 kg robot" to "how much label error, and of what structure, can DRIVE-style slip-model learning tolerate?". It now tests a stated assumption of DRIVE and T-FR rather than reproducing a result.
2. Real head-to-head against the expensive stack. Calibrated label degradations (white noise, drift, speed-proportional scale bias, yaw-to-lateral leakage, and the team's own measured sensor error "transplanted" in by block bootstrap) are injected into DRIVE's released lidar-ICP labels for the Husky and Warthog. Models are retrained with DRIVE's code and scored against the original ICP labels with DRIVE's 2-s MRMSE. This produces a label-quality vs. retained-gain curve on the 75-470 kg robots.
3. Baselines and numbers corrected. All four DRIVE models are used: naive, powertrain-aware, acceleration-BLR and slip-BLR. The numbers are correctly attributed: 34%/38% is naive vs. powertrain, and 6%/22% is slip-BLR vs. acceleration-BLR, with 23%/71% on Warthog-gravel. The retained-gain fraction is computed only where the reference gain's CI excludes zero, which handles the HD2-tile-style null.
4. One explicit non-obvious idea. Slip-BLR is a linear-Gaussian regressor, so zero-mean label noise should mostly cost convergence time, while input-correlated bias should cap the gain. The fix is therefore an in-protocol zero-slip self-calibration of the cheap sensor's scale and lever arm, appended to DRIVE's existing wheel-limit step. It costs about 20 s per session.
5. A transfer test was added. Feeding the sensor's measured bias/noise decomposition into the offline curve must predict the on-robot retained gain within ±15 pp.
6. Sensor physics fixed:
   - The PMW3901 is described correctly: about 80 mm minimum range, 121 FPS, 42° FOV, angular flow scaled by a ToF height reading (Pimoroni page verified).
   - Yaw labels come explicitly from the gyro, following T-FR.
   - The SparkFun OTOS (mouse-class, 10-27 mm, $84.95, verified) is added as a second error-structure point on concrete only.
   - The wrong "fixed focus breaks on grass" mechanism is dropped.
7. Feasibility:
   - The outdoor 3-terrain matrix and the tall outdoor mast are removed. All runs are indoors in a garage: sealed concrete plus a pea-gravel bed on a tarp, under a ceiling-mounted wide-angle tracker.
   - 30 two-hour outdoor sessions become 20 thirty-minute indoor sessions, which are weather-proof.
   - All label sources are logged at the same time in each session, so label comparisons are paired.
   - A geofenced command sampler replaces DRIVE's safety-operator perimeter.
8. Mass is now a controlled secondary variable (4 kg vs. 12 kg with ballast on the same chassis). Before, it was speculation.
9. Prior work corrected and expanded: T-FR 2025, Song et al. IROS 2008, Ross & Devlin ICARCV 2010, Cimino & Pagilla ICRA 2010, Yi et al. ICCAS 2015, Pentzer et al. JFR 2014, and SlipOdometryNet 2026 (abstract read). The novelty claim is now the learning-label question, not the sensor.
10. Cost revised to about $660, with the gravel bed, ballast, OTOS, ToF and IMU itemized and the estimates labeled.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 6.5 / I 5.5 = 5.67. *Novelty:* I found no paper that measures how label error affects a DRIVE-style learned slip model; a search for label noise plus dynamics plus robot plus terrain on the arXiv API returned 0 hits. T-FR 2025 explicitly just assumes the noise is 'reasonable'. However, H1's central claim (bias matters, white noise mostly doesn't) follows almost directly from linear-regression theory. In the DRIVE PDF, slip-BLR's regressors are commanded-velocity basis functions, so label noise is pure output noise: it leaves γ unbiased and only slows convergence. A speed-proportional scale bias, by contrast, is correlated with f_x and shifts γ by a predictable amount. T-FR already notes that steady-state averaging cancels noise, and calibrating the flow sensor's scale and lever arm is established (Cimino ICRA 2010; Paijens 2020). The study is therefore new in its measurements, but its headline mechanism can be predicted. *Feasibility:* The offline half is very feasible. The in-repo data exist with ICP poses at 20 Hz (I verified about 35 and 30 min), the code is public, and H1 could be finished by November, which gives a real fallback. The on-robot half has several risks. A 4-12 kg robot may slip too little on concrete or gravel. A PMW3901 at 90-100 mm over loose gravel may drop out. Differentiating a ceiling AprilTag tracker with 1.5 cm error at 20 Hz gives a velocity reference that can be noisier than the cheap sensor under test. On top of that come a ceiling camera, a gravel bed and 20 sessions, all in about 12 weeks part-time. *Impact:* An offline sensitivity sweep on another lab's data, plus a small-robot validation, fits an ICRA 2027 workshop, CASE or ARSO. An IROS main-track reviewer is likely to call H1 an expected consequence of Gauss-Markov / BLR algebra, so acceptance depends on surprises in the transfer test (H3) or the mass result (H4), and those are exactly the parts that may come out undefined if the robot barely slips. As a college-application story, it is a solid quantitative reproduction-plus-extension of an ICRA paper. *Main criticism:* The headline finding, that label bias rather than noise limits the slip learner, can be derived on paper rather than discovered. DRIVE's slip-BLR regresses observed slip on commanded-velocity basis functions, so zero-mean label noise leaves the weights unbiased and only slows convergence, while a scale bias correlated with commanded speed moves the weights by a predictable amount. A 20-seed simulation sweep would mostly confirm algebra a reviewer can do in their head. The parts that could surprise (H3 transfer, H4 mass) depend on a 4-12 kg robot producing enough measurable slip under a reference tracker whose differentiated velocity is itself noisy. *Closest prior work:* [DRIVE: Data-driven Robot Input Vector Exploration (Baril et al., ICRA 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10718); [DRIVE Through the Unpredictability (Samson et al., IEEE T-FR 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.16593); [norlab-ulaval/DRIVE repository (calib_data)](https://github.com/norlab-ulaval/DRIVE); [Planar Velocity Estimation for Fast-Moving Mobile Robots Using Event-Based Optical Flow (arXiv 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11116)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 7 / I 5 = 5.67. *Novelty:* I found no paper that sweeps label quality against learned slip-model gain. Two Google Scholar queries returned zero matches, and DRIVE and T-FR never vary label quality. That gap is real. The headline finding, though, is foreseeable from textbook regression: DRIVE's slip-BLR (blr_slip.py) regresses slip = measured minus ideal-diff-drive velocity on encoder-derived inputs, with 1, 1 and 3 parameters per axis. Zero-mean label noise therefore leaves the weights unbiased, while a scale bias or lever-arm leakage maps directly into the weights. That is exactly the kind of 'predictable comparison' the 25-reviewer round penalized. *Feasibility:* The offline half carries little risk. I loaded the in-repo Husky data (about 35 min) and the Warthog data (about 30 min), both 20 Hz with ICP pose. The training code is plain numpy, so H1 can be done by November. The on-robot half has real risks. A 4-12 kg robot inside a geofenced 3x4 m patch will have a small slip signal. Concrete and gravel cannot share one patch without shoveling roughly 0.3 m³ (about 500 kg) of gravel between conditions. The ceiling tracker also needs ≤1.5 cm accuracy on both surfaces. Because the offline fallback is credible, I score 7 rather than 6. *Impact:* At IROS, reviewers who know regression will see H1 as an errors-in-variables textbook result: label noise on a 1-3-parameter linear model with thousands of samples barely matters, and label bias passes straight through. They will see H3 (offline predicts on-robot) as close to tautological. Toy 4-12 kg robot results on a garage gravel tarp will not move field-robotics reviewers. Realistically this is CASE, workshop or student-conference level. For college applications it is a reasonable but not standout story. *Main criticism:* The core scientific claim, that label bias rather than label noise limits a linear-Gaussian slip learner, follows almost analytically from DRIVE's own model structure. The verified code shows the inputs come only from wheel velocities and the labels sit on the target side. Zero-mean noise on the targets of a 1-3-parameter BLR trained on thousands of samples is unbiased, and speed-proportional scale bias enters the weights one-for-one. A reviewer can derive H1 and most of H3 on a whiteboard, so the expensive-looking 20-seed sweep reads as confirming Gauss-Markov rather than discovering something. The on-robot half adds a small, slow robot whose slip may sit below sensor noise. *Closest prior work:* [DRIVE: Data-driven Robot Input Vector Exploration (Baril et al., ICRA 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10718); [DRIVE Through the Unpredictability (Samson et al., IEEE T-FR 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.16593); [norlab-ulaval/DRIVE repository (BSD-3)](https://github.com/norlab-ulaval/DRIVE); [Learning and prediction of slip from visual information (Angelova, Matthies, Helmick, Perona, JFR 2007)](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1002/rob.20179); [Improving trajectory tracking accuracy for faster and safer autonomous navigation of ground vehicles in off-road settings (Gregory, Warnell, Fink, 2021)](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/abstract/document/9597858/); [SparkFun Optical Tracking Odometry Sensor PAA5160E1](https://www.sparkfun.com/sparkfun-optical-tracking-odometry-sensor-paa5160e1-qwiic.html)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5.5 / F 7 / I 6 = 6.17. *Novelty:* Neither DRIVE paper nor any other source I found measures how much slip-model gain survives degraded labels (T-FR explicitly assumes the noise is 'reasonable'), so the gap is real. But the headline finding is close to textbook. In a linear Bayesian regression, zero-mean label noise does not bias the estimate and only adds variance or slows convergence. A speed-proportional scale bias shifts the learned slip coefficients directly. Optical-flow/mouse slip estimation and odometry go back to at least 2004-2015, so H1 reads as a foreseeable confirmation. The genuinely new parts are the H3 transfer test and the self-calibration. *Feasibility:* I verified that the offline half is low-risk. The in-repo Husky (about 35 min) and Warthog (about 30 min) logs contain ICP poses, IMU, wheel velocities and commands, so H1 can be finished before any hardware exists, which gives a credible fallback. The on-robot half has real risk. DRIVE's own 80 kg HD2 on tile showed no slip-BLR gain, and a 4-12 kg robot on concrete or 2-3 cm of pea gravel may show none either. The Pimoroni PMW3901 is listed as pre-order, which is a lead-time risk. 20 tracker sessions plus bootstrap and error-transplant statistics in 12 part-time weeks is tight but doable. *Impact:* A quantitative label-requirement curve on real 75-470 kg lidar-ICP data is useful and fits IROS field-robotics and model-learning reviewers. However, the core result is predictable from regression theory and sits on another lab's two logs plus a small toy robot, so IROS main track is a long shot. CASE, an ICRA workshop or ARSO is more realistic. The offline-only fallback, a perturbation study, is workshop-level. It is still a respectable, rigorous-looking application story. *Main criticism:* The headline hypothesis (H1: white label noise is tolerated but structured bias is not) is close to a textbook property of linear least squares and Bayesian linear regression: zero-mean output noise leaves estimates unbiased, and a systematic scale bias shifts them directly. A reviewer will likely call it foreseeable, which is the same 'predictable comparison' problem the earlier 25 reviewers raised. Meanwhile the on-robot part may produce no measurable slip gain at 4-12 kg, as DRIVE's HD2-tile result already shows, and the offline data cannot reproduce the paper's aggregate numbers without Norlab's external datasets. *Closest prior work:* [DRIVE: Data-driven Robot Input Vector Exploration (Baril et al., ICRA 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10718); [DRIVE Through the Unpredictability (Samson et al., T-FR 2025)](https://arxiv.org/html/2506.16593); [norlab-ulaval/DRIVE repository (BSD-3, ROS 2 Humble)](https://github.com/norlab-ulaval/DRIVE); [AnyCar to Anywhere: Learning Universal Dynamics Model for Agile and Adaptive Mobility (Xiao et al., 2024/2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.15783); [Meta-learning online dynamics model adaptation in off-road autonomous driving (Levy et al., arXiv 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16923); [Pimoroni PMW3901 optical flow breakout (product page)](https://shop.pimoroni.com/products/pmw3901-optical-flow-sensor-breakout)

### R5.v2 (round 2, median 5.67): The ground is the ruler: brand-agnostic docking pose for unmodified refuse carts from a $14 camera, vs. known-model PnP and a $96 2D lidar

- **Question:** For unmodified 64/96-gal refuse carts of a brand the system has never seen, does a single $14-26 wide-FOV camera estimate the 3-DoF docking pose (axle-midpoint x, y and yaw) to within 3 cm / 1.15 deg at 0.5-1 m? The method back-projects two learned wheel-ground-contact keypoints onto the calibrated ground plane. Over what range, and at what driveway slope mismatch, does it beat keypoint EPnP with a held-out-brand mean model, a per-brand measured model, and an LD19 2D-lidar line fit? (3 cm / 1.15 deg are Xiao et al.'s short-range lidar figures, 0.03 m / 0.02 rad.)
- **Hypothesis:** H1 (cost-down replication). With a per-brand measured model, keypoint EPnP on the $14 camera matches the published camera stages: MAE of 15 cm or less at 3 m (Sun et al.: 0.139 m with a camera of about US$400; Xiao et al.: 0.17 m) and 3 cm or less at 0.5 m.

H2 (the gap). On held-out brands, mean-shape EPnP has at least 2x the per-brand position error at 1 m and beyond. Its error grows roughly in proportion to range times the brand dimension mismatch, which is up to about 29% in width among 96-gal carts per spec sheets.

H3 (the idea). The ground-contact (GC) estimator uses no cart model. Where the robot-to-cart slope mismatch is under 1 deg, on held-out brands, it reaches 3 cm or less and 1.15 deg or less at 0.5 m, and 4 cm or less at 1 m. At 1 m or closer it beats held-out-brand EPnP by at least 50% in median position error, and it is within 1 cm of the LD19 line fit.

H4 (error model). GC depth error grows with the measured slope mismatch as (Z^2+h^2)/h x delta-theta, and the fitted coefficient lies within ±30% of that prediction. The model therefore predicts a crossover range beyond which held-out-brand EPnP wins. With camera height h = 0.45 m this is estimated at about 1.5-2.5 m for 1 deg of mismatch (estimate).

Falsified if any of these hold:
