- GC median error at 0.5 m on near-flat trials exceeds 3 cm;
- GC does not beat held-out-brand EPnP at 1 m;
- the slope-error coefficient is off by more than 30%.
- **Measurements:** Design: 160 static trials = 5 carts (target: Toter 64 and 96, Cascade 96, Rehrig 65 or 95, plus one more; the team's own carts plus loaned neighbours' carts, left unmodified) x 4 nominal ranges (0.5, 1, 2, 3 m to the axle midpoint) x 8 poses (lateral offset ±0.3 m crossed with cart yaw 0/±20 deg, randomized). Trials are spread over 3 sites: a planar driveway, a sloped driveway with an apron or garage-lip transition, and a garage floor.

Per trial:
- 2 s of camera frames, LD19 scans and IMU pitch/roll;
- the median estimate of each method plus its per-frame jitter.

Ground truth (independent of odometry and of the camera):
- Tape trilateration of 4 distances, from two plumb-bob points on the sensor head to the two chalked wheel-contact points of the cart. The contact points are found with a carpenter's square against each tire.
- This gives the relative SE(2) pose. Estimated accuracy is about 3-5 mm and about 0.7 deg (estimate). It is verified by a repeatability pre-study: 3 configurations x 10 repeats, measured by both students.
- Slope at the sensor patch and the cart patch, from a digital angle gauge along the line of sight. This defines delta-theta.
- Lux reading and sun/overcast/dusk logged as covariates.

Metrics, per method and range:
- position error of the axle midpoint (cm);
- Sun et al.'s MAE (|dx|+|dy|) and RMSE, for comparability;
- yaw error (deg);
- keypoint-detection success rate.

Methods compared on identical frames:
- (a) GC ground-contact back-projection;
- (b) EPnP with per-brand measured model (oracle);
- (c) EPnP with a leave-one-brand-out mean model (the detector is also trained leave-one-brand-out);
- (d) LD19 RANSAC line fit to the cart body, mapped to the axle frame using a per-brand measured offset (generous to the lidar);
- optional (e) GC-rescaled PnP, where the mean model is scaled by the GC-measured wheel track.

Analysis:
- paired differences between methods with bootstrap 95% CIs clustered by cart;
- per-cart and per-brand tables;
- a mixed model: error ~ method x range + |delta-theta| + lighting + (1|cart) + (1|site);
- regression of GC depth error on the predicted (Z^2+h^2)/h x delta-theta (slope and R^2), with the empirical crossover range compared against the predicted one;
- accuracy-per-dollar curve: $14 camera vs $96 LD19 vs the published camera of about $400.

The predictions and analysis plan are frozen in a dated commit before data collection.

Optional stretch: 20 closed-loop docking attempts with a passive hitch on the bin-robot base using GC, reporting success rate with a Wilson CI.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** In-house baselines, run on every trial from the same frames and scans:
- (1) keypoint EPnP with a per-brand measured 3D model (oracle upper bound, standing in for the published keypoint-PnP pipelines);
- (2) keypoint EPnP with a leave-one-brand-out mean-shape model. This is the realistic deployment case and the category-statistics scale recovery that Wei et al. describe.
- (3) a $95.99 LD19 2D lidar with a RANSAC line fit. Its spec is ±45 mm per point, so it is presented as a cheap range-sensor comparator, not as lidar-grade.

Published reference numbers, not paired, used with the same range-binned protocol:
- Sun et al., Robotica 2025: keypoint EPnP with one camera of about US$400, xy MAE 0.1386 / RMSE 0.1167 (verified in their PDF);
- Xiao et al., ICRA 2022: camera PnP 0.17 m / 0.11 rad at long range, lidar plane 0.03 m / 0.02 rad at short range, from a 5-sensor stack (3D lidar, two 2D lidars, solid-state lidar, RGB-D). The stack is not priced and is not bought (cost UNVERIFIED). No code is linked on its arXiv page, so the protocol and metrics are replicated, not the code.
- **Novelty claim:** The new part is not the ground-plane cue. It is classical (Stein et al. 2003) and used in monocular 3D detection (MonoGround) and in depth estimation, where GVDepth (ICCV 2025) fuses size and vertical-position cues on driving data. The new part is applying it to the 3-DoF docking problem for a category whose members differ in size by up to about 29%, and measuring when it wins.

Three contributions:
- (1) the first held-out-brand docking-pose benchmark on unmodified refuse carts, with ground truth independent of odometry, comparing size-cue PnP, ground-cue GC and a cheap lidar on identical frames;
- (2) a validated error model that predicts the crossover range between size-cue PnP and ground-cue GC from measured driveway slope mismatch, which no cited work measures for docking;
- (3) a cost-down replication of the published keypoint-PnP docking protocol (Sun et al.: camera of about $400; Xiao et al.: a 5-sensor stack) on a $14-26 camera.

Relation to prior work:
- Xiao et al. and Sun et al. use per-object models on one trolley type indoors.
- DA-VPC (2025) needs active IR markers on the trolley.
- Lei et al. need QR markers plus rangefinders.
- Feng and Zhang (ICSSIP 2025, DOI 10.1109/ICSSIP68411.2025.11692816, abstract via Semantic Scholar) localize a robot from a fixed infrastructure camera via perspective mapping, which is the inverse setup and has no category variation.
- Wei et al. recover monocular metric scale from category statistics, which is the baseline this paper tests and expects to fail across brands.
- The previously proposed weave is classical bearing-only and active-SfM observability (2405.05937, 2302.01729, 1807.00753) and is dropped.

Search coverage:
- arXiv API: 'docking AND ground plane' and 'monocular AND wheel AND docking' returned 0; trash-bin pose/docking returned nothing relevant; 'trolley AND docking' returned only Xiao, Xie and DA-VPC.
- Semantic Scholar: no refuse-cart docking-pose paper.
- Not searched (WebSearch budget exhausted): Google Scholar, IEEE Xplore, Google Patents. Refuse-truck arm cart-localization patents and monocular trailer-coupler work (Atoum et al., ICCV 2017, DOI 10.1109/ICCV.2017.584, metadata only, method unread) are unchecked threats.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection with Progressive Perception and Nonlinear Model Predictive Control (Xiao et al., ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [A Systematic Evaluation of Different Indoor Localization Methods in Robotic Autonomous Luggage Trolley Collection at Airports (Sun et al., Robotica 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551); [RGB-based Category-level Object Pose Estimation via Decoupled Metric Scale Recovery (Wei et al., 2023)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.10255); [GVDepth: Zero-Shot Monocular Depth Estimation for Ground Vehicles based on Probabilistic Cue Fusion (Koledic et al., ICCV 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06080); [MonoGround: Detecting Monocular 3D Objects from the Ground (Qin and Li, CVPR 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.07372); [How do neural networks see depth in single images? (van Dijk and de Croon, 2019)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1905.07005); [DA-VPC: Disturbance-Aware Visual Predictive Control of Docking Maneuvers for Autonomous Trolley Collection (Pang et al., 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07413); [Active Structure-from-Motion for 3D Straight Lines (Mateus, Tahri, Miraldo, IROS 2018)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1807.00753); [High-precision docking of wheelchair/beds through LIDAR and visual information (Lei et al., 2024)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11408198/)
- **Build:** Mechanical/hardware student:
- A sensor head on a rolling dolly: a repurposed garden wagon or hand truck, or the existing bin-robot base if it is ready.
- On the head: the wide-FOV IMX219-160 camera about 0.45 m up, pitched about 30 deg down so the wheel contacts stay in view at 0.5 m; the LD19 at about 0.3 m; an MPU-6050 IMU; the Pi 5 and a power bank.
- Two plumb-bob reference points at surveyed offsets on the head.
- A printed ground calibration board (an environment marker, used only for per-session camera height and pitch calibration).
- A ground-truth kit and procedure: carpenter's-square contact finder, chalk, two steel tapes, a digital angle gauge.
- Cart placement and pose sampling sheets, and a slope survey of the 3 sites.
- Stretch: a passive hitch for a 20-attempt closed-loop docking demo.

Software/perception student:
- Fisheye intrinsic calibration (OpenCV) and ground-plane extrinsic calibration from the board, plus the plumb-point-to-camera transform.
- Label about 750 images (about 150 per cart) with 8 keypoints: 2 wheel-ground contacts, 2 hubs, 2 handle-bar ends and 2 lid-hinge ends. Train YOLO-pose models leave-one-brand-out.
- The GC estimator: back-project the two contacts onto the ground plane with IMU pitch correction, giving the axle midpoint and yaw.
- EPnP with per-brand and mean-shape models built from tape-measured cart keypoints.
- LD19 RANSAC line fit.
- Synchronized logging, ground-truth trilateration solver, the analysis notebook (bootstrap and mixed model) and the error-model fit.

No odometry, weave controller or bundle adjustment is needed.
- **Cost:** $370: Verified prices:
- Raspberry Pi 5 4GB: $60.00 (LeKiwi BOM, fetched).
- microSD: $11.23 (LeKiwi BOM).
- Waveshare IMX219-160 wide-FOV CSI camera: $13.99-25.99 (Waveshare product page, fetched); budget $26.
- LD19 2D lidar: $95.99 (Waveshare, marked discontinued). An LD06 or D500 substitute's price is UNVERIFIED.

Estimates (UNVERIFIED):
- Pi 5 camera adapter cable: about $5.
- MPU-6050 IMU: about $8.
- USB-C power bank: about $35.
- Mast, mounts and dolly (repurposed wagon or hand truck, plywood, 3D prints): about $50.
- Ground-truth kit (2 plumb bobs, 2 steel tapes, carpenter's square, chalk, digital angle gauge): about $60.
- Printed calibration board on foam core: about $15.

Total: about $366, rounded to $370.

Carts: $0 (household carts plus loaned neighbours' carts, unmodified).

Optional closed-loop demo: $0 if the bin-robot base already exists; otherwise a used hoverboard base at about $80 (UNVERIFIED), for a maximum of about $450.

Reference systems are not bought:
- Sun et al.'s keypoint camera at about US$400 (verified in their paper) makes the camera here about 15-28x cheaper.
- Xiao's 5-sensor stack cost is UNVERIFIED.

Everything is well under the $1,000 cap.
- **Venue:** IEEE CASE 2027 regular paper. The deadline, Mar 1 2027, is confirmed in venues.md. CASE reviewers value cost and accuracy for automation, a measured replication against published protocols (Sun et al., Xiao et al.), and a validated error model, and the format is 6+2 pages. The CASE WIP track (Apr 1, 2027) is a confirmed fallback if the full study slips. The same paper can be reused for an ICRA 2027 workshop, whose CFPs appear Feb-Mar. IROS 2027 (also Mar 1) is a stretch only if the crossover-model result is clean and the closed-loop demo is included.
- **Main risk:** The main risk is that pitch and slope mismatch dominate. GC sensitivity is about 1.7 cm per degree at 0.5 m and about 4.4 cm per degree at 1 m for h = 0.45 m (my estimate). If driveway patches differ by more than 1 deg, or the dolly rocks, GC may miss 3-4 cm beyond 0.5 m. A second risk is that the wheel-contact keypoints may not transfer to held-out brands, or may be hard to localize at dusk.

Fallbacks:
- (1) The error model and crossover range are the publishable result even if GC wins only within about 0.7 m. The paper becomes a measured cross-brand error budget of size cue vs ground cue vs a cheap lidar.
- (2) The GC-rescaled PnP arm (mean model scaled by the GC-measured wheel track at close range) is reported as the practical hybrid.
- (3) Per-brand results are reported, and hub keypoints serve as a backup contact estimate (contact = hub projected down by the measured wheel radius).
- (4) The November pilot gate catches failure before parts and fieldwork are committed.
- **Changes from previous version:** 1) Dropped the weave and active triangulation as the contribution. They are now cited as classical (2405.05937, 2302.01729, 1807.00753, de Croon 1506.01153) and listed as a rejected alternative.

2) New research question: brand-agnostic (held-out brand) docking pose. The new single idea is back-projecting two learned wheel-ground-contact keypoints onto the calibrated ground plane. It needs no cart model, no motion and no range sensor. It is cited against MonoGround, GVDepth and Stein 2003 as an applied classical cue, not a new insight.

3) New baselines, all computed from the same frames:
- EPnP with a per-brand measured model (oracle);
- EPnP with a leave-one-brand-out mean model (realistic deployment, matching the Wei et al. category-statistics approach);
- the LD19 RANSAC line fit (described honestly as ±45 mm per point, not "lidar-grade").
Published reference numbers are reported without being paired: Sun et al. (keypoint EPnP, about US$400 camera, MAE 0.139 m) and Xiao (0.17 m camera stage, 0.03 m / 0.02 rad lidar stage). Sun's MAE (|dx|+|dy|) and RMSE metrics are adopted so the numbers are comparable.

4) Ground truth no longer uses odometry. Each trial is static and measured by tape trilateration: four distances between two plumb points on the sensor head and two chalked wheel-contact marks. A pre-study measures ground-truth repeatability. The relative slope between the robot and cart patches is measured with a digital angle gauge as a covariate.

5) The truncation problem is fixed. A wide-FOV $14-26 IMX219-160 camera (Waveshare, verified) is mounted about 0.45 m up and pitched down, so the wheel contacts stay in view at 0.5 m.

6) The hypothesis now has a quantitative error model. Ground-cue depth error is predicted as (Z^2+h^2)/h·Δθ, which gives a predicted crossover range against PnP. Falsification criteria are stated.

7) Design: 5 carts (3 brands, both sizes, neighbours' carts loaned and left unmodified) × 4 ranges × 8 poses = 160 static trials at 3 sites with different slope profiles. The analysis uses estimation with bootstrap CIs clustered by cart and a mixed model. The underpowered TOST is gone.

8) Robot build risk is removed. Trials use a sensor dolly. A closed-loop docking demo on the bin-robot base is optional.

9) A November pilot go/no-go gate was added, plus a pre-registration commit in December.

10) Costs were re-checked. Pi 5 at $60 and microSD at $11.23 come from the LeKiwi BOM (fetched). IMX219-160 at $13.99-25.99 and LD19 at $95.99 (discontinued) come from Waveshare. The total is about $370, or about $450 with the optional hoverboard.

11) Venue: CASE 2027 regular paper (Mar 1), with the CASE WIP track (Apr 1) and an ICRA 2027 workshop as fallbacks.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 6.5 / I 5.5 = 5.67. *Novelty:* The core estimator is textbook flat-ground inverse perspective mapping. The proposal concedes this (Stein 2003, MonoGround), and robotics already uses ground planarity plus known camera height for metric scale (Clement et al., arXiv 1707.08989). GVDepth (arXiv 2412.06080) already studies size-cue vs ground-cue depth. H2 (a mean-shape model fails when widths differ by 29%) and H4 (a first-order (Z^2+h^2)/h x delta-theta slope error) can both be derived on paper before any data is collected. That makes them 'foreseeable comparisons', the same critique that sank round 1. What is genuinely new is a held-out-brand refuse-cart docking benchmark with odometry-independent ground truth. That is real but thin. Caveat: my web search budget and the arXiv/Semantic Scholar APIs were exhausted or rate-limited (HTTP 429), so ADAS 'tire-ground-contact distance estimation' work and trailer-coupler patents (Atoum et al. ICCV 2017) remain unchecked threats that could lower this further. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and the software is well trodden: OpenCV fisheye calibration, YOLO-pose, EPnP and a RANSAC line fit. The static protocol needs no locomotion. But several risks compound:
(1) Getting 5 distinct brands is doubtful. A municipality usually issues one brand street-wide, and 'Toter 64 and 96' is one brand, so leave-one-brand-out may come down to 2-3 brands and H2/H3 become underpowered.
(2) The ground-truth yaw uncertainty (about 0.7 deg, the proposal's own estimate) is more than half of the 1.15 deg pass/fail threshold.
(3) The GC yaw error is set by the wheel track (about 0.5 m). A 1 cm contact error per wheel gives about 1.6 deg (my estimate), so the yaw target likely fails.
(4) Cart wheels sit at the handle side and are partly recessed, so contact patches are occluded from many approach angles and at ±20 deg yaw.
(5) 4 min per trial for 4 tape trilaterations, square placement, angle gauge and logging is optimistic; 8-10 min is more realistic (estimate), which pushes 160 trials to 20+ h of fieldwork. *Impact:* This is a clean, pre-registered static benchmark with a validated error model, which suits a CASE WIP paper, an ICRA 2027 workshop or SoutheastCon. CASE main track is plausible but unlikely. The headline results are predictable from geometry. The baseline set omits what a 2027 robotics reviewer will expect: a metric monocular depth foundation model on the same $14 camera, a cheap multizone ToF (e.g., VL53L5CX, about $20, verified in rounds.md), and AprilTag-on-ground or closed-loop docking success as the task metric. The closed-loop demo is only a stretch goal, so the paper lacks a task-level outcome. *Main criticism:* The 'idea' is flat-ground back-projection of contact points, a textbook cue already used for monocular scale in robotics (Clement et al. 1707.08989) and fused against the size cue in GVDepth. The three headline hypotheses (mean-shape PnP degrades with brand mismatch, GC wins at short range, GC error scales as (Z^2+h^2)/h x delta-theta) can all be predicted on paper. A reviewer will read it as a careful measurement of a foreseeable geometry result. Two things make it worse: the baseline set is strawman-leaning (no metric-depth foundation model, no cheap ToF, no closed-loop success metric), and the ground-truth yaw precision (about 0.7 deg) is too close to the 1.15 deg claim to support it. *Closest prior work:* [Monocular Visual Teach and Repeat Aided by Local Ground Planarity (Clement et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.08989); [Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection with Progressive Perception and Nonlinear MPC (Xiao et al., ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [A Systematic Evaluation of Different Indoor Localization Methods in Robotic Autonomous Luggage Trolley Collection at Airports (Sun et al., Robotica 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551); [MonoGround: Detecting Monocular 3D Objects from the Ground (CVPR 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.07372); [GVDepth: Zero-Shot Monocular Depth Estimation for Ground Vehicles based on Probabilistic Cue Fusion (ICCV 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06080); [US10358287B2 Automated container handling system for refuse collection vehicles (Con-Tech)](https://patents.google.com/patent/US10358287); [High-precision docking of wheelchair/beds through LIDAR and visual information (Lei et al., 2024)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11408198/)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 7 / I 5.5 = 5.83. *Novelty:* The estimator is flat-ground back-projection of contact points, which dates to Stein et al. 2003 (doi 10.1109/ivs.2003.1212895). That line of work already analyzes how pitch error drives range error, so the 'error model' contribution is a known sensitivity re-measured. The docking-pose protocol, keypoint-PnP baseline and cost-vs-accuracy framing already exist in Xiao 2022, Sun 2025 and Xie 2024 (arXiv 2401.08433). What is genuinely new is a held-out-brand refuse-cart benchmark with odometry-independent ground truth. That is real but thin: known techniques applied to a new setting. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and verified: the IMX219-160 is $14-26, the LD19 is $95.99 but discontinued, and the build totals about $370. The trials are static, with no controller and no odometry, and 160 trials at about 4 min is about 11 h. There is a November go/no-go pilot. The risks are real, though. 'Five carts / held-out brands' realistically gives 3 brands, since Toter 64 and 96 are one brand and neighbours usually share one municipal brand, so leave-one-brand-out trains on 1-2 brands. The tape ground truth's estimated yaw accuracy of about 0.7 deg is too close to the 1.15 deg pass/fail threshold to resolve it. Wheel contacts are visible only from the handle side. *Impact:* The headline results follow from geometry before any data is taken. A ground cue wins close up, the size cue degrades with brand mismatch, and slope makes the crossover move. Earlier rounds penalized exactly this kind of foreseeable comparison. A clean, pre-registered cross-brand benchmark with a $14 camera is a solid fit for the CASE WIP track, ARSO or an ICRA workshop. A CASE main-track paper is possible but unlikely, because the 3 cm docking requirement is not motivated against passive funnel hitches that absorb error. *Main criticism:* The two main claims are both foreseeable from known geometry. The estimator is textbook flat-ground back-projection (Stein 2003). The 'validated error model' is the standard pitch-sensitivity derivative. 'Ground cue beats size cue up close, then crosses over with slope' can be computed on paper before any data is collected. That leaves the paper as a well-run benchmark of a predictable result. The measurement also has a resolution problem: the tape-trilateration ground truth (about 0.7 deg yaw, estimate) cannot reliably resolve the 1.15 deg yaw pass/fail criterion. The 3 cm accuracy target is also never tied to what a cart hitch actually needs. *Closest prior work:* [Vision-based ACC with a single camera: bounds on range and range rate accuracy (Stein, Mano, Shashua, IEEE IV 2003)](https://doi.org/10.1109/ivs.2003.1212895); [Autonomous Multiple-Trolley Collection System with Nonholonomic Robots (Xie et al., 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.08433); [A Systematic Evaluation of Different Indoor Localization Methods in Robotic Autonomous Luggage Trolley Collection at Airports (Sun et al., Robotica 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551); [Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection with Progressive Perception and Nonlinear MPC (Xiao et al., ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [DA-VPC: Disturbance-Aware Visual Predictive Control of Docking Maneuvers for Autonomous Trolley Collection (Pang et al., 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07413); [Monocular Video-Based Trailer Coupler Detection Using Multiplexer CNN (Atoum et al., ICCV 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iccv.2017.584); [MonoGround (Qin and Li, CVPR 2022) / GVDepth (Koledic et al., ICCV 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06080)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5 / F 7 / I 5 = 5.67. *Novelty:* Ground-plane back-projection for metric range is classical (Stein 2003, MonoGround). The size-cue vs ground-cue trade-off is already modelled for ground vehicles in GVDepth (arXiv 2412.06080). The H4 'error model' is just the first-order derivative of Z = h/tan(alpha), so the crossover is predictable before any data is collected, which is the 'foreseeable comparison' failure the earlier reviewers flagged. What is genuinely new is thin but real: a held-out-brand refuse-cart docking benchmark with independent ground truth. I found no paper doing this for carts, but my searches were limited (budget exhausted, arXiv/Semantic Scholar 429s), and monocular trailer-coupler hitch assist (Atoum ICCV 2017, doi 10.1109/iccv.2017.584) remains an unchecked close analogue. *Feasibility:* It is static trials only, with no drive base, odometry or controller. The parts are cheap and verified (the Waveshare IMX219 series is $13.99-25.99 per the product page I opened, and the LD19 is $95.99). YOLO-pose plus a ground-plane solver is well-trodden. Two real risks remain. First, getting 4-5 distinct brands: one municipality usually issues a single brand, and Toter 64 and 96 are the same brand, so leave-one-brand-out may really have only 2-3 brands. Second, the 4-minute tape-trilateration trial, plus slope gauging and cart repositioning, is optimistic (8-10 min is more realistic), during January weather. *Impact:* It is a clean, preregistered measurement paper that fits CASE or an ICRA workshop, but a main-track reviewer will see several problems: (a) the result follows from textbook geometry; (b) no tolerance-derived need for 3 cm / 1.15 deg docking, since a passive funnel hitch likely tolerates more; (c) the obvious cheapest range comparator (a $10-20 single-point or multizone ToF, as in Lei et al.) is missing, so the 'cheaper' claim is only shown against a ±45 mm LD19; (d) leave-one-brand-out over 2-4 brands is statistically thin. In practice this is workshop or WIP level, with an outside chance at the CASE main track. *Main criticism:* The headline finding (the ground-contact cue beats a size-based mean-model PnP at close range, and its error grows with (Z^2+h^2)/h x slope mismatch) follows from first-order geometry and is already the subject of size-vs-ground cue fusion work (GVDepth). It is benchmarked against a weak cheap comparator (a ±45 mm LD19) rather than the cheapest real alternative (a $10-20 ToF rangefinder). The claimed accuracy target is also not tied to any measured hitch tolerance. A reviewer will likely conclude it is predictable and does not show the accuracy is needed or that the camera is the cheapest way to get it. *Closest prior work:* [Robotic Autonomous Trolley Collection with Progressive Perception and Nonlinear MPC (Xiao et al., ICRA 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06648); [A Systematic Evaluation of Different Indoor Localization Methods in Robotic Autonomous Luggage Trolley Collection at Airports (Sun, Chen, Wang, Meng; Robotica 43, 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06551); [Monocular Video-Based Trailer Coupler Detection Using Multiplexer CNN (Atoum et al., ICCV 2017)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iccv.2017.584); [GVDepth: Zero-Shot Monocular Depth Estimation for Ground Vehicles based on Probabilistic Cue Fusion (Koledic et al., ICCV 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06080); [High-precision docking of wheelchair/beds through LIDAR and visual information (Lei et al., 2024)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11408198/); [DA-VPC: Disturbance-Aware Visual Predictive Control of Docking Maneuvers for Autonomous Trolley Collection (Pang et al., 2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07413)

### P2.v2 (round 2, median 5.33): How many hits does a post need? A beam-budget error model and post-layout planner for $80 dToF lidars on retroreflective posts, patching RTK dead zones in a yard

- **Question:** Can an error model built only from a consumer dToF lidar's angular sample spacing, its range noise and a one-hour bench calibration of retroreflector blooming and saturation bias predict, before any post is planted, the per-point 95th-percentile position error of retroreflective-post localization outdoors (across post diameter, range, scan count K, post geometry and measured ambient lux)? Is it accurate enough to plan the fewest posts that bring the dead zones of a single NTRIP RTK rover to at most 3 cm?
- **Hypothesis:** H1 (beam budget): after bench calibration of a blooming width b(r), mean hits per scan follow (D + b(r)) / (r * dtheta) within ±20% for both lidars, both post diameters (10 and 25 cm) and ranges of 1-10 m. Bare-geometry D / (r * dtheta) undercounts. Example (our arithmetic): the LD19 class at 5 Hz has 0.4 deg spacing, so a 25 cm post at 8 m gets about 4.5 hits and a 10 cm post about 1.8.
H2 (inverted mixed pixel): because a retroreflector returns far more light than the background, edge hits on retro-wrapped posts have a median range bias at most 1/3 of that on same-diameter matte-white posts, while their apparent angular width is inflated.
H3 (dither): the rotor is not phase-locked to sampling, so the error in a static post's center bearing falls roughly as K^-0.5 over K scans. Falsified if the fitted log-log slope is shallower than -0.3, which would mean phase lock.
H4 (prediction, primary): the model fitted on site-1 and bench data, combined with GDOP, predicts site-2 per-point static 95th-percentile error within a factor of 1.5 of the measured value at ≥80% of test points, for both lidars. Calibration slope 0.7-1.4. The prediction for the second lidar uses only its spec-sheet sample spacing plus its own bench blooming calibration.
H5 (hybrid): adding the planner-chosen ≤4 posts of 25 cm to RTK-only raises the share of test points meeting ≤3 cm static and ≤5 cm dynamic 95th-percentile error to ≥90% at both sites, for ≤$200 added hardware and ≤45 min setup. The planner's post count is within 1 of the empirically minimal subset.
- **Measurements:** A. Bench and driveway characterization (Nov-Dec)
- Factors: both lidars; 25 cm retro, 10 cm retro and 25 cm matte-white posts; ranges 1-10 m in 1 m steps; scan rate at minimum and typical; lux measured with a meter at the post (night, overcast, direct sun).
- Per cell, 300 scans. Metrics: hits per scan, detection probability under the intensity gate, per-hit range bias and SD against tape, edge-hit range bias, apparent angular width (blooming b), bearing error of the center vs K = 1, 2, 5, 10, 50 scans.
- False positives: count over 30 min near cars, windows, house numbers and road reflectors.
- Analysis: fit H1 and H2 with bootstrap CIs (≥200 edge hits per cell). H3 is the log-log slope with a 95% CI.

B. Field validation, 2 sites (home driveway and yard about 12 x 15 m including the garage mouth and eaves; a second home or school lot)
- Per site: 4 control points used only for RTK-to-local 2D Helmert alignment (residuals reported), plus 30 test points.
- All points are tape-trilaterated from two baseline nails, with a redundant third distance per point. The closure error estimates ground-truth sigma, which is added to the predicted variance.
- Six 25 cm posts are installed at planner-candidate locations.
- At each point, 30 s static (about 150-250 scans) logged simultaneously from both lidars (stacked on one mast) and the RTK rover. Fix and float state are logged and false fixes counted (fix reported but more than 10 cm off).
- Sessions: night, overcast, bright sun (lux logged). Total 3 sessions x 30 points x 2 sites = 180 point-sessions per system.
- Site-2 predicted error maps are committed in a dated, pre-registered git or OSF entry before site-2 data collection.

C. Layout
- Detections from any post can be masked offline, so all 2^6 = 64 subsets are scored against ground truth at every test point. This gives an empirical posts-vs-coverage Pareto front.
- Metrics: the planner's chosen subset size, its coverage, and its rank among subsets of the same size.

D. Dynamic
- 12 runs per site of a 25 m route through RTK-good and RTK-dead zones at 0.3 m/s. The rover runs closed-loop on the hybrid estimator.
- 6 stops per run (72 per site). At each stop a plumb mark is chalked under the lidar axis and tape-trilaterated.
- Every estimator is scored on the same stops at the instant of stopping (no static averaging): RTK + odometry, posts-only per lidar, and hybrid.

Analysis
- Calibration plots of predicted vs measured error, with the fraction of points within a factor of 1.5 (Wilson CI).
- Paired McNemar test on per-point pass/fail between RTK-only and hybrid.
- Bootstrap CIs on paired median error differences.
- Cost per covered square meter and setup minutes for each configuration.
- **Baseline / expensive reference:** Commercial capability being matched: centimeter positioning for wire-free yard robots. Husqvarna claims 2-3 cm with satellite navigation plus a reference station (Wikipedia, verified; also the press release in prior_art.md). Consumer RTK mowers such as the Navimow i105N ($799-1,000, prior_art.md) publish no accuracy data, and a lidar mower exists (AM Robots Storm, 2024, per Wikipedia).

Measured baseline: one u-blox ZED-F9P rover kit with antenna (ArduSimple simpleRTK2B Basic Starter Kit, $263.75; or SparkFun RTK2 at $259.95 plus antenna; both prices verified in prior_art.md) on free NTRIP corrections. This is the honest single-receiver baseline, run on the same points and the same stops.

Secondary comparisons:
- Posts-only localization with each lidar.
- Bare-geometry and many-hit cylinder-fit models, as the prediction baselines that H1 and H4 must beat.

The paper does not claim to be cheaper than RTK. It claims the cheapest validated way to reach a coverage target, reported as dollars per covered square meter and setup minutes for RTK-only, posts-only and hybrid. Industrial reflector navigation scanners are mentioned only qualitatively (price and specs UNVERIFIED).
- **Novelty claim:** Already established, not claimed as new:
- Reflector localization with lidar, including outdoors against a GNSS reference: Lutwitzi et al. IV 2022 with an Ouster OS1-32 and retroreflective cylinders.
- Data association for identical reflectors (ICRA 2011).
- Indoor reflector GDOP/FIM optimization (Xie et al., IEEE Sensors J 2024).
- Reflector placement optimization for radar (IPIN 2023).
- Open-source reflector-localization stacks (RLML, 2D_reflector_localization_Algorithm), which we reuse.
- A cheap 2D lidar vs RTK outdoors, where RTK won (CACS 2023).
- RTK fragility near buildings (Janos & Kuras 2021).

What is new is the few-hit regime of sub-1-degree consumer dToF lidars, which none of these addresses. An $80 lidar gets only about 1-5 samples per post per scan, so the cylinder fitting used with many-hit industrial or 3D lidars does not apply. Our specific insight about why the cheap version can still work:
(i) Retroreflector dominance should make edge hits range-correct while inflating apparent width by a calibratable blooming term. This is the reverse of the usual mixed-pixel problem.
(ii) Unsynchronized rotation dithers the sample phase, so a static post's bearing can be recovered below the beam spacing over K scans; during driving, motion and odometry provide the dither.
(iii) Hit count, and therefore error, is predictable from (D + b) / (r * dtheta). Post diameter and layout can thus be designed before installation.

The contribution is a falsifiable, pre-registered predicted-vs-measured error map across 2 lidars (2 vendors), 2 post diameters, 2 sites and measured lux. It comes with a planner that fills measured RTK dead zones, validated against all 64 post subsets.

Search status (this session): the Crossref, arXiv and GitHub APIs found no LD06/LD19/RPLIDAR C1 reflector-localization paper or repository. A GitHub search for Eurobot lidar returned an LD06 repo (Sudriabotik) and an LDS02RR repo, neither describing beacon localization. Google Patents (HTTP 503) and Google Scholar could not be searched, so a patent and Scholar check (mower laser and retroreflective beacons) is a week-1 go/no-go task. The Xie 2024 and IPIN 2023 abstracts were not read; only title and venue were verified via Crossref.
- **Prior work cited by the generator:** [Lidar and Landmark based Localization System for a Wheeled Mobile Driving Simulator (Lutwitzi, Betschinske, Albrecht, Winner; IEEE IV 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iv51971.2022.9827085); [Reflective Markers Assisted Indoor LiDAR Self-Positioning Algorithm Based on Joint Optimization With FIM and GDOP (Xie et al., IEEE Sensors Journal 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/JSEN.2023.3294934); [Indoor Positioning Based on Active Radar Sensing and Passive Reflectors: Reflector Placement Optimization (IPIN 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/ipin57070.2023.10332502); [Outdoor Positioning Based on ROS LiDAR Navigation Compared with RTK GPS Accuracy (CACS 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/cacs60074.2023.10325865); [RLML: Reflector-based LiDAR Mapping and Localization (GitHub, 2025)](https://github.com/monkeydchopper/RLML); [2D_reflector_localization_Algorithm (GitHub)](https://github.com/unlogical0327/2D_reflector_localization_Algorithm); [Application-driven Test and Evaluation Framework for Indoor Localization Systems in Warehouses (Schyga et al., arXiv 2107.10597)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2107.10597); [AGV global localization using indistinguishable artificial landmarks (ICRA 2011)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2011.5979759); [Janos & Kuras 2021: ZED-F9P with a patch antenna got a fix in 4 of 8 series in an urban canyon](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8401267/); [Waveshare D500 DTOF lidar kit (replacement for the discontinued LD19): $95.99, 5000 Hz, 6-13 Hz, ±30 mm, 60 klux](https://www.waveshare.com/d500-lidar-kit.htm); [Waveshare RPLIDAR C1: $79.99, 5000 Hz, 8-12 Hz, 0.72 deg, ±30 mm, reflectivity output](https://www.waveshare.com/rplidar-c1.htm); [LDROBOT ldlidar_stl_ros2 driver (LD06/LD19) publishes per-point intensity](https://github.com/ldrobotSensorTeam/ldlidar_stl_ros2); [Robotic lawn mower (Wikipedia): AM Robots Storm lidar mower (2024); Husqvarna 2-3 cm with satellite navigation plus a reference station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robotic_lawn_mower)
- **Build:** Mechanical student:
- Six 25 cm diameter, 1 m tall posts from cardboard concrete-form tube or similar, with a 30 cm band of retroreflective sheeting, on weighted quick-set bases.
- Two 10 cm retro posts and one 25 cm matte-white post for the bench factors.
- A mast on the existing hoverboard rover that stacks both lidars and the RTK antenna on one vertical axis, with a plumb-bob hook for stop-and-mark.
- A survey kit: baseline nails, two 30 m steel tapes, chalk.
- A rail jig for bench tests that holds a post at known range and incidence.

Software/perception student:
- Drivers and loggers for the RPLIDAR C1 and the D500 (or an LD06/LD19 if one can be sourced), plus an RTK logger with fix/float flags.
- Intensity gating and clustering. Post-center estimators: a K-scan dither estimator and a known-diameter estimator with blooming correction. Trilateration and an EKF with wheel odometry (RLML can be reused as a cross-check).
- The beam-budget and GDOP error model, plus a greedy or exhaustive post-layout planner over candidate locations constrained by the measured RTK fix map.
- Offline subset masking, and an analysis pipeline for calibration plots, McNemar tests and bootstrap CIs.
- A route follower for the dynamic runs.
- A dated pre-registration commit of the site-2 predictions.
- **Cost:** $985: Verified prices:
- RPLIDAR C1: $79.99 (Waveshare)
- D500 DTOF lidar kit: $95.99 (Waveshare)
- RTK rover: ArduSimple simpleRTK2B Basic Starter Kit (ZED-F9P plus antenna), $263.75, from prior_art.md. Alternative: SparkFun RTK2 at $259.95 plus an antenna (about $60, estimate). Free NTRIP; caster availability near home is UNVERIFIED.
- Hoverboard rover base: $139; ESP32: $19.95 (both from earlier angles).

Estimates (UNVERIFIED):
- Rover frame and mast: about $100
- Posts: 6 x 25 cm form tubes plus 3 bench posts, about $110
- Retroreflective sheeting: about $70
- Weighted bases: about $30
- Lux meter: about $25
- Steel tapes, nails, plumb bob, chalk: about $50
- Logging on a team-owned laptop: $0

Total about $985, just under the $1,000 flag.

FLAGS:
- Adding a Raspberry Pi 5 brings it to about $1,095.
- If no NTRIP caster is in range, an own F9P base adds about $264, for about $1,250.

Capability-cost framing (honest):
- Hybrid add-on to RTK: one lidar plus 4 posts, about $180-200, plus measured setup minutes.
- RTK-only: about $264, but it leaves dead zones.
- Posts-only: about $180-200, but needs surveyed infrastructure.
- All are reported as dollars per covered square meter at ≤3 cm and as setup minutes. The Navimow price ($799-1,000, a whole mower) is context only.
- **Venue:** IEEE CASE 2027, regular paper (deadline Mar 1, 2027, confirmed in venues.md). The contribution is a predictive design rule and a minimum-cost layout planner for automation infrastructure, judged on cost per covered area and setup time, which suits CASE's automation-economics focus. The WIP track (Apr 1, 4 pages) is the fallback if the site-2 validation is partial. IROS 2027 (same deadline) is the alternative if the sensor-model result (H2, H3) is strong.
- **Main risk:** Main risk: the model may not transfer. Possible causes:
- Direct winter sun may exceed the C1's (unrated) or the D500's 60 klux tolerance and raise the noise floor unpredictably.
- The rotor may be phase-locked, so dither never appears (H3 false).
- The D500 may not output intensity.

Each is tested on the bench in October or November, before the build commits. Fallbacks:
- If dither is absent, use the known-diameter estimator plus motion and odometry dither, and report phase lock as a finding.
- If sun breaks detection, report detection probability vs lux as the design limit and restrict the claims to the measured lux range.
- If the D500 lacks intensity, substitute an LD06/LD19-class unit, whose driver publishes intensity (verified); price UNVERIFIED.
- If H4 fails, a measured (not predicted) beam-budget curve and the 64-subset Pareto front still give empirical design rules, written up as a CASE WIP paper.

Secondary risks:
- Snow or frozen ground at the sites in Dec-Feb: schedule around the weather and use the driveway.
- No NTRIP caster in range: own base, +$264, FLAG.
- A prior hobbyist or patent result on LD06 reflector beacons: checked in October week 1. If found, reframe around the H2 and H3 sensor model.
- **Changes from previous version:** 1) The contribution is now a beam-budget error model plus a post-layout planner, not "a $96 lidar matches RTK." The model predicts per-point 95th-percentile error from angular sample spacing, post diameter, range, number of scans K, post geometry (GDOP) and measured lux. The planner picks the fewest posts that bring RTK dead zones to 3 cm or better.
2) New, falsifiable physical hypotheses specific to cheap lidars on retroreflectors:
- Saturation "blooming" makes a post look wider than it is, by a calibratable amount.
- An inverted mixed-pixel effect: edge hits on a retroreflector should have less range bias than edge hits on a matte post.
- Unsynchronized rotation gives sub-beam bearing dither across scans.
3) Posts are resized from the beam math: 25 cm cylinders plus 10 cm posts as a diameter factor, with lidars at 5-8 Hz. The model predicts the hit counts and is checked against them.
4) Sensors changed because the LD19 is discontinued:
- RPLIDAR C1 at $79.99, reflectivity output verified.
- Waveshare D500 at $95.99, 60 klux rating verified. Its intensity output is UNVERIFIED and will be checked on the bench.
- The second sensor is a transfer test: model fitted on one sensor, predictions made for the other.
5) Pre-registered validation:
- Site-2 predictions are committed with a date before any site-2 measurement.
- Two sites.
- All 64 subsets of 6 installed posts are scored offline, giving an empirical posts-vs-coverage Pareto front to judge the planner against.
6) Honest baseline and cost:
- One NTRIP rover kit ($263.75), not a pair.
- No "cheaper than RTK" claim. The hybrid is reported as cost per covered square meter and setup minutes.
- The Navimow price is context only. The SICK NAV350 claim is dropped.
7) Measurement fixes:
- RTK-to-local alignment from 4 separate control points (2D Helmert fit).
- Ground-truth error estimated from redundant tape closures and folded into the predicted variance.
- The fragile ±1 cm TOST is dropped.
- Dynamic error is measured by stop-and-mark at the instant the rover stops, so no static averaging can mask it, and all estimators are scored on the same stops.
8) Cost now ~$985 (Pi 5 dropped; laptop logging). Flagged options: +$110 for a Pi 5, +$264 for an own RTK base.
9) Venue unchanged (CASE 2027, Mar 1), with CASE WIP on Apr 1 as fallback.
- **Reviews:**
  - R1 (Academic depth): N 5 / F 4.5 / I 5.5 = 5.00. *Novelty:* The few-hit consumer-dToF regime and the pre-registered predicted-versus-measured map are a real but thin differentiator. H1 is first-order geometry: angular subtense divided by sample spacing, which the proposal's own arithmetic computes. H2 recalibrates a known mixed-pixel effect (Tuley et al., ICRA 2005). The layout planner overlaps an uncited ICRA/IROS/RAS literature on landmark placement for localization accuracy (Beinhofer, Mueller, Burgard 2011-2013, verified via Crossref). Lutwitzi et al. (IV 2022) already showed retro cylinders plus lidar against a GNSS reference outdoors. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap and off the shelf, but the experiment matrix is very large for two part-time students in Dec-Feb weather. The bench grid alone is 2 lidars x 3 posts x 10 ranges x 2 scan rates x 3 lux conditions at 300 scans per cell, about 360 cells. On top of that come 180 static point-sessions per system across 2 sites, 24 closed-loop dynamic runs with 144 chalk-and-tape stops, an EKF, a planner and a pre-registration. The 3 cm target also matches the D500's own ±30 mm (2-12 m) range spec (verified on the Waveshare page), so tape ground truth needs sub-centimeter closure over 12-15 m, which is hard to get outdoors in winter. A single ZED-F9P may also have barely any dead zone in an open suburban yard, which would make H5 trivial or untestable. *Impact:* A falsifiable, pre-registered predictive model with dollars per covered square meter is a good fit for the CASE WIP track or a workshop, and it is a respectable application story. Main-track CASE/IROS reviewers will likely see the model as geometry plus GDOP, and the planner as a re-implementation of known landmark-placement methods unless it beats them. The yard RTK-dead-zone use case is also narrow. *Main criticism:* The three claimed insights may all be judged foreseeable or already known:
- Hit count follows angular subtense over sample spacing.
- Retroreflectors bias edge returns and bloom.
- Unsynchronized sampling dithers bearing.

The post-layout planner also re-enters an established landmark-placement literature (Beinhofer et al., ICRA 2011, IROS 2013, RAS 2013) that the proposal does not cite or compare against. Meanwhile, the measurement program (2 lidars, 3 post types, about 360 bench cells, 2 sites, 3 lux sessions, 144 dynamic stops) is too large to finish well in 12 part-time winter weeks. The yard may also have no meaningful RTK dead zone to fill. *Closest prior work:* [Lidar and Landmark based Localization System for a Wheeled Mobile Driving Simulator (IEEE IV 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iv51971.2022.9827085); [Effective landmark placement for accurate and reliable mobile robot navigation (Beinhofer, Mueller, Burgard; Robotics and Autonomous Systems 2013)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.robot.2012.08.009); [Near-optimal landmark selection for mobile robot navigation (Beinhofer, Mueller, Burgard; ICRA 2011)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icra.2011.5979871); [On achievable accuracy for range-finder localization (ICRA 2007)](https://doi.org/10.1109/robot.2007.364120); [Analysis and Removal of Artifacts in 3-D LADAR Data (Tuley, Vandapel, Hebert; ICRA 2005)](https://doi.org/10.1109/robot.2005.1570440); [Reflective Markers Assisted Indoor LiDAR Self-Positioning Algorithm Based on Joint Optimization With FIM and GDOP (IEEE Sensors J 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/JSEN.2023.3294934); [RLML: Reflector-based LiDAR Mapping and Localization](https://github.com/monkeydchopper/RLML); [Application-driven Test and Evaluation Framework for Indoor Localization Systems in Warehouses (arXiv 2107.10597)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2107.10597)
  - R2 (Patents & products): N 5 / F 5.5 / I 5.5 = 5.33. *Novelty:* The pieces already exist. Retroreflector-plus-lidar localization outdoors against a GNSS reference: Lutwitzi IV 2022. GDOP/FIM marker error bounds: Xie 2024. Beacon and reflector placement optimization: McGuire Symmetry 2020 (abstract read), ITSC 2021, IPIN 2023. Open stacks: RLML. That leaves the 'few-hit consumer dToF' sensor model. Its core, hits ≈ (D+b)/(r·dθ) with error ~ σ/√n·GDOP and K^-0.5 averaging, is first-principles arithmetic a reviewer will see as foreseeable. The only part with real surprise potential is H2 (retro edge hits being range-correct while the target looks wider). The patent check is still open: Google Patents returned 503 and DuckDuckGo a captcha this session. *Feasibility:* The parts are cheap, verified and off-the-shelf: C1 $79.99 with reflectivity output; D500 $95.99, 60 klux, intensity not stated (both pages fetched). But the plan carries several risks at once:
- A 360-cell bench matrix.
- 2 sites with 68 tape-trilaterated points.
- 144 chalk-and-tape dynamic stops.
- A pre-registration gate.
- Three lux conditions, done outdoors in Dec-Feb snow season, part-time.
- Bench work front-loaded into Oct-Nov, outside the stated Dec-Feb build window.
On top of that, a 12x15 m yard may have too few true RTK dead zones for H5 to be meaningful. *Impact:* A pre-registered predicted-vs-measured error map is a rigorous and unusual habit for a student paper, and it makes a good application story. At CASE or IROS main track, though, reviewers would likely see a yard-scale sensor characterization plus a textbook GDOP planner, and the 'cheaper' claim is weak: the hybrid adds about $180-200 on top of a $264 RTK rather than replacing anything. That makes it a realistic ARSO, SoutheastCon, CASE WIP or workshop paper, not a main-track one. *Main criticism:* The contribution sits in the gap between known pieces, and that gap is mostly arithmetic. Reflector-plus-lidar localization against a GNSS reference outdoors (Lutwitzi 2022), GDOP-driven marker error bounds (Xie 2024) and beacon-placement optimization for GNSS-denied localization (McGuire 2020, IPIN 2023, ITSC 2021) all exist. Hit count = subtense/spacing, plus √n and K^-0.5 averaging, is what a reviewer would predict without running the experiment, which repeats the 'foreseeable result' critique from rounds.md. The application framing (patching RTK dead zones in a 12x15 m yard) also rests on an unmeasured premise: that a home yard has dead zones big enough to need 4 posts. Meanwhile the experiment matrix (360 bench cells, 2 sites, 144 dynamic stops, 3 lux regimes in winter) is too large for 12 part-time weeks. *Closest prior work:* [Lidar and Landmark based Localization System for a Wheeled Mobile Driving Simulator (Lutwitzi et al., IEEE IV 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iv51971.2022.9827085); [Reflective Markers Assisted Indoor LiDAR Self-Positioning Algorithm Based on Joint Optimization With FIM and GDOP (Xie, Hu, Du, Zhou, Wang; IEEE Sensors J 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/JSEN.2023.3294934); [Optimal Beacon Placement for Self-Localization Using Three Beacon Bearings (McGuire, Law, Chahl, Dogancay; Symmetry 2020)](https://doi.org/10.3390/sym13010056); [Landmark Placement Optimization for Accurate Localization in Autonomous Vehicles (Moreno, Hussein, Garcia; IEEE ITSC 2021)](https://doi.org/10.1109/itsc48978.2021.9564926); [Indoor Positioning Based on Active Radar Sensing and Passive Reflectors: Reflector Placement Optimization (Hinderer et al., IPIN 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/ipin57070.2023.10332502); [Research on AGV Global Positioning Algorithm Based on Reflector and Lidar (IJCSIT 2024)](https://doi.org/10.62051/ijcsit.v2n3.10); [A New Three Object Triangulation Algorithm for Mobile Robot Positioning (Pierlot & Van Droogenbroeck, IEEE T-RO 2014)](https://doi.org/10.1109/tro.2013.2294061); [Mobile Robot Localization Using Beacons and the Kalman Filter Technique for the Eurobot Competition (CCIS 2011)](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-21975-7_6); [Outdoor Positioning Based on ROS LiDAR Navigation Compared with RTK GPS Accuracy (CACS 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/cacs60074.2023.10325865); [RLML: Reflector-based LiDAR Mapping and Localization (GitHub)](https://github.com/monkeydchopper/RLML); [Robotic lawn mower (Wikipedia): AM Robots Storm lidar mower (2024); Husqvarna 2-3 cm with satellite navigation plus a reference station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robotic_lawn_mower)
  - R3 (Student/maker realism & venue fit): N 5.5 / F 5 / I 6 = 5.50. *Novelty:* Every building block is published:
- reflector-plus-lidar localization outdoors against a GNSS reference (Lutwitzi, IV 2022);
- retroreflector blooming inflating apparent object size (BloomNet, IV 2024; ICCE-Asia 2024);
- GDOP-driven reflector layout (IEEE Sensors J 2024; IPIN 2023);
- sparse cylindrical-landmark 2D-lidar localization outdoors (ECMR 2025 orchards).

The core model, hits ≈ (D+b)/(r·dθ) plus K^-0.5 averaging plus GDOP, is first-principles geometry and statistics that reviewers will find predictable. The real but thin differentiators are the few-hit consumer-dToF regime, the edge-hit range-bias claim, sunlight as a measured factor, and pre-registered prediction-vs-measurement used to plan posts for RTK dead zones. I found no LD06/C1/D500 reflector-localization paper. GitHub searches for 'lidar reflector localization trilateration' and 'eurobot lidar beacon' returned nothing, but the patent check (mower, laser, retroreflective beacons) is still undone. *Feasibility:* The hardware is cheap and off-the-shelf, but the experiment matrix is very large for two part-time high-schoolers doing outdoor work from December to February:
- Bench: about 2 lidars x 3 post types x 10 ranges x 2 scan rates x 3 lux conditions, at 300 scans per cell.
- Field: 180 point-sessions per system across 2 sites, each point tape-trilaterated.
- Dynamic: 24 closed-loop runs with 144 chalk-and-tape stops.
- Process: a pre-registration freeze in the middle.

Specific risks:
- Winter weather (snow, frozen ground, short daylight, low sun shining straight into the scanner).
- An NTRIP caster that may not exist nearby.
- Steel-tape ground truth over 15 m of uneven lawn has to reach well under 1 cm to grade a 3 cm target.
- The ±30 mm (C1/D500) range spec makes the ≤5 cm dynamic 95th-percentile target with no averaging doubtful.
- Several go/no-go checks could each force a reframe. *Impact:* If it executes cleanly, a pre-registered predicted-vs-measured calibration across 2 vendors, 2 sites and measured lux, plus a 64-subset Pareto validation of the planner, is unusually rigorous for a low-cost paper. It fits CASE's cost and automation framing (CASE 2027 deadline Mar 1, confirmed in venues.md).

Main-track acceptance is still uncertain:
- The model is simple geometry.
- Retroreflector blooming is already known.
- The application, a yard with only a few RTK dead zones under eaves or in the garage mouth, is narrow.
- A 12x15 m yard may have very little RTK dead area, which weakens H5.

Realistic outcome: CASE regular paper borderline, with WIP or a workshop likely. That is a solid but not standout application story. *Main criticism:* The 'error model' is close to first-principles: hit count from angular spacing, averaging over K scans, then GDOP. Its most interesting ingredient, retroreflector blooming, is already documented in automotive lidar. So the intellectual contribution is thin relative to an enormous winter field campaign (2 sites, 3 lighting sessions, 60 tape-surveyed points, 144 stop-and-mark dynamic stops, pre-registration) that two part-time high-schoolers are unlikely to finish cleanly by Feb 20. A slip in any go/no-go check (intensity output, sun tolerance, NTRIP caster, rotor phase lock) collapses the schedule. *Closest prior work:* [Lidar and Landmark based Localization System for a Wheeled Mobile Driving Simulator (IEEE IV 2022)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iv51971.2022.9827085); [BloomNet: Perception of Blooming Effect in ADAS using Synthetic LiDAR Point Cloud Data (IEEE IV 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/iv55156.2024.10588461); [LiDAR Blooming Artifacts Estimation Method Induced by Retro-Reflectance with Synthetic Data Modeling and Deep Learning (ICCE-Asia 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/icce-asia63397.2024.10774004); [A Blooming-Region-Aware Directional Weighting Filter for Correcting Blooming-Induced Background Depth Distortion in AMCW-ToF LiDAR Imaging of a High-Reflectance Object (Sensors 2026)](https://doi.org/10.3390/s26154997); [Odometry Enhancement and Localization in Orchards Using Tree Trunk Landmarks with 2D LiDAR (ECMR 2025)](https://doi.org/10.1109/ecmr65884.2025.11163213); [Reflective Markers Assisted Indoor LiDAR Self-Positioning ... FIM and GDOP (IEEE Sensors J 2024)](https://doi.org/10.1109/JSEN.2023.3294934); [RLML: Reflector-based LiDAR Mapping and Localization (GitHub)](https://github.com/monkeydchopper/RLML); [Outdoor Positioning Based on ROS LiDAR Navigation Compared with RTK GPS Accuracy (CACS 2023)](https://doi.org/10.1109/cacs60074.2023.10325865); [Integration of Laser Scanner and Odometry for Autonomous Robotics Lawn-mower (ASABE 2015)](https://doi.org/10.13031/aim.20152190609)
